LECTURE NOTES 

# Optimization, Optimal Control, and Reinforcement Learning for Robotics 

# Yonghyeon Lee 

Department of Artificial Intelligence Yonsei University Fall 2026 arXiv:2610.32538v1 [cs.RO] 23 Oct 2026 ii Preface 

These notes introduce optimal control and reinforcement learning as two complementary per-spectives on sequential decision-making , with a particular emphasis on robotics. Optimal control traditionally begins with mathematical models, objectives, and the design of feedback laws, whereas reinforcement learning emphasizes learning effective decision-making strategies through interaction and experience. Despite their different starting points, the two fields share a common mathematical foundation in optimization, dynamical systems, value functions, and Bellman recursions. The intended audience is graduate students, researchers, and engineers working in robotics and related fields . The assumed prerequisites are undergraduate-level linear algebra, multivariable calculus, and probability, along with basic familiarity with robot kinematics and dynamics. Part I establishes the mathematical foundations of optimization, while subsequent chapters introduce additional concepts as needed. The goal is to provide a concise, self-contained introduction , rather than an exhaustive survey of either field. Mathematical derivations are included when they clarify underlying principles, algorithms, or modeling choices, while technical regularity conditions and specialized proofs are generally omitted or treated as optional material. Throughout the text, emphasis is placed on physical and geometric intuition, illustrative examples, and practical implementation considerations. Examples are drawn primarily from robotics, including robot motion and control, contact dynamics, trajectory optimization, and learning-based control, to illustrate how the theoretical concepts arise in real robotic systems. These notes are developed at the Robotics and Physical AI Laboratory (RoPhi Lab) at Yonsei University. Further information about the laboratory and its research can be found at https://rophi-lab. github.io .

Acknowledgment of AI Assistance. This manuscript was developed through a human–AI collaborative writing process. Yonghyeon Lee established the intellectual direction, selected and organized the topics, guided the mathematical and pedagogical development, and reviewed and revised the manuscript. Under his guidance, GPT contributed substantially to the drafting, rewriting, and stylistic refinement of the text. All final decisions regarding the content, mathematical interpretation, and presentation, as well as responsibility for the resulting manuscript, remain with Yonghyeon Lee. # Optimization in Vector Spaces 

Chapter map. The geometry, differential calculus, and optimality conditions underlying optimization with finitely many variables. 

# 1.1 Vector Spaces and Geometry 

Optimization begins with a choice of variables and a description of the space in which those variables live. An optimization variable may be a vector in Rm, or a sequence of such vectors stacked into one longer vector. Although these objects may have different interpretations, the same geometric ideas—linearity, length, angle, and projection—organize all of them. Throughout this chapter, vector spaces are defined over the real numbers unless stated otherwise. Vectors are written as column vectors. In particular, for x ∈ Rm,

x =

...

xm

 . (1.1) The vectors e1, . . . , e m, where ei has a 1 in position i and zeros elsewhere, form the standard basis 

of Rm. Every vector can be written as 

x = x1e1 + · · · + xmem. (1.2) The coefficients x1, . . . , x m are called the standard coordinates of x. They are the entries of the displayed column vector. 2Section 1.1 3

For x, y ∈ Rm, their dot product is

xiyi. (1.3) Its geometric interpretation as an inner product is developed below. 

1.1.1 Vectors, Matrices, and Linear Maps 

The word vector does not necessarily mean a list of numbers. It means an element of a set on which addition and scalar multiplication behave linearly. 

Definition 1.1 (Vector space) . A real vector space V is a set equipped with vector addition and scalar multiplication. It is closed under linear combinations, and for every x, y, z ∈ V and a, b ∈ R,

x + y = y + x, (x + y) + z = x + ( y + z), (1.4) 

x + 0 = x, x + ( −x) = 0 , (1.5) 

a(x + y) = ax + ay, (a + b)x = ax + bx, (1.6) (ab )x = a(bx ), 1x = x. (1.7) The most familiar example is Rn. Other examples include matrices Rm×n and polynomials of bounded degree. For now, we focus on these spaces with finitely many scalar coordinates. Given vectors v1, . . . , v k ∈ V , a vector of the form 

a1v1 + · · · + akvk (1.8) is a linear combination . The set of all such combinations is their span, span {v1, . . . , v k} = 

> {k∑
> i=1

aivi : ai ∈ R

> }

. (1.9) The vectors are linearly independent if ∑ki=1 aivi = 0 implies ai = 0 for every i. Thus, independence means that none of the vectors can be reconstructed from the others. A vector space is finite-dimensional if it is spanned by a finite collection of vectors. All vector spaces in Part I are finite-dimensional. 

Example 1.2 (A stacked sequence) . Suppose an optimization problem chooses vectors ut ∈ Rm

indexed by t = 0 , . . . , T − 1. The entire sequence can be stacked into one vector,

Definition 1.3 (Linear map) . A map L : V → W is linear if 

L(ax + by ) = aL (x) + bL (y) (1.11) for all x, y ∈ V and a, b ∈ R.Using the standard coordinates of Rn and Rm, every linear map L : Rn → Rm has a matrix representation: there is a matrix A ∈ Rm×n such that L(x) = Ax . Matrix multiplication then represents composition: if M (y) = By , then ( M ◦ L)( x) = BAx .Two sets associated with a linear map describe what it removes and what it can produce: ker( A) = {x ∈ Rn : Ax = 0 }, (1.12) range( A) = {Ax : x ∈ Rn}. (1.13) The kernel is also called the null space, and the range of a matrix is its column space. The kernel contains directions removed by the map, while the range contains every output the map can produce. An affine map has the form x 7 → Ax + b. It need not be linear because it may not map the origin to the origin. Affine maps are nevertheless central to optimization because many constraints and approximations have this form. For A ∈ Rm×n, the transpose AT ∈ Rn×m is defined by ( AT)ij = Aji : it exchanges rows and columns. Its geometric role is explained after inner products are defined. 

1.1.2 Norms, Inner Products, and Distances 

A vector space describes which algebraic operations are possible, but it does not by itself specify the size of a vector or whether two vectors are close. These geometric notions are introduced by norms and inner products. 

Definition 1.4 (Norm) . A norm on V is a function ∥·∥ : V → R satisfying, for all x, y ∈ V and 

a ∈ R,

∥x∥ ≥ 0, ∥x∥ = 0 ⇐⇒ x = 0 , (1.14) 

∥ax ∥ = |a|∥ x∥, (1.15) 

∥x + y∥ ≤ ∥ x∥ + ∥y∥. (1.16) The last property is the triangle inequality. Section 1.1 5

The most common norms on Rn are

d(x, y ) = ∥x − y∥ (1.20) and therefore defines neighborhoods and convergence: xk → x means ∥xk − x∥ → 0. The open ball of radius r centered at x is 

Br(x) = {y ∈ V : ∥y − x∥ < r }. (1.21) A set is open if every point in it is the center of some open ball contained in the set. A function f

is continuous at x if f (xk) → f (x) whenever xk → x. These definitions make the words “nearby” and “small change” precise. On Rn, all norms define the same convergent sequences and open sets, although the shapes of their balls differ. 

Definition 1.5 (Inner product) . An inner product on a real vector space V is a function ⟨· , ·⟩ :

V × V → R that is symmetric, linear in each argument, and positive definite: 

⟨x, y ⟩ = ⟨y, x ⟩, ⟨x, x ⟩ > 0 for x̸ = 0 . (1.22) Every inner product induces a norm, 

∥x∥ =

⟨x, x ⟩. (1.23) The standard inner product on Rn is the dot product 

⟨x, y ⟩ = xTy =

⟨Ax, y ⟩ = ⟨x, A Ty⟩, x ∈ Rn, y ∈ Rm. (1.25) The linear map that moves an operation between the two arguments in this way is called its 

adjoint . Thus AT is the adjoint of x 7 → Ax under the standard Euclidean inner products. 6 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

An eigenvector of a square matrix W is a nonzero vector v satisfying 

W v = λv, (1.26) where the scalar λ is the corresponding eigenvalue . Multiplying an eigenvector by W therefore changes only its magnitude, not its direction. A real symmetric matrix has mutually orthogonal unit eigenvectors and can be written 

W = U ΛU T, (1.27) where the columns of U are eigenvectors and the diagonal entries of Λ are their eigenvalues. A symmetric matrix W is called positive definite , written W ≻ 0, if xTW x > 0 for every nonzero 

x. For a symmetric matrix, this is equivalent to requiring every eigenvalue to be positive. Indeed, with z = U Tx,

xTW x = zTΛz =

> n∑
> i=1

λiz2 

> i

. (1.28) Thus the quadratic form is positive in every nonzero direction precisely when each λi > 0. For such a matrix, 

⟨x, y ⟩W = xTW y, ∥x∥W = √xTW x (1.29) define a weighted inner product and norm. Along a unit eigenvector vi, ∥vi∥W = √λi. Large eigenvalues therefore make their directions more expensive and make the weighted unit ball narrower along those directions. 

Proposition 1.6 (Cauchy–Schwarz inequality) . For any inner-product space and any x, y ∈ V ,

|⟨ x, y ⟩| ≤ ∥ x∥∥ y∥. (1.30) 

Equality holds if and only if x and y are linearly dependent. 

For nonzero vectors, the angle θ ∈ [0 , π ] between them is consequently well defined by cos θ = ⟨x, y ⟩∥x∥∥ y∥ . (1.31) Vectors are orthogonal when ⟨x, y ⟩ = 0. Orthogonality is more than a visual notion: it identifies directions whose effects decouple under the chosen inner product. 

Example 1.7 (Geometry of a quadratic function) . Let Q ≻ 0 and consider J(x) = xTQx . For 

c > 0, the set {x : J(x) = c}, called a level set, is an ellipsoid. In the decomposition Q = U ΛU T,the eigenvectors give the principal axes and the eigenvalues determine the scaling along those axes. A large eigenvalue produces a narrow direction in the level set and a rapidly changing direction in the function. 

1.1.3 Subspaces, Bases, and Orthogonal Projections 

A subset of a vector space is a subspace when it retains the vector-space structure. Section 1.1 7

Definition 1.8 (Subspace) . A nonempty subset S ⊆ V is a linear subspace if 

ax + by ∈ S (1.32) for every x, y ∈ S and a, b ∈ R.The span of any collection of vectors is a subspace. Kernels and ranges of linear maps are also subspaces. By contrast, an affine set 

x0 + S = {x0 + s : s ∈ S} (1.33) is a translated subspace and need not contain the origin. For example, the solution set of Ax = b,when nonempty, is affine: 

{x : Ax = b} = xp + ker( A), (1.34) where xp is any particular solution. The null space therefore describes all feasible directions along which an equality-constrained variable can move. 

Definition 1.9 (Basis and dimension) . A basis of a vector space V is a linearly independent collection B = {v1, . . . , v k} that spans V. Given this basis, every x ∈ V has a unique representation 

x =

> i=1

xivi. (1.35) The scalars x1, . . . , x k are the coordinates of x with respect to B. The number k of basis vectors is the dimension of V.

Change of basis. Coordinates describe a vector relative to a chosen basis, so the coordinate column changes when the basis changes even though the underlying vector does not. In Rn, let 

B =

S = C−1B. (1.38) It converts coordinates in basis B to coordinates in basis C:

xC = Sx B , xB = S−1xC . (1.39) 

Linear maps under a change of basis. Let L : Rn → Rn be a linear operator. Denote its matrix in basis B by AB and its matrix in basis C by AC , so that (Lx )B = AB xB , (Lx )C = AC xC . (1.40) 8 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

Using xC = Sx B gives 

AC = SA B S−1. (1.41) This is a similarity transformation. The entries of the matrix change, but the linear operator and its eigenvalues do not. More generally, if input coordinates change by xnew = Sx old and output coordinates by ynew = T y old , then 

Anew = T A old S−1. (1.42) 

Inner products under a change of basis. In basis B, an inner product is represented by a symmetric positive-definite Gram matrix GB :

⟨x, y ⟩ = xT 

> B

GB yB . (1.43) Because xC = Sx B , the matrix in basis C is 

GC = ( S−1)TGB S−1. (1.44) The coordinates and the Gram matrix change together, so lengths and angles do not change. In an orthonormal basis, G = I. For the standard Euclidean inner product in a general basis matrix 

C, the Gram matrix is GC = CTC.The rank of a matrix is the dimension of its range. For A ∈ Rm×n, the rank–nullity relation always states that dim ker( A) + dim range( A) = n. (1.45) The right-hand side is n because the domain of A is Rn. Since rank( A) ≤ min {m, n }, if m < n 

then dim ker( A) = n − rank( A) ≥ n − m > 0. (1.46) Thus a map from a higher-dimensional input space to a lower-dimensional output space must remove some nonzero input directions. If A has full row rank m, its null space has dimension exactly n − m; if its rank is smaller, the null space is even larger. A basis {v1, . . . , v n} is orthonormal if 

⟨vi, v j ⟩ =

Hence coordinates in an orthonormal basis preserve inner products, lengths, and angles. In Rn with the standard inner product, let V = [ v1 · · · vn]. Orthonormality is equivalent to 

V TV = V V T = I, V −1 = V T. (1.50) The coordinate conversion is therefore xV = V Tx, and ∥xV ∥2 = ∥x∥2. Its 2-norm condition number is 1, so changing to an orthonormal basis does not amplify vector lengths or small numerical errors. For a subspace S, its orthogonal complement is 

S⊥ = {z ∈ V : ⟨z, s ⟩ = 0 for all s ∈ S} . (1.51) In finite dimensions, every vector can be decomposed uniquely into a component in S and a component in S⊥.

Theorem 1.10 (Orthogonal projection) . Let S be a linear subspace of a finite-dimensional inner-product space V. For every x ∈ V , there is a unique vector s⋆ ∈ S satisfying 

s⋆ = arg min 

> s∈S

∥x − s∥. (1.52) 

It is characterized by the orthogonality condition 

x − s⋆ ∈ S ⊥. (1.53) 

The vector s⋆ is the orthogonal projection of x onto S.

Suppose the columns of B ∈ Rn×k form a basis for S. Every s ∈ S can be written as s = Bz .Projecting x therefore means solving 

z⋆ = arg min 

> z

∥x − Bz ∥22. (1.54) The residual must be orthogonal to every column of B, giving the normal equations 

BT(Bz ⋆ − x) = 0 . (1.55) If the columns of B are linearly independent, then 

z⋆ = ( BTB)−1BTx, s⋆ = B(BTB)−1BT

Example 1.11 (Projection as least squares) . If the equation Bz = x has no exact solution, x

does not belong to the column space of B. The least-squares solution chooses Bz ⋆ as the point in that column space nearest to x. Its residual x − Bz ⋆ is orthogonal to the column space. Thus least squares is fundamentally a geometric projection problem, not merely an algebraic recipe. 10 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

Section summary. Vector spaces provide the algebra of optimization variables; norms determine size and proximity; inner products add angles and orthogonality; and projections find the closest point in a subspace. The remaining sections build differential calculus and optimality conditions on top of this geometry. 

Exercise 1.12. Show that 

S = {x ∈ R3 : x1 + x2 + x3 = 0 } (1.57) is a subspace. Find a basis and its dimension. 

Short solution. The set is the kernel of the linear map x 7 → x1 + x2 + x3, so it is a subspace. Every element has the form (a, b, −a − b) = a(1 , 0, −1) + b(0 , 1, −1) . (1.58) The two displayed vectors are independent, so they form a basis and dim S = 2. 

Exercise 1.13. Let v1 = (1 , 1) T and v2 = (1 , −1) T. Find the coordinates of x = (3 , 1) T in the basis {v1, v 2}.

Short solution. Solving x = av 1 + bv 2 gives a + b = 3 and a − b = 1. Hence a = 2, b = 1, and the coordinate column is (2 , 1) T.

# 1.2 Differential Calculus in Vector Spaces 

Optimization asks how a function changes when its argument changes. Derivatives provide a local linear description of that change. We begin with scalar-valued functions and then extend the definitions to vector-valued maps. 

1.2.1 Directional and Total Derivatives 

Let f : Rn → R and fix a point x ∈ Rn. A direction d ∈ Rn defines the one-dimensional curve 

t 7 → x + td . The derivative of f along this curve measures the instantaneous change of f in direction d.

Definition 1.22 (Directional derivative) . The directional derivative of f at x in direction d is 

Df (x)[ d] = lim 

> t→0

f (x + td ) − f (x)

t , (1.69) provided the limit exists. For a coordinate vector ei, the directional derivative Df (x)[ ei] is the partial derivative ∂f (x)/∂x i.Directional derivatives are therefore a coordinate-free extension of partial derivatives. Their existence in every direction, however, does not by itself guarantee that one linear map describes all small perturbations consistently. The reason is that a directional derivative examines one fixed line at a time. A genuine derivative must give one linear approximation that remains accurate for perturbations approaching the point from any direction. In particular, if f is differentiable, the map d 7 → Df (x)[ d] must be linear in d.For example, define f : R2 → R by 

f (x1, x 2) = 

> 

x31

x21 + x22

, (x1, x 2)̸ = (0 , 0) ,

0, (x1, x 2) = (0 , 0) .

(1.70) Section 1.2 13 

For a nonzero direction d = ( d1, d 2)T,

Df (0)[ d] = lim 

> t→0

f (td ) − f (0) 

t = d31

d21 + d22

. (1.71) Thus every directional derivative exists. Nevertheless, 

Df (0)[ e1] = 1 , Df (0)[ e2] = 0 , Df (0)[ e1 + e2] = 12 . (1.72) The last value is not the sum of the first two, so the directional derivatives do not form a linear map. Consequently, f is not differentiable at the origin, even though it is continuous there and has a derivative along every line. 

Definition 1.23 (Total derivative) . A function f : Rn → Rm is differentiable at x if there exists a linear map L : Rn → Rm such that 

f (x + h) = f (x) + Lh + r(h), lim 

> ∥h∥2→0

∥r(h)∥2

∥h∥2

= 0 . (1.73) The unique map L is the total derivative of f at x, denoted Df (x). The remainder condition is also written r(h) = o(∥h∥2). Equation (1.73) says that the error of the linear approximation becomes negligible compared with the size of the perturbation. Differentiability implies continuity. It also implies that every directional derivative exists and satisfies 

Df (x)[ d] = Df (x)d. (1.74) The expression on the left emphasizes a directional rate of change; the one on the right emphasizes application of a linear map. 

Example 1.24 (A linear and an affine map) . For f (x) = Ax , the derivative is Df (x) = A at every 

x, and the remainder is zero. The same derivative is obtained for the affine map f (x) = Ax + b

because the constant vector b disappears under subtraction. Differentiable maps compose according to the chain rule. 

Theorem 1.25 (Chain rule) . Let f : Rn → Rm be differentiable at x, and let g : Rm → Rp be differentiable at f (x). Then g ◦ f is differentiable at x and 

D(g ◦ f )( x) = Dg (f (x)) Df (x). (1.75) The order in (1.75) follows the order of function composition: a perturbation first passes through 

Df (x) and then through Dg (f (x)). 

1.2.2 Gradients, Jacobians, and Hessians 

In Euclidean coordinates, derivatives are represented by familiar vectors and matrices. 14 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

Definition 1.26 (Gradient) . For a differentiable scalar function f : Rn → R, the gradient 

∇f (x) ∈ Rn is the unique vector satisfying 

Df (x)[ d] = ⟨∇ f (x), d ⟩ = ∇f (x)Td for every d ∈ Rn. (1.76) In standard coordinates, 

∇f (x) = 

[ ∂f ∂x 1 (x) · · · ∂f ∂x n (x)

]T

. (1.77) The gradient points in the direction of greatest instantaneous increase under the Euclidean norm. Indeed, Cauchy–Schwarz gives 

Df (x)[ d] ≤ ∥∇ f (x)∥2∥d∥2, (1.78) with equality among unit directions when d = ∇f (x)/∥∇ f (x)∥2. Consequently, −∇ f (x) is the direction of steepest instantaneous decrease. 

Definition 1.27 (Jacobian) . For f : Rn → Rm with component functions f1, . . . , f m, the Jacobian is the matrix

Jg◦f (x) = Jg(f (x)) Jf (x). (1.80) If g : Rm → R is scalar-valued, then 

∇(g ◦ f )( x) = Jf (x)T∇g(f (x)) . (1.81) The transpose in (1.81) follows directly from the adjoint identity introduced in equation (1.25).

Example 1.29 (Derivative of a composition) . Let f (x) = 12 ∥Ax − b∥22. Define r(x) = Ax − b and

1.2.3 Taylor Expansions and Local Models 

Taylor expansions use derivative information to approximate f (x + h) when the displacement h is small. For a differentiable function, the first-order model built at x is 

m1(h; x) = f (x) + ∇f (x)Th. (1.87) Here h is the model variable, while the argument after the semicolon, x, identifies the fixed point at which the model is built. 

Theorem 1.30 (First-order Taylor expansion) . If f : Rn → R is differentiable at x, then 

f (x + h) = f (x) + ∇f (x)Th + o(∥h∥2). (1.88) If second derivatives exist and are continuous, curvature can also be included. 

Theorem 1.31 (Second-order Taylor expansion) . Suppose f is twice continuously differentiable on an open set containing the line segment from x to x + h. Then there exists θ ∈ (0 , 1) such that 

f (x + h) = f (x) + ∇f (x)Th + 12 hT∇2f (x + θh )h. (1.89) 

As h → 0,

f (x + h) = f (x) + ∇f (x)Th + 12 hT∇2f (x)h + o(∥h∥22). (1.90) The corresponding second-order local model is 

m2(h; x) = f (x) + ∇f (x)Th + 12 hT∇2f (x)h. (1.91) Many numerical algorithms choose h by approximately minimizing either the linear model m1 or the quadratic model m2 while controlling how large the step may be. 

Example 1.32 (A one-dimensional local model) . For f (x) = ex, the expansion around x = 0 is 

eh = 1 + h + 12 h2 + o(h2). (1.92) Near zero, the linear model 1 + h gives the slope, while the quadratic term h2/2 captures the 16 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

upward curvature.

# 1.3 Unconstrained Optimality Conditions 

We now use local derivative information to characterize candidate solutions of min  

> x∈Rn

f (x). (1.108) This compact expression specifies an optimization problem: • x = ( x1, . . . , x n)T is the decision variable , the vector whose entries are to be chosen; • x ∈ Rn states that every candidate choice is a real vector with n components; • f : Rn → R is the objective function , which assigns a scalar cost f (x) to each choice x; and • min asks for a choice of x whose objective value is no larger than that of any other admissible choice. Thus, the variable x is the input being selected, whereas f (x) is the number being made as small as possible. If a solution exists, a minimizer is usually denoted by x⋆ and satisfies 

f (x⋆) ≤ f (x) for every x ∈ Rn. (1.109) The vector x⋆ is an optimal solution , while the scalar 

f ⋆ = f (x⋆) = min  

> x∈Rn

f (x) (1.110) is the optimal value . These objects should not be confused. When several points attain the same smallest value, the complete set of solutions is written arg min 

> x∈Rn

f (x) = {x ∈ Rn : f (x) = f ⋆}. (1.111) The notation x⋆ ∈ arg min f selects one member of this set. A minimum need not exist. To distinguish existence of an optimizer from the best achievable bound, it is useful to define minimum, infimum, maximum, and supremum more formally. Section 1.3 19 

Definition 1.43 (Minimum, infimum, maximum, and supremum) . Let X be a nonempty feasible set and let f : X → R. A number α is a lower bound for f on X if 

α ≤ f (x) for every x ∈ X . (1.112) If f is bounded below, its infimum is the unique number α = inf x∈X f (x) satisfying 

α ≤ f (x) for every x ∈ X , (1.113) 

f (xϵ) < α + ϵ for every ϵ > 0 and some xϵ ∈ X . (1.114) Thus f never falls below its infimum, but its values can be found arbitrarily close to the infimum from above. The infimum is a minimum if it is attained: there exists x⋆ ∈ X such that 

f (x⋆) = inf  

> x∈X

f (x). (1.115) In that case, min x∈X f (x) = f (x⋆) and x⋆ ∈ arg min x∈X f (x). Similarly, β is an upper bound if f (x) ≤ β for every x ∈ X . If f is bounded above, its supremum 

is the unique number β = sup x∈X f (x) satisfying 

f (x) ≤ β for every x ∈ X , (1.116) 

β − ϵ < f (xϵ) for every ϵ > 0 and some xϵ ∈ X . (1.117) It is a maximum when some x⋆ attains it. Then 

f (x⋆) = max  

> x∈X

f (x) = sup 

> x∈X

f (x), x⋆ ∈ arg max 

> x∈X

f (x). (1.118) Thus inf and sup describe limiting objective values and do not assert that an optimizing point exists. By contrast, writing min or max asserts that the corresponding value is attained. For example, f (x) = ex on R has inf  

> x∈R

ex = 0 , sup 

> x∈R

ex = + ∞, (1.119) but it has neither a minimum nor a maximum. Extended values such as + ∞ or −∞ indicate that the objective is unbounded in the corresponding direction. The word unconstrained refers to the admissible choices. Here the feasible set is all of Rn: there are no additional equality constraints, inequality constraints, or boundary restrictions. Consequently, from any point x, every direction d ∈ Rn is locally allowable, since x + td ∈ Rn for all sufficiently small positive and negative t.Unconstrained does not mean that the problem is easy or that the objective has a unique minimum. The function may be nonconvex, may have many local minima and saddle points, or may fail to attain a minimum. The absence of constraints only describes the feasible set. This distinction explains the first-order condition developed below. At an unconstrained local minimizer, movement is possible in both d and −d, so the directional derivative must vanish in every direction. In a constrained problem, some directions may be forbidden by the feasible-set 20 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

boundary, and an optimum may therefore have a nonzero gradient. 

1.3.1 Local and Global Minima 

Definition 1.44 (Global and local minimizers) . A point x⋆ ∈ Rn is a global minimizer of f if 

f (x⋆) ≤ f (x) for every x ∈ Rn. (1.120) It is a local minimizer if there exists r > 0 such that 

f (x⋆) ≤ f (x) whenever ∥x − x⋆∥2 < r. (1.121) The minimizer is strict if equality occurs only at x⋆. More precisely, there is an r > 0 such that 

f (x⋆) < f (x) whenever 0 < ∥x − x⋆∥2 < r. (1.122) Here < is a strict inequality : it excludes equality, unlike the non-strict inequality ≤. Thus every sufficiently close point distinct from x⋆ has a genuinely larger objective value. A non-strict local minimum may instead lie on a flat region containing other nearby points with the same objective value. Every global minimizer is local, but a local minimizer need not be global. As the preceding definitions emphasize, even a finite infimum need not be attained. Existence is a separate question from optimality. In the examples below, the quadratic structure will make existence easy to check directly. 

1.3.2 First-Order Necessary Conditions 

Definition 1.45 (Stationary point) . A differentiable function has a stationary point at x if 

∇f (x) = 0 . (1.123) 

Theorem 1.46 (First-order necessary condition) . If f : Rn → R is differentiable and x⋆ is a local minimizer, then 

∇f (x⋆) = 0 . (1.124) Intuitively, imagine the graph of f as a smooth landscape. The gradient ∇f (x) points in the direction of greatest local increase, while −∇ f (x) points downhill. If the gradient at x⋆ were nonzero, then moving a sufficiently small distance in the direction d = −∇ f (x⋆) would decrease the objective because 

Df (x⋆)[ d] = −∥∇ f (x⋆)∥22 < 0. (1.125) That would contradict the assumption that x⋆ is a local minimizer. Therefore a differentiable objective cannot have a nonzero slope in any direction at an unconstrained local minimum. Section 1.3 21 

Equivalently, restrict the function to any line through the point by defining ϕ(t) = f (x⋆ + td ). Because both positive and negative small values of t are allowable, t = 0 must minimize this one-dimensional function, and hence 

ϕ′(0) = ∇f (x⋆)Td = 0 . (1.126) This equality holds for every direction d. Choosing d = ∇f (x⋆) gives ∥∇ f (x⋆)∥22 = 0, so the gradient must be zero. Stationarity is necessary but generally not sufficient. For example, f (x) = x3 has f ′(0) = 0, yet values on one side of zero are smaller and values on the other side are larger. A stationary point may be a minimum, a maximum, or neither. 

1.3.3 Second-Order Necessary and Sufficient Conditions 

First derivatives detect slope; second derivatives distinguish different types of stationary points through curvature. 

Definition 1.47 (Matrix definiteness) . A symmetric matrix H is positive semidefinite, written 

H ⪰ 0, if dTHd ≥ 0 for every d. It is positive definite, written H ≻ 0, if the inequality is strict for every nonzero d. Negative semidefiniteness and negative definiteness are defined by reversing the inequalities. A square matrix is singular if it is not invertible. 

Theorem 1.48 (Second-order necessary condition) . If f is twice differentiable in a neighborhood of a local minimizer x⋆, then 

∇f (x⋆) = 0 , ∇2f (x⋆) ⪰ 0. (1.127) The curvature condition follows by restricting f to any line through x⋆: its second derivative is 

dT∇2f (x⋆)d and cannot be negative at a local minimum. 

Theorem 1.49 (Second-order sufficient condition) . Suppose f is twice continuously differentiable near x⋆ and 

∇f (x⋆) = 0 , ∇2f (x⋆) ≻ 0. (1.128) 

Then x⋆ is a strict local minimizer. 

Positive semidefiniteness alone is inconclusive. Both f (x) = x4 and f (x) = x3 have zero gradient and zero Hessian at the origin, but the former has a strict minimum there and the latter does not. Higher-order terms determine the answer when the Hessian is singular. A stationary point whose Hessian has both positive and negative curvature directions is called a 

saddle point . Equivalently, the symmetric Hessian has at least one positive eigenvalue and at least one negative eigenvalue; such a matrix is called indefinite . If v+ and v− are corresponding unit eigenvectors with eigenvalues λ+ > 0 and λ− < 0, then 

vT

> +

∇2f (x)v+ = λ+ > 0, vT

> −

∇2f (x)v− = λ− < 0. (1.129) 22 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

Thus the function curves upward along v+ and downward along v−, so the stationary point cannot be a local minimum or a local maximum. 

Exercise 1.50. Classify all stationary points of f (x1, x 2) = x21 − x22 using its Hessian. 

Short solution. ∇f = (2 x1, −2x2)T, so the only stationary point is (0 , 0). The Hessian is diag(2 , −2), which has one positive and one negative eigenvalue. Hence the origin is a saddle point. 

Exercise 1.51. Find and classify the stationary points of f (x) = x4 − 2x2. Which minima are global? 

Short solution. f ′(x) = 4 x(x2 − 1), so the stationary points are 0 and ±1. Since f ′′ (x) = 12 x2 − 4, the origin is a strict local maximum and ±1 are strict local minima. Because f (x) = ( x2 − 1) 2 − 1 ≥−1, both minima are global. 

Exercise 1.52. Find and classify the stationary point of f (x1, x 2) = x21 + 4 x22 − 2x1 + 8 x2.

Short solution. ∇f = (2 x1 − 2, 8x2 + 8) T, so x⋆ = (1 , −1). The Hessian diag(2 , 8) is positive definite; therefore x⋆ is the unique strict global minimizer. 

Exercise 1.53. Classify the origin for f (x1, x 2) = x41 + x22. Why is the second-order test alone inconclusive there? 

Short solution. The gradient vanishes at the origin, where the Hessian is diag(0 , 2), which is positive semidefinite but not positive definite. Nevertheless, f (x1, x 2) > 0 = f (0 , 0) for every nonzero point, so the origin is a strict global minimizer. The fourth-order term supplies the missing curvature in the x1 direction. 

Exercise 1.54. Classify the origin for f (x1, x 2) = x31 + x22 and compare its gradient and Hessian there with those in the previous exercise. 

Short solution. At the origin the gradient is zero and the Hessian is again diag(0 , 2). However, 

f (x1, 0) = x31 is positive for x1 > 0 and negative for x1 < 0. Thus the origin is a saddle point. This shows that a singular positive-semidefinite Hessian does not determine the classification. 

Exercise 1.55. For 

fa(x1, x 2) = x21 + 2 ax 1x2 + x22, (1.130) classify the origin for all a ∈ R.

Short solution. The Hessian has eigenvalues 2(1 + a) and 2(1 − a). If |a| < 1, both are positive and the origin is a strict global minimum. If |a| > 1, their signs differ and the origin is a saddle point. If |a| = 1, the function is ( x1 ± x2)2, so the origin is a non-strict global minimum lying on a line of minimizers. 

Exercise 1.56. Find and classify the stationary point of f (x1, x 2) = −x21 − 3x22 + 4 x1 − 6x2.Section 1.4 23 

Short solution. Solving ∇f = ( −2x1 + 4 , −6x2 − 6) T = 0 gives (2 , −1). The Hessian diag( −2, −6) is negative definite, so this point is the unique strict global maximum and is not a minimum. 

Exercise 1.57. Find and classify every stationary point of f (x1, x 2) = ( x21 + x22 − 1) 2.

Short solution. The gradient is 4( x21 + x22 − 1)( x1, x 2)T. Thus the stationary set is the origin together with the unit circle. The origin has Hessian −4I and is a strict local maximum. Every point on the unit circle is a non-strict global minimum because f = 0 there and nearby points on the circle have the same value. 

Exercise 1.58. Classify the origin for f (x1, x 2) = x21 − x42. What does this example show about the second-order necessary condition? 

Short solution. The gradient vanishes and the Hessian at the origin is diag(2 , 0) ⪰ 0. Yet f (x1, 0) >

0 for x1̸ = 0, while f (0 , x 2) < 0 for x2̸ = 0. Hence the origin is a saddle point. Positive semidefiniteness is necessary at a local minimum but is not sufficient. 

Exercise 1.59. Let 

f (x) = 12 xTHx + cTx, (1.131) where H = HT ≻ 0. Find the stationary point and prove directly that it is the unique global minimizer. 

Short solution. Since ∇f (x) = Hx + c, the stationary point is x⋆ = −H−1c. Completing the square gives 

f (x) = f (x⋆) + 12 (x − x⋆)TH(x − x⋆). (1.132) The final term is positive for every x̸ = x⋆, proving uniqueness and global optimality. 

# 1.4 Canonical Optimization Examples 

The following problems illustrate how geometry and derivatives combine in models that recur throughout optimization. 

1.4.1 Least-Squares Problems 

Given A ∈ Rm×n and observations b ∈ Rm, the linear least-squares problem is min  

> x∈Rn

f (x), f (x) = 12 ∥Ax − b∥22. (1.133) The residual r(x) = Ax − b measures disagreement between the model Ax and the data b. From equation (1.85), 

∇f (x) = AT(Ax − b), ∇2f (x) = ATA ⪰ 0. (1.134) 24 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

Every minimizer therefore satisfies the normal equations 

ATAx = ATb. (1.135) If the columns of A are linearly independent, then ATA ≻ 0 and the minimizer is unique: 

x⋆ = ( ATA)−1ATb. (1.136) Geometrically, Ax ⋆ is the orthogonal projection of b onto the column space of A, and b − Ax ⋆ is orthogonal to that space. If the columns are dependent, minimizers may not be unique, but all of them produce the same fitted vector Ax ⋆.A weighted least-squares model assigns different importance to different residual directions: min 

> x

12 (Ax − b)TW (Ax − b), W ≻ 0. (1.137) Its normal equations are ATW Ax = ATW b .

1.4.2 Quadratic Optimization 

A general unconstrained quadratic function can be written 

f (x) = 12 xTQx + cTx + r, (1.138) where Q = QT, c ∈ Rn, and r ∈ R. Any nonsymmetric quadratic matrix can be replaced by its symmetric part because xTQx = xT(Q + QT)x/ 2. The derivatives are 

∇f (x) = Qx + c, ∇2f (x) = Q. (1.139) If Q ≻ 0, the unique stationary point 

x⋆ = −Q−1c (1.140) is the unique global minimizer. Completing the square makes this explicit: 

f (x) = f (x⋆) + 12 (x − x⋆)TQ(x − x⋆). (1.141) If Q ⪰ 0 but is singular, a minimizer exists only when the equation Qx = −c is solvable. Here 

solvable means that there is at least one vector xp satisfying Qx p = −c; equivalently, −c belongs to the range of Q. Because a singular matrix has no inverse, one cannot simply write x = −Q−1c,and the system may have either no solution or infinitely many solutions. When one solution xp exists, every solution has the form 

x = xp + z, z ∈ ker( Q), (1.142) Section 1.4 25 

and all such vectors are global minimizers. Indeed, 

f (x) = f (xp) + 12 (x − xp)TQ(x − xp) ≥ f (xp). (1.143) Moving in a null-space direction does not change the objective. Since Q is symmetric, solvability is also equivalent to c being orthogonal to every vector in ker( Q). If this condition fails, the linear term decreases without bound along one of those zero-curvature directions, so no minimizer exists. If Q has a negative eigenvalue, the objective tends to −∞ along a suitable direction and has no global minimizer. Quadratic functions are especially important because the second-order Taylor model of any twice differentiable objective is quadratic. 

1.4.3 Regularization and Ill-Posed Problems 

An optimization problem is informally called ill-posed when a solution fails to exist, is not unique, or changes excessively under small changes in the data. A matrix is rank deficient when its rank is smaller than its number of columns. Rank-deficient least squares illustrates the nonuniqueness issue. Even when ATA is invertible, very small eigenvalues can make the solution sensitive to perturbations in b. To see why, write 

ATA = U ΛU T, Λ = diag( λ1, . . . , λ n), (1.144) where every λi > 0. In the eigenvector coordinates of ATA, the least-squares solution is 

x⋆ =

> n∑
> i=1

uT 

> i

ATbλi

ui. (1.145) Thus each component is obtained by dividing by an eigenvalue. If the data changes from b to 

b + δb , the resulting change δx satisfies (ATA)δx = ATδb. (1.146) Writing δg = ATδb , its component along ui is 

uT 

> i

δx = uT 

> i

δg λi

Geometrically, the least-squares objective has curvature λi along ui, so a small eigenvalue describes a nearly flat direction: the objective changes very little as x moves in that direction, and the location of its minimizer can move substantially under a small data perturbation. The ratio λmax (ATA)/λ min (ATA) is the spectral condition number of ATA; a large ratio signals this numerical sensitivity. Regularization modifies the objective to prefer solutions with additional structure. The most 26 Chapter 1. Optimization in Vector Spaces 

common smooth example is Tikhonov, or ridge, regularization: min 

> x

12 ∥Ax − b∥22 + λ

2 ∥x∥22, λ > 0. (1.148) Its Hessian is ATA + λI ≻ 0, so the minimizer is unique even when A has dependent columns: 

xλ = ( ATA + λI )−1ATb. (1.149) The parameter λ controls a tradeoff. A larger value reduces the size and data sensitivity of xλ but generally increases the residual ∥Ax λ − b∥2.More generally, one may solve min  

> x

f (x) + λR (x), (1.150) where R expresses a preference such as small magnitude, smoothness, or few nonzero coordinates. The function R(x) = ∥x∥1 favors solutions with many zero entries, but it is not differentiable when a coordinate is zero. Later algorithmic tools will handle such objectives without pretending that an ordinary gradient exists there.

Chapter summary. Finite-dimensional optimization rests on a small set of ideas. Vector-space geometry defines directions and distances; derivatives create local linear and quadratic models; optimality conditions identify and classify candidates; and least-squares, quadratic, and regularized objectives provide canonical examples. The next chapter adds convexity, a global geometric structure under which local information becomes much more powerful. 2

# Convex Optimization and Standard Problems 

Chapter map. Convex geometry, global optimality, and the problem forms most useful in engineering. 

Convexity is valuable because it turns local information into global information. For a convex problem, a local minimum is global, and first-order conditions are often enough to certify a solution. We develop only the tools needed later for constrained optimization, control, and learning. 

# 2.1 Convex Sets and Functions 

2.1.1 Convex Sets and Convex Combinations 

Definition 2.1 (Convex set) . A set C ⊆ Rn is convex if 

θx + (1 − θ)y ∈ C (2.1) for every x, y ∈ C and θ ∈ [0 , 1]. Thus the line segment between any two points of C remains inside C.A convex combination of x1, . . . , x k is 

> k

∑

> i=1

θixi, θi ≥ 0,

> k

∑

> i=1

θi = 1 . (2.2) The convex hull conv( S) is the set of all finite convex combinations of points in S; it is the smallest convex set containing S.29 30 Chapter 2. Convex Optimization and Standard Problems 

Several important sets are convex: • An affine set has the form 

x0 + S = {x0 + s : s ∈ S} , (2.3) where S is a linear subspace. If two points belong to this set, every point on the line through them also belongs to it, and therefore so does the line segment between them. Solution sets of linear equations, {x : Ax = b}, are affine whenever they are nonempty. • A norm ball centered at xc with radius r ≥ 0 is 

{x : ∥x − xc∥ ≤ r}. (2.4) If x and y lie in the ball, the triangle inequality gives 

∥θx + (1 − θ)y − xc∥ ≤ θ∥x − xc∥ + (1 − θ)∥y − xc∥ (2.5) 

≤ r, (2.6) so the segment joining them remains in the ball. This argument applies to every norm, not only the Euclidean norm. • A halfspace is a set 

{x : aTx ≤ b}. (2.7) For feasible x and y,

aT(θx + (1 − θ)y) = θa Tx + (1 − θ)aTy ≤ b, (2.8) which proves convexity. Geometrically, the hyperplane {x : aTx = b} divides space into two halfspaces. Convexity is also preserved by several constructions. The intersection ⋂ 

> j∈J

Cj of any collection of convex sets is convex: two points in the intersection belong to every Cj , so their entire line segment belongs to every Cj as well. This fact explains why imposing several convex constraints simultaneously still produces a convex feasible set. If C is convex and T (x) = Ax + b is affine, its affine image 

T (C) = {Ax + b : x ∈ C} (2.9) is convex because affine maps preserve convex combinations: 

T (θx + (1 − θ)y) = θT (x) + (1 − θ)T (y). (2.10) Similarly, if D is convex, the inverse image 

T −1(D) = {x : Ax + b ∈ D} (2.11) is convex. These closure rules generate most feasible sets used in engineering from a small collection of elementary building blocks. Section 2.1 31 

2.1.2 Convex Functions and Supporting Models 

Definition 2.2 (Convex function) . A function f : C → R, defined on a convex set C, is convex if 

f (θx + (1 − θ)y) ≤ θf (x) + (1 − θ)f (y) (2.12) for all x, y ∈ C and θ ∈ [0 , 1]. It is strictly convex if the inequality is strict for x̸ = y and θ ∈ (0 , 1). Geometrically, choose two points on the graph, ( x, f (x)) and ( y, f (y)). The straight chord joining them contains the point (θx + (1 − θ)y, θf (x) + (1 − θ)f (y)). (2.13) At the same horizontal location, the graph has height f (θx + (1 − θ)y). The convexity inequality says that this height is no greater than the height of the chord. Thus the curve lies on or below each chord joining two of its points. For example, the parabola f (x) = x2 bends upward and lies below its chords. By contrast, f (x) = −x2 lies above its chords and is concave. The set lying on or above the graph, epi f = {(x, t ) : t ≥ f (x)}, (2.14) is called the epigraph . A function is convex exactly when its epigraph is a convex set. Affine functions, norms, squared norms, and maxima of affine functions are convex. A function is concave if its negative is convex. For a differentiable function, convexity has a particularly useful characterization. 

Theorem 2.3 (First-order characterization) . A differentiable function on an open convex set is convex if and only if 

f (y) ≥ f (x) + ∇f (x)T(y − x) (2.15) 

for all x, y in its domain. 

Thus every tangent plane of a convex function is a global lower bound. If ∇f (x⋆) = 0, then 

f (y) ≥ f (x⋆) for every y, so x⋆ is globally optimal. 

Advanced note: subgradients. The gradient-based characterization above applies only where 

f is differentiable. A more advanced theory, called subdifferential calculus , extends similar geometric ideas to nonsmooth convex functions. In this theory, a vector g is called a subgradient of f at x if 

f (y) ≥ f (x) + gT(y − x) for every y. (2.16) Thus the affine function f (x) + gT(y − x) is a global supporting lower bound, just as the tangent plane is in the differentiable case. The set of all such vectors is the subdifferential , denoted ∂f (x). For example, the absolute-value function is not differentiable at the origin, but 

∂|x|∣∣x=0 = [ −1, 1] . (2.17) 32 Chapter 2. Convex Optimization and Standard Problems 

The condition 0 ∈ ∂f (x⋆) characterizes a global minimizer of a convex function. We mention this result for context; a systematic treatment of subgradients and nonsmooth algorithms is beyond the scope of this chapter. 

2.1.3 Curvature, Strong Convexity, and Smoothness 

If f is twice continuously differentiable, it is convex exactly when 

∇2f (x) ⪰ 0 (2.18) throughout its convex domain. Positive definiteness everywhere is sufficient for strict convexity. A differentiable function is μ-strongly convex if 

f (y) ≥ f (x) + ∇f (x)T(y − x) + μ

2 ∥y − x∥22, μ > 0. (2.19) Strong convexity rules out flat directions and ensures that, if a minimizer exists, it is unique. A differentiable function is L-smooth if 

∥∇ f (x) − ∇ f (y)∥2 ≤ L∥x − y∥2. (2.20) It then satisfies the upper quadratic bound 

f (y) ≤ f (x) + ∇f (x)T(y − x) + L

2 ∥y − x∥22. (2.21) When both properties hold, κ = L/μ measures how uneven the curvature is. Large κ produces elongated level sets and slower first-order optimization. 

# 2.2 Standard Convex Problems 

2.2.1 General Form and Global Optimality 

In an unconstrained problem, every x in the variable’s domain is an allowable choice. A constraint 

is an additional requirement that restricts which choices are allowed. A point satisfying every constraint is called feasible , and the set of all such points is the feasible set . A problem is infeasible 

if no point satisfies all its constraints. The two basic types are 

fi(x) ≤ 0 inequality constraint , (2.22) 

hj (x) = 0 equality constraint . (2.23) For example, x1 + x2 ≤ 10 may represent a resource limit, while x1 + x2 = 10 requires the entire resource to be used. At a feasible point, an inequality constraint is active if fi(x) = 0 and inactive Section 2.2 33 

if fi(x) < 0. Equality constraints are always active because they must hold exactly. The objective and the constraints play different roles. The objective ranks the feasible points according to desirability, while the constraints determine which points may be considered at all. Thus a constrained optimization problem asks for the feasible point having the smallest objective value. With this terminology, a convex optimization problem has the standard form minimize  

> x

f0(x)subject to fi(x) ≤ 0, i = 1 , . . . , m, Ax = b, 

(2.24) Here f0 is the objective, the functions f1, . . . , f m define the inequality constraints, and Ax = b

collects affine equality constraints. The feasible set is 

F = {x : fi(x) ≤ 0, i = 1 , . . . , m, Ax = b}. (2.25) The problem is convex when f0, f 1, . . . , f m are convex and the equality constraints are affine. Each inequality defines a convex sublevel set, and Ax = b defines an affine set; their intersection F is therefore convex. General nonlinear equalities are excluded because their solution sets need not be convex. 

Theorem 2.4 (The central advantage of convex optimization) . For a convex optimization problem, every local minimizer is a global minimizer. If the objective is strictly convex on the feasible set, the problem has at most one minimizer. 

This result is the principal reason convexity is so valuable: finding a point that is best among all sufficiently nearby feasible points is enough to certify that it is best among all feasible points. Convex problems therefore have no inferior local minima that can trap a local optimization method. To see the first claim, suppose that a local minimizer x⋆ were not global. Then some feasible y

would satisfy f0(y) < f 0(x⋆). Because the feasible set is convex, the points 

xθ = (1 − θ)x⋆ + θy, 0 < θ < 1, (2.26) are feasible and can be made arbitrarily close to x⋆. Convexity of the objective gives 

f0(xθ) ≤ (1 − θ)f0(x⋆) + θf 0(y) < f 0(x⋆), (2.27) contradicting local optimality. For uniqueness, if two distinct minimizers existed, strict convexity would make the objective at every point strictly between them smaller than the common minimum value, which is impossible. 34 Chapter 2. Convex Optimization and Standard Problems 

2.2.2 Linear and Quadratic Programs 

A linear program (LP) has a linear objective and affine constraints: minimize  

> x

cTx

subject to Gx ≤ h, Ax = b. 

(2.28) A convex quadratic program (QP) has minimize 

> x

12 xTQx + cTx

subject to Gx ≤ h, Ax = b, Q ⪰ 0. (2.29) LPs model linear costs and resource limits; QPs additionally model energy, tracking error, and local quadratic approximations. QPs are especially common in optimal control and model predictive control. Euclidean norm constraints such as 

∥Ax + b∥2 ≤ cTx + d (2.30) define second-order cone constraints. Problems built from linear objectives, affine equalities, and such constraints are second-order cone programs (SOCPs). For an introductory treatment, it is enough to recognize that they remain convex and can represent many norm-bounded design problems. 

How these problems are solved in practice. LPs, QPs, and SOCPs are normally passed to specialized numerical solvers rather than solved by hand. An LP may be solved by a simplex method, which moves among vertices of the feasible polyhedron, or by an interior-point method, which approaches the solution through the interior. Convex QPs are commonly solved by active-set methods, interior-point methods, or first-order splitting methods; these algorithms repeatedly solve linear systems involving the quadratic objective and the active constraints. SOCPs are usually handled by conic interior-point or conic first-order methods that preserve the cone structure. In engineering software, the user typically specifies the objective and constraints through a modeling interface. The software converts the model to a standard matrix or cone form, selects or calls a suitable solver, and returns a candidate solution together with feasibility and optimality diagnostics. Sparsity, scaling, warm starts, and the requested accuracy strongly affect runtime. The main algorithmic ideas are summarized in Chapter 4. 

# 2.3 Practical Convex Modeling Section 2.3 35 

2.3.1 Epigraph and Minimax Reformulations 

An epigraph reformulation introduces a scalar t that bounds an objective from above: min  

> x

f (x) ⇐⇒ 

minimize  

> x,t

t

subject to f (x) ≤ t. 

(2.31) For any fixed x, the smallest feasible value of t is f (x). Consequently, the two formulations have the same optimal value and the same optimal choices of x, and the bound is active at an attained optimum: t⋆ = f (x⋆). The reformulation does not remove the nonlinearity; it moves it from the objective into a constraint. Its value is structural. A modeling system may recognize the new constraint as a standard linear, quadratic, or cone constraint and pass the problem to an appropriate solver. The reformulated problem is not automatically easier, and introducing unnecessary variables can even add overhead. The method is most useful when it exposes a standard problem class or removes an explicit nonsmooth maximum from the objective. 

Example 2.5 (An unsquared norm modeled exactly) . The isolated problem min x ∥Ax − b∥2 has the same minimizers as squared least squares and is naturally treated as a QP. A cone reformulation is more useful when squaring the norm would change the model. For example, minimize  

> x

cTx + γ∥Ax − b∥2

subject to Gx ≤ h, γ > 0, (2.32) balances a linear cost against residual magnitude. It is equivalent to minimize  

> x,t

cTx + γt 

subject to ∥Ax − b∥2 ≤ t, Gx ≤ h. 

(2.33) This is an SOCP. Replacing the norm by its square would penalize residuals differently and would produce a different problem. The SOCP is useful because it preserves the intended norm model exactly, not because SOCPs are inherently easier than QPs. The same construction handles a pointwise maximum. If f (x) = max i fi(x), then min  

> x

max  

> i

fi(x) ⇐⇒ 

minimize  

> x,t

t

subject to fi(x) ≤ t, i = 1 , . . . , m. 

(2.34) Here t is a common upper bound, and at an attained optimum t⋆ = max i fi(x⋆). This minimax form is appropriate when the worst error or most adverse operating condition matters more than average performance. If every fi is convex, all the new inequalities are convex. 36 Chapter 2. Convex Optimization and Standard Problems

2.3.2 Composition Rules and Common Checks 

The following rules cover most modeling decisions in this book: • nonnegative sums and pointwise maxima preserve convexity; • composing a convex function with an affine map preserves convexity; • intersections of convex constraints preserve convexity; • constraints f (x) ≤ 0 are convex when f is convex; • nonlinear equality constraints are generally not convex. Absolute values and norms can often be converted into standard constraints. For example, 

|aTx − b| ≤ t is equivalent to 

aTx − b ≤ t, −aTx + b ≤ t. (2.37) The goal is not to memorize a large catalog, but to recognize a small set of reusable convex building blocks. 

Exercise 2.7. Show directly that intersections and affine images of convex sets are convex. 

Short solution. If x, y ∈ ⋂ 

> j

Cj , then x, y ∈ Cj for every j, so θx + (1 − θ)y ∈ Cj for every j

and hence belongs to the intersection. If u = T (x) and v = T (y) for an affine map T , then 

θu + (1 − θ)v = T (θx + (1 − θ)y), which belongs to T (C) when C is convex. 

Exercise 2.8. Use equation (2.15) to prove that every local minimizer of a convex function is global. 

Short solution. If a local minimizer x⋆ were not global, some y would satisfy f (y) < f (x⋆). Points 

xθ = (1 − θ)x⋆ + θy are arbitrarily close to x⋆ for small θ > 0, and convexity gives f (xθ) < f (x⋆), a contradiction.

Exercise 2.12. Let f (x) = 12 xTQx + cTx + r with Q = QT. State conditions on Q under which f

is convex, strictly convex, and strongly convex. 

Short solution. Because ∇2f = Q, the function is convex exactly when Q ⪰ 0. It is strictly convex when Q ≻ 0. If λmin (Q) > 0, it is μ-strongly convex for every 0 < μ ≤ λmin (Q). 

Exercise 2.13. For f (x1, x 2) = 12 (x21 +10 x22), find valid strong-convexity and smoothness constants and compute the condition number κ = L/μ .

Short solution. The Hessian is diag(1 , 10). Its smallest and largest eigenvalues are 1 and 10, so one may take μ = 1 and L = 10. Hence κ = 10. 

Exercise 2.14. Compare the sets 

C1 = {x ∈ R : x2 ≤ 1}, C2 = {x ∈ R : x2 = 1 }. (2.41) Which is convex, and what does this illustrate about nonlinear equalities? 

Short solution. C1 = [ −1, 1] is convex. By contrast, C2 = {− 1, 1} is not convex because its midpoint 0 is absent. A convex function may define a convex inequality sublevel set, while its nonlinear equality set need not be convex. 38 Chapter 2. Convex Optimization and Standard Problems

Chapter summary. Convexity makes local models globally informative. Convex sets remain intact under line segments, convex functions lie below their chords, and first-order conditions can certify global solutions. LPs, QPs, and norm-constrained problems provide the main engineering problem classes needed in the chapters that follow. 3

# Constrained Optimization and KKT Conditions 

Chapter map. Feasibility, Lagrange multipliers, duality, KKT conditions, and their engineer-ing interpretation. 

Consider minimize  

> x∈Rn

f (x)subject to gi(x) ≤ 0, i = 1 , . . . , m, hj (x) = 0 , j = 1 , . . . , p. 

(3.1) Constraints may represent actuator limits, safety requirements, conservation laws, or finite resources. At a constrained optimum, the objective gradient usually does not vanish; it is balanced by the boundaries that prevent further improvement. 

# 3.1 Feasibility and Local Geometry 

3.1.1 Feasible and Active Constraints 

The feasible set is 

F = {x : gi(x) ≤ 0, h j (x) = 0 }. (3.2) An inequality is active at a feasible point x when gi(x) = 0 and inactive when gi(x) < 0. Only active inequalities restrict sufficiently small motions. For example, minimize  

> x

(x − 2) 2 subject to x ≤ 1 (3.3) has solution x⋆ = 1. The derivative is not zero there: the objective would decrease by moving right, but the active constraint forbids that motion. 39 40 Chapter 3. Constrained Optimization and KKT Conditions 

3.1.2 Linearized Feasible Directions 

At a feasible point, a direction d is compatible with the constraints to first order when 

∇gi(x)Td ≤ 0, i ∈ A (x), (3.4) 

∇hj (x)Td = 0 , j = 1 , . . . , p, (3.5) where A(x) = {i : gi(x) = 0 }. Equality gradients remove motion normal to the equality surface; active inequality gradients allow motion only toward the feasible side. At a local minimizer x⋆, no such feasible direction can decrease the objective: 

∇f (x⋆)Td ≥ 0. (3.6) The directional condition in equation (3.6) is geometrically useful, but we would also like an algebraic condition that can be checked by solving equations. To derive one, we need a mild regularity assumption on the constraints. The idea is simply that the active constraint boundaries should meet in a nondegenerate way: their normal vectors should provide genuinely distinct information rather than being redundant. The next section uses this assumption to introduce Lagrange multipliers and express the required balance of gradients. A common assumption is the linear independence constraint qualification (LICQ). It requires the vectors 

{∇ gi(x) : i ∈ A (x)} ∪ {∇ hj (x) : j = 1 , . . . , p } (3.7) to be linearly independent. In practical terms, no active constraint normal can be reconstructed as a linear combination of the others. Under LICQ, the local feasible geometry is regular enough for the KKT multiplier description to apply cleanly. LICQ is a sufficient technical condition, not a claim that problems violating it have no solutions. An optimum may still exist when LICQ fails; the usual KKT theorem may simply require a different constraint qualification, and the multipliers may be nonunique or harder to interpret. 

# 3.2 The Lagrangian and Duality 

3.2.1 Equality Constraints First 

The main idea behind Lagrange multipliers is easiest to see when there are only equality constraints. Consider minimize  

> x

f (x)subject to hj (x) = 0 , j = 1 , . . . , p. (3.8) Near a feasible point, the first-order feasible directions satisfy 

∇hj (x)Td = 0 , j = 1 , . . . , p. (3.9) Section 3.2 41 

Thus feasible directions are tangent to the constraint surfaces, while the gradients ∇hj (x) are normal to those surfaces. At a regular local minimizer x⋆, the objective cannot decrease along any of these tangent directions. Consequently, ∇f (x⋆) must lie in the span of the constraint normals. There therefore exist scalars 

ν⋆ 

> 1

, . . . , ν ⋆p such that 

∇f (x⋆) + 

> p∑
> j=1

ν⋆j ∇hj (x⋆) = 0 . (3.10) The scalars ν⋆j are called Lagrange multipliers . They are unrestricted in sign because an equality constraint prevents motion through its surface in either normal direction. It is convenient to collect the objective and constraints into the equality-constrained Lagrangian 

L(x, ν ) = f (x) + 

> p∑
> j=1

νj hj (x). (3.11) Then equation (3.10) is simply 

∇xL(x⋆, ν ⋆) = 0 . (3.12) It must be combined with the original feasibility equations hj (x⋆) = 0. The unknowns are now both the optimizer x⋆ and the multipliers ν⋆.

Example 3.1 (Projection onto a hyperplane) . Let a, c ∈ Rn with c̸ = 0. The problem minimize 

> x

12 ∥x − a∥22

subject to cTx = b

(3.13) asks for the point on the hyperplane cTx = b closest to a. Its Lagrangian is 

L(x, ν ) = 12 ∥x − a∥22 + ν(cTx − b). (3.14) Stationarity and feasibility give 

x − a + νc = 0 , cTx = b. (3.15) Hence 

ν⋆ = cTa − b

∥c∥22

, x⋆ = a − cTa − b

∥c∥22

c. (3.16) The correction from a to x⋆ is parallel to the normal vector c, exactly as the gradient-balance picture predicts. The multiplier also has a useful quantitative interpretation. From x⋆ − a = −ν⋆c, the value of ν⋆

gives the signed strength of the constraint normal needed to balance the objective gradient. Its sign tells which direction along c the constraint pushes the solution, while |ν⋆|∥ c∥2 is the magnitude of that balancing vector. It also measures sensitivity to the constraint level. Let p(b) be the optimal value when the 42 Chapter 3. Constrained Optimization and KKT Conditions 

hyperplane is cTx = b. For this example, 

p(b) = (cTa − b)2

2∥c∥22

, dp db = −ν⋆. (3.17) Thus, for a small change ∆ b,

p(b + ∆ b) − p(b) ≈ − ν⋆∆b. (3.18) A large multiplier in magnitude means that moving the constraint surface even slightly has a large first-order effect on the best achievable objective value. This sensitivity interpretation extends to general equality constraints and will reappear later as the shadow-price interpretation of multipliers. 

3.2.2 Adding Inequality Constraints 

Now return to the general problem in equation (3.1). An inequality gi(x) ≤ 0 describes an allowed side and a forbidden side of a boundary. If gi(x) < 0, the point lies strictly inside the allowed region. A sufficiently small move in any direction will not cross that boundary, so the constraint has no immediate effect. Its multiplier should therefore be zero. If gi(x) = 0, the point lies on the boundary. The constraint now prevents motion toward the forbidden side, although motion back into the allowed region is still possible. For example, the constraint x ≤ 1 does nothing locally when x = 0, but at x = 1 it prevents any motion to the right. Because an inequality blocks motion in only one direction, the sign of its multiplier matters. With our convention gi(x) ≤ 0, the gradient ∇gi(x) points toward increasing values of gi and hence toward the forbidden side of an active boundary. At an optimum with one active inequality, gradient balance has the form 

−∇ f (x⋆) = λ⋆i ∇gi(x⋆). (3.19) The left-hand side is the unconstrained downhill direction. If the constraint is what prevents that downhill motion, this direction must point toward the forbidden side, in the same direction as ∇gi(x⋆). Its coefficient λ⋆i must therefore be nonnegative. A negative coefficient would point toward the allowed side, indicating an available feasible descent direction rather than a constrained minimum. For example, write x ≤ 1 as g(x) = x − 1 ≤ 0. Then g′(x) = 1 points to the right, toward the forbidden region x > 1. For f (x) = ( x − 2) 2 at x⋆ = 1, 

−f ′(1) = 2 = λ⋆g′(1) , (3.20) so λ⋆ = 2 ≥ 0. The positive multiplier records that the desired downhill motion is precisely the rightward motion blocked by the constraint. The sign rule depends on notation. If the same constraint were written using the convention 

gi(x) ≥ 0, the associated multiplier would instead be nonpositive when the Lagrangian is written with the same plus sign. Throughout this book we use gi(x) ≤ 0 and therefore λi ≥ 0. Equality multipliers νj remain unrestricted because equality constraints block both normal directions. Section 3.2 43 

Definition 3.2 (Lagrangian) . The Lagrangian of the general constrained problem is 

L(x, λ, ν ) = f (x) + 

> m∑
> i=1

λigi(x) + 

> p∑
> j=1

νj hj (x). (3.21) The Lagrangian places the objective and all constraint-normal terms in one expression. At a regular optimum, suitable multipliers satisfy 

∇xL(x⋆, λ ⋆, ν ⋆) = ∇f (x⋆) + ∑

> i

λ⋆i ∇gi(x⋆) + ∑

> j

ν⋆j ∇hj (x⋆) = 0 . (3.22) This is the stationarity condition . It says that the objective gradient is canceled by constraint-normal terms, so no feasible first-order motion can improve the objective. 

Important multiplier rules. The distinction between equality and inequality multipliers is fundamental: 

hj (x⋆) = 0 =⇒ ν⋆j ∈ R, equality multiplier: either sign ,gi(x⋆) < 0 =⇒ λ⋆i = 0 , inactive inequality: no influence ,gi(x⋆) = 0 =⇒ λ⋆i ≥ 0, active inequality: possibly positive .

(3.23) An active inequality may still have a zero multiplier; activity means that the point is on the boundary, not necessarily that the boundary affects the objective to first order. Stationarity, these multiplier rules, and the original feasibility requirements lead directly to the KKT conditions in the next section. 

3.2.3 Dual Bounds 

So far, the Lagrangian has been used to describe first-order balance at a candidate optimum. It has a second role that is especially valuable: it can produce a guaranteed lower bound on the best feasible objective value. Such a bound tells us how well any feasible point could possibly perform and can therefore help certify optimality. To see the idea, take any feasible point x. Its equality constraints satisfy hj (x) = 0, so the terms 

νj hj (x) vanish. Its inequality constraints satisfy gi(x) ≤ 0. If λi ≥ 0, then 

λigi(x) ≤ 0. (3.24) Adding these nonpositive terms to the objective can only lower its value: 

L(x, λ, ν ) ≤ f (x) for every feasible x and every λ ≥ 0. (3.25) This is another reason the sign restriction λ ≥ 0 is important. Now fix some multipliers ( λ, ν ) and minimize the Lagrangian over all x, without enforcing the 44 Chapter 3. Constrained Optimization and KKT Conditions 

original constraints. This defines the dual function 

q(λ, ν ) = inf  

> x

L(x, λ, ν ) (3.26) Because an infimum is no larger than the value at any particular x, for every feasible x we have 

q(λ, ν ) ≤ L (x, λ, ν ) ≤ f (x). (3.27) Thus q(λ, ν ) is a lower bound on the objective value of every feasible point, and therefore on the primal optimal value p⋆. Some choices of multipliers give weak bounds, or even −∞ , so we seek the largest valid bound. This gives the dual problem 

maximize  

> λ≥0,ν

q(λ, ν ). (3.28) If d⋆ denotes its optimal value, then 

d⋆ ≤ p⋆. (3.29) This inequality is called weak duality . The difference p⋆ − d⋆ ≥ 0 is the duality gap . If the gap is zero, then d⋆ = p⋆ and strong duality holds. In that case, a feasible primal point and a set of dual multipliers attaining the same value certify one another as optimal. Strong duality does not hold for every optimization problem. For a convex problem, however, it is guaranteed under a common strict-feasibility assumption called Slater’s condition : there exists a point satisfying the equality constraints exactly and the inequality constraints strictly, gi(x) < 0. Under this condition, the multiplier description loses no information about the primal optimum. The main practical consequence is that the KKT conditions in the next section become a complete certificate of global optimality. 

# 3.3 Karush–Kuhn–Tucker Conditions 

3.3.1 The Four Conditions 

The Karush–Kuhn–Tucker (KKT) conditions are 

∇xL(x⋆, λ ⋆, ν ⋆) = 0 , stationarity , (3.30) 

gi(x⋆) ≤ 0, hj (x⋆) = 0 , primal feasibility , (3.31) 

λ⋆i ≥ 0, dual feasibility , (3.32) 

λ⋆i gi(x⋆) = 0 , complementary slackness . (3.33) Complementary slackness connects activity and influence. If a constraint is inactive, its multiplier must be zero. If its multiplier is positive, the constraint must be active. An active constraint may still have zero multiplier when it does not affect the optimum to first order. 

Theorem 3.3 (KKT necessity and global sufficiency) . The KKT conditions have two distinct roles. Section 3.3 45 

1. For a smooth problem satisfying LICQ, every local minimizer has multipliers that satisfy the KKT conditions. 2. For a convex problem—a convex objective, convex inequality functions, and affine equality constraints—every point satisfying the KKT conditions is a global minimizer. If the objective is strictly convex on the feasible set, that minimizer is unique. 

The second statement is the central payoff. KKT is not merely a test for a possible local solution in a convex problem; it is a certificate that no feasible point anywhere has a smaller objective value. To see why, suppose ( x⋆, λ ⋆, ν ⋆) satisfies KKT. Since λ⋆ ≥ 0, the function L( · , λ ⋆, ν ⋆) is convex in 

x. Stationarity therefore makes x⋆ a global minimizer of this Lagrangian: 

L(x⋆, λ ⋆, ν ⋆) ≤ L (y, λ ⋆, ν ⋆) for every y. (3.34) Primal feasibility and complementary slackness give 

L(x⋆, λ ⋆, ν ⋆) = f (x⋆). (3.35) For any other feasible point y, the dual-bound argument gives 

L(y, λ ⋆, ν ⋆) ≤ f (y). (3.36) Combining the three relations yields 

f (x⋆) ≤ f (y) for every feasible y, (3.37) which is precisely global optimality. This conclusion does not hold in general for nonconvex problems; there, a KKT point may instead be a local maximum or a saddle point.

For each i, either ( Ax − b)i < 0 and λi = 0, or the constraint is active, ( Ax − b)i = 0, and its multiplier may be positive. The basic idea is therefore simple: choose a candidate active set A, treat its inequalities as equalities, set the other multipliers to zero, and solve the resulting equality-constrained problem. For a hand calculation with only a few inequalities, one can test the possible subsets, often beginning with the unconstrained minimizer to see which constraints it violates. An active-set algorithm performs this search systematically: it maintains a working set, adds a constraint when the current step would violate it, and removes an active constraint when its computed multiplier has the wrong sign. The simplest solution method. For a small QP, the most direct method is to test the possible active set. If the inequality is inactive, complementary slackness gives λ = 0. Stationarity would then give the unconstrained minimizer ( x1, x 2) = (2 , 1), but this point violates x1 + x2 ≤ 2. The inactive case is therefore impossible. Active-set QP algorithms automate the same logic by adding or removing constraints as needed. Chapter 4 introduces scalable alternatives, including interior-point and operator-splitting methods, that do not explicitly enumerate active sets. 

# 3.4 Sensitivity and Engineering Interpretation 

3.4.1 Why Sensitivity Matters 

An optimizer tells us what is best for the current model. In engineering, however, the model rarely remains fixed. A power limit may be increased, a safety margin may be tightened, a reference value may change, or an uncertain parameter may be estimated slightly differently. After finding an optimum, we therefore also want to know: • Which constraints are genuine bottlenecks? • How much would performance improve if one limit were relaxed? Section 3.4 49 

• Which changes would have almost no effect? • Is the reported solution stable under small modeling errors? One could answer these questions by changing each parameter and solving the optimization problem again. Sensitivity analysis instead uses information already produced at the original solution to predict the effect of small changes. Lagrange multipliers provide exactly this information. 

3.4.2 Multipliers as Shadow Prices 

To measure the effect of changing a constraint, perturb its right-hand side: minimize  

> x

f (x)subject to gi(x) ≤ ui, i = 1 , . . . , m, hj (x) = vj , j = 1 , . . . , p. 

(3.63) Let p(u, v ) denote the optimal objective value of this perturbed problem. The original problem corresponds to u = 0 and v = 0. Positive ui relaxes the ith inequality, while vj moves the jth equality surface. The central sensitivity result is 

∂p ∂u i

(0 , 0) = −λ⋆i , ∂p ∂v j

(0 , 0) = −ν⋆j . (3.64) Thus a multiplier is not merely an auxiliary variable used in the KKT equations. It is the rate at which the best achievable objective value changes when the corresponding constraint level moves. We first derive this statement for one equality, where the calculation is easiest to see. 

Derivation for one equality constraint. Consider 

p(v) = min  

> x

f (x) subject to h(x) = v, (3.65) and write x⋆(v) for its optimizer. Since p(v) = f (x⋆(v)), the chain rule gives 

dp dv = ∇f (x⋆)T dx ⋆

dv . (3.66) This says that the optimal value changes because the optimizer moves. Two equations describe that motion. First, stationarity gives 

∇f (x⋆) = −ν⋆∇h(x⋆). (3.67) Second, the moving optimizer must remain feasible: h(x⋆(v)) = v. Differentiating this identity with respect to v gives 

∇h(x⋆)T dx ⋆

dv = 1 . (3.68) 50 Chapter 3. Constrained Optimization and KKT Conditions 

Substituting equations (3.67) and (3.68) into equation (3.66) yields 

dp dv = −ν⋆∇h(x⋆)T dx ⋆

dv = −ν⋆. (3.69) Thus the multiplier summarizes the effect of the optimizer’s motion; we do not need to calculate 

dx ⋆/dv explicitly. 

Extension to an active inequality. Suppose g(x) ≤ u remains active as u changes slightly. Then g(x⋆(u)) = u, and differentiation gives 

∇g(x⋆)T dx ⋆

du = 1 . (3.70) Stationarity gives ∇f (x⋆) = −λ⋆∇g(x⋆), and therefore 

dp du = ∇f (x⋆)T dx ⋆

du = −λ⋆. (3.71) If the inequality is inactive, then λ⋆ = 0 and a sufficiently small relaxation does not move that boundary into contact with the solution, so the optimal value has zero first-order change. 

Several constraints. With several constraints, the same argument is applied to one right-hand side at a time while the others are held fixed. The perturbed Lagrangian 

L(x, λ, ν ; u, v ) = f (x) + ∑

> i

λi

> (

gi(x) − ui 

> )

+ ∑

> j

νj

> (

hj (x) − vj 

> )

(3.72) also makes the result visible: ui and vj appear with coefficients −λi and −νj . Under suitable regularity and differentiability assumptions, the motion of the optimizer is already accounted for by stationarity, leading to equation (3.64). This general principle is often called the envelope theorem .

First-order prediction. The sensitivity formula gives the local approximation 

p(u, v ) ≈ p(0 , 0) − ∑

> i

λ⋆i ui − ∑

> j

ν⋆j vj . (3.73) For a change affecting only the ith inequality, this becomes 

p(ui) − p(0) ≈ − λ⋆i ui. (3.74) Here p(u, v ) is evaluated after reoptimizing for the changed constraints. The formula predicts the change in the best achievable performance, not the performance of the old solution under the new model. Section 3.4 51 

Shadow-price interpretation. The inequality multiplier λ⋆i is called a shadow price . It measures the value of one additional unit of the constrained resource. Since λ⋆i ≥ 0, relaxing an inequality has predicted change −λ⋆i ui ≤ 0: the best achievable objective can improve or stay unchanged, but it cannot become worse. A large multiplier identifies an expensive bottleneck. A zero multiplier says that a small relaxation has no first-order benefit. The units make this interpretation concrete. If the objective is measured in dollars and ui in kilowatts, then λ⋆i has units of dollars per kilowatt. It estimates the reduction in optimal cost from one additional kilowatt, provided the change is small enough for the first-order approximation to remain accurate. Equality multipliers have the same sensitivity meaning, but may have either sign because moving an equality surface in opposite directions can either increase or decrease the optimal value. 

Example 3.4 (The value of relaxing a bound) . Return to minimize  

> x

(x − 2) 2 subject to x ≤ 1. (3.75) The solution is x⋆ = 1 and the multiplier is λ⋆ = 2. Relax the bound to x ≤ 1 + u. For small 

u ≥ 0, the new solution is x⋆(u) = 1 + u and 

p(u) = (1 − u)2. (3.76) Therefore 

p′(0) = −2 = −λ⋆. (3.77) A small relaxation u is predicted to reduce the optimal cost by approximately 2 u. For instance, increasing the bound by 0 .01 gives the first-order estimate p(0 .01) − p(0) ≈ − 0.02. 

3.4.3 Limits of the Interpretation 

Sensitivity formulas are local, first-order approximations. If a parameter changes substantially, the active set may change: an inactive constraint may become active, or a formerly limiting constraint may cease to matter. The optimal-value function can then change slope or fail to be differentiable, and the original multiplier may no longer predict the change accurately. Regularity also matters. Redundant or degenerate constraints can produce nonunique multipliers, making an individual shadow price ambiguous. Poorly conditioned problems may have solutions and multipliers that change sharply under small perturbations. Finally, KKT equations are local first-order conditions for a nonconvex problem. A KKT point may be a local minimum, a local maximum, or a saddle point, so its multipliers need not describe the sensitivity of the global optimum. Second-order checks, comparison of candidate solutions, or a global method may still be required. In practice, one should inspect feasibility, stationarity, complementary slackness, and problem convexity rather than reporting an objective value alone.

Chapter summary. Constraints restrict the allowable directions of motion. Lagrange multipliers balance the objective gradient against active constraint normals, duality gives bounds, and KKT conditions combine stationarity, feasibility, and complementarity. In convex problems, these conditions provide a complete global certificate; their multipliers also quantify the value of relaxing constraints. 4

# Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

Chapter map. Practical iterative methods for smooth, constrained, nonsmooth, and data-driven objectives. 

The previous chapters described optimality conditions: they tell us what a solution must look like. Numerical optimization asks the complementary question: how can a computer actually find such a point? Closed-form solutions are available for a few important problems, such as unconstrained least squares and certain quadratic programs. Most realistic models, however, are too large, nonlinear, constrained, or data-dependent to be solved by a single formula. Instead, an algorithm starts from an initial guess x0 and constructs a sequence 

x0, x 1, x 2, . . . (4.1) that is intended to approach a solution. Each iteration uses local information to decide where to move and how far to move. Three questions organize much of this chapter: 1. Direction: which change in x appears promising? 2. Step size: how far should the algorithm move in that direction? 3. Structure: should the method use curvature, constraints, sparsity, or stochastic data information? An algorithm must also decide when to stop. A small objective change alone is not a certificate of success, so practical methods monitor quantities such as gradient norms, feasibility violations, and KKT residuals. 

# 4.1 First-Order Methods 

55 56 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

4.1.1 Gradient Descent 

Direction and step size. Gradient descent is built from the local linear model 

f (x + d) ≈ f (x) + ∇f (x)Td. (4.2) Among all unit-length directions, the one with the most negative predicted change solves min  

> ∥d∥2≤1

∇f (x)Td. (4.3) By Cauchy–Schwarz, the minimum is attained at d = −∇ f (x)/∥∇ f (x)∥2 whenever the gradient is nonzero. Thus the negative gradient is the direction of steepest local decrease under the Euclidean norm. The algorithm repeatedly moves in this direction: 

xk+1 = xk − αk∇f (xk), (4.4) where αk > 0 is the step size. Substitution into the linear model predicts the change 

−αk∥∇ f (xk)∥22, (4.5) which is negative away from a stationary point. This prediction is only local, however. The direction may be correct while an excessively long step still increases the true objective. A step that is too small is safe but makes little progress. A step that is too large may pass across a valley, increase the objective, or even cause the iterates to diverge. Smoothness provides a useful quantitative guide. 

Smoothness and guaranteed decrease. Definition 4.1 (L-Lipschitz gradient) . A differentiable function f has an L-Lipschitz gradient, or is L-smooth, if 

∥∇ f (x) − ∇ f (y)∥2 ≤ L∥x − y∥2 for all x, y. (4.6) This condition limits how rapidly the slope can change as the point moves. A small L describes gently varying gradients, whereas a large L permits sharp curvature and therefore calls for shorter steps. If f is twice differentiable, it is sufficient that
