Smooth strongly convex case. The O(1 /k ) rate allows the objective to become arbitrarily flat near its minimum. A faster rate follows when such flatness is ruled out. If f is μ-strongly convex, then it has at least quadratic curvature in every direction. More precisely, for every x and y,

f (y) ≥ f (x) + ∇f (x)T(y − x) + μ

Conditioning and geometry. The ratio 

κ = Lμ (4.43) is the condition number associated with these curvature bounds. If κ is close to one, the contraction factor 1 − 1/κ is small and gradient descent is fast. If κ is large, the factor is close to one and progress is slow. These rate bounds are called global because they hold from any starting point under the stated assumptions, not merely after the iterates are already close to the minimizer. Section 4.1 61 

For the quadratic objective 

f (x) = 12 xTQx − bTx, Q ≻ 0, (4.44) 

L and μ may be chosen as the largest and smallest eigenvalues of Q. Their ratio describes the shape of the level sets. A large ratio produces a long, narrow valley: the gradient points mostly across the narrow direction, and the iterates zig-zag rather than moving directly toward the minimizer. One remedy is to rescale the coordinates so that comparable changes in different directions have comparable effects on the objective. A preconditioner performs this scaling inside the algorithm. Instead of using the raw negative gradient, preconditioned gradient descent takes 

xk+1 = xk − αkPk∇f (xk), Pk ≻ 0, (4.45) where the positive-definite matrix Pk changes the relative step length in different directions. Directions believed to have high curvature receive shorter effective steps, while flatter directions receive longer ones. For the quadratic objective above, the ideal choice would be P = Q−1. Then all curvature is normalized and, with α = 1, the method reaches the minimizer in one step in exact arithmetic. Computing or applying Q−1 is usually as difficult as solving the original problem, so practical preconditioners use inexpensive approximations, such as a diagonal matrix formed from the diagonal of Q. A useful preconditioner does not need to remove all anisotropy; even a rough scaling that reduces the effective condition number can substantially accelerate convergence. 

4.1.2 Backtracking Line Search 

The safe fixed-step rule above requires knowledge of L, which is often unavailable or overly conservative. A line search chooses the step size by testing the objective directly. Suppose dk is a descent direction, so ∇f (xk)Tdk < 0. Backtracking line search begins with a trial step α, often α = 1, and checks 

f (xk + αd k) ≤ f (xk) + cα ∇f (xk)Tdk, c ∈ (0 , 1) . (4.46) If the condition fails, the method replaces α by ρα , where ρ ∈ (0 , 1), and tries again. The right-hand side is the current value plus a fraction c of the decrease predicted by the linear model. Since ∇f (xk)Tdk < 0, it is smaller than f (xk). The Armijo condition therefore asks the actual objective decrease to be meaningfully related to the predicted decrease, without demanding that the local model be exact. Backtracking is popular because it is simple and robust. It adapts to local curvature and requires only additional objective evaluations. Although it was introduced here immediately after gradient descent, the procedure applies to any direction dk satisfying ∇f (xk)Tdk < 0. Later sections will construct other such directions using curvature information; the same backtracking test can then be used to choose their step lengths. The parameters are commonly chosen with a small c, such as 10 −4, and a reduction factor such as ρ = 1 /2. 62 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

4.1.3 Momentum and Acceleration 

Gradient descent treats every iteration independently. Momentum introduces a state variable that remembers recent directions: 

vk+1 = βv k + ∇f (xk), (4.47) 

xk+1 = xk − αv k+1 , β ∈ [0 , 1) . (4.48) The new gradient is combined with an exponentially decaying history of earlier gradients. When gradients repeatedly point in the same direction, their effects accumulate and the method moves faster. When a component alternates sign, as in zig-zagging across a narrow valley, the positive and negative contributions partly cancel. The parameter β controls memory. Values close to zero resemble ordinary gradient descent; values close to one preserve information for many iterations. Nesterov acceleration uses a related idea but evaluates the gradient at an extrapolated point. For smooth convex problems it improves the worst-case objective rate from order 1 /k to order 1 /k 2.Acceleration is not free. Momentum may overshoot, objective values need not decrease at every iteration, and the step size and momentum parameter interact. In nonconvex learning problems it is best viewed as a powerful practical heuristic rather than an automatic guarantee of faster convergence. 

# 4.2 Curvature-Based Methods 

4.2.1 Newton’s Method 

Gradient descent uses only the slope of the objective. Newton’s method also uses curvature. Near 

xk, the second-order Taylor model is 

mk(d) = f (xk) + ∇f (xk)Td + 12 dTHkd, Hk = ∇2f (xk). (4.49) Rather than minimizing the original objective immediately, Newton’s method minimizes this local quadratic approximation. Differentiating mk with respect to d gives 

∇mk(d) = ∇f (xk) + Hkd. (4.50) Setting this gradient to zero produces the Newton equation 

Hkdk = −∇ f (xk), (4.51) followed by xk+1 = xk + dk.When Hk ≻ 0, the quadratic model is strictly convex and dk is its unique minimizer. The Hessian rescales the gradient according to local curvature: directions with high curvature receive smaller motion, while flatter directions receive larger motion. For a strictly convex quadratic objective, the Section 4.2 63 

model is exact and Newton’s method reaches the minimizer in one iteration in exact arithmetic. Near a smooth minimizer with a nonsingular positive-definite Hessian, Newton’s method can converge quadratically: once sufficiently close, the number of correct digits may roughly double at each iteration. This rapid local behavior is its main attraction. Far from a minimizer, several problems can occur. The Hessian may be indefinite, so the Newton direction need not be a descent direction. Even with a positive-definite Hessian, the quadratic model may be accurate only near xk, making the full step too aggressive. A damped Newton method uses xk+1 = xk + αkdk with a line search. Trust-region methods provide another safeguard by minimizing the model only within a neighborhood where it is believed to be reliable. Computationally, one should solve the linear system Hkdk = −∇ f (xk) using an appropriate factorization or iterative solver. Explicitly forming H−1 

> k

is slower, less accurate, and usually destroys sparsity. 

4.2.2 Quasi-Newton Methods 

Forming and factoring the exact Hessian may be too expensive, especially when n is large. Quasi-Newton methods build a curvature approximation from changes already observed during the iteration: 

sk = xk+1 − xk, yk = ∇f (xk+1 ) − ∇ f (xk). (4.52) For a quadratic function with constant Hessian H, these vectors satisfy yk = Hs k. Quasi-Newton methods imitate this relation by constructing a matrix Bk+1 satisfying the secant equation 

Bk+1 sk = yk. (4.53) They then compute a Newton-like direction from 

Bkdk = −∇ f (xk). (4.54) One vector equation cannot determine an entire matrix, so an additional rule is needed to choose the update. BFGS selects a symmetric update that changes the previous approximation as little as possible while satisfying the secant equation. With a suitable line search and sT 

> k

yk > 0, it preserves positive definiteness, so the resulting direction remains a descent direction. Dense BFGS stores an n × n matrix and is attractive for small and medium-sized problems. Limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) avoids storing this matrix. It keeps only a short history of pairs (sk, y k) and applies the implied inverse-Hessian approximation through vector operations. This makes L-BFGS a standard choice for large smooth problems. Quasi-Newton methods occupy a useful middle ground: they are more expensive per iteration than gradient descent but avoid exact second derivatives and often need far fewer iterations. 64 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

# 4.3 Constraints, Nonsmoothness, and Structured Solvers 

4.3.1 Projected and Proximal Gradient 

Sometimes the feasible set is simple enough that the nearest feasible point can be computed cheaply. Examples include boxes, Euclidean balls, and affine subspaces. In this case, projected gradient descent separates improvement of the objective from restoration of feasibility: 

xk+1 = Π C

> (

xk − αk∇f (xk)), (4.55) where the projection operator is defined by ΠC (z) = arg min 

> y∈C

12 ∥y − z∥22. (4.56) Thus Π C (z) is the point in C nearest to z. In finite-dimensional Euclidean space, nonemptiness and closedness of C guarantee that at least one nearest point exists. Convexity makes that nearest point unique, so Π C is a single-valued mapping. Projected gradient descent first takes an ordinary gradient step, which may leave C, and then applies this well-defined projection to restore feasibility. In particular, ΠC (z) = z whenever z ∈ C, (4.57) because a feasible point is already its own nearest feasible point. The geometry is especially clear for a Euclidean ball. Consider 

C = {x ∈ Rn : ∥x − c∥2 ≤ r}, (4.58) with center c and radius r > 0. The ball includes both its boundary and its interior. This is important: the boundary alone, {x : ∥x − c∥2 = r}, is a sphere and is not a convex set. If z already lies in the ball, it is feasible and projection leaves it unchanged. If z lies outside, the nearest feasible point lies on the radial line from c through z. Projection therefore shortens the distance from the center to exactly r:ΠC (z) = 

> 

z, ∥z − c∥2 ≤ r, c + r z − c

∥z − c∥2

, ∥z − c∥2 > r. (4.59) As a concrete two-dimensional example, let C be the unit disk centered at the origin and take 

z = (3 , 4). Since ∥z∥2 = 5, the point lies outside the disk. Normalizing it to unit length gives ΠC (3 , 4) = (3 , 4) 5 = 

> (

35 , 45

> )

. (4.60) Thus the projection preserves the direction from the center while reducing the radius from 5 Section 4.3 65 

to 1. This simple radial correction is what projected gradient descent performs whenever an unconstrained step leaves a ball-shaped feasible region. Projection is designed for hard constraints. A related method applies when the objective has the form 

F (x) = f (x) + g(x), (4.61) where f is smooth but g may have corners. For example, g(x) = λ∥x∥1 is not differentiable when a coordinate is zero, so ordinary gradient descent cannot be applied directly to the whole objective. The proximal operator 

prox αg (z) = arg min 

> x
> {

g(x) + 12α ∥x − z∥22

> }

(4.62) chooses a point that reduces g without moving too far from z. Proximal gradient first takes a gradient step for the smooth term and then applies this operator: 

xk+1 = prox αk g

> (

xk − αk∇f (xk)). (4.63) For g(x) = λ∥x∥1, the proximal operator is soft thresholding , applied to each coordinate: [prox αλ ∥·∥ 1 (z)] i = sign( zi) max {| zi| − αλ, 0}. (4.64) It shrinks large coordinates and sets sufficiently small ones exactly to zero. For instance, if 

z = (3 , 0.4, −2) and αλ = 1, the output is (2 , 0, −1). This example is the main reason proximal methods appear in sparse estimation; further theory is not needed for the developments that follow. 

4.3.2 Penalty and Barrier Methods 

Projected gradient is attractive when projection onto the feasible set is easy. For general nonlinear constraints, however, computing that projection may be as difficult as solving the original problem. Other methods therefore handle the constraints in different ways. Penalty and barrier methods replace the constrained problem by a sequence of problems with modified objectives, but they approach feasibility from opposite sides. Penalty methods allow infeasible iterates and increasingly punish constraint violations, whereas barrier methods keep iterates strictly inside the feasible region. 

Penalty methods. A penalty method permits temporary constraint violation but makes it costly. For example, equality constraints h(x) = 0 can be incorporated into the objective through the quadratic penalty 

f (x) + ρ

2 ∥h(x)∥22. (4.65) The parameter ρ > 0 determines how strongly violations are punished. A small value gives the algorithm freedom to improve the original objective, whereas a large value places greater emphasis on feasibility. Since no finite ρ necessarily enforces h(x) = 0 exactly, a penalty method commonly 66 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

solves several problems while gradually increasing ρ. Very large penalties can make the numerical problem poorly conditioned, so this increase must be handled with care. 

Barrier methods. For inequality constraints gi(x) ≤ 0, a barrier method takes the opposite viewpoint. It starts strictly inside the feasible region and prevents the iterates from crossing its boundary. A common modified objective is 

f (x) − μ ∑

> i

log( −gi(x)) , gi(x) < 0. (4.66) As an iterate approaches a boundary, the logarithmic term becomes arbitrarily large and pushes it back toward the interior. The parameter μ > 0 controls the strength of this artificial repulsion. Solving a sequence of problems with decreasing values of μ allows the solution to move progressively closer to the boundary when the true optimum lies there. This is the central idea behind many interior-point methods. The two methods therefore approach constraints differently. Penalty methods are conceptually simple and tolerate infeasible iterates, while barrier methods remain strictly feasible and approach inequality boundaries from the inside. Neither strategy is uniformly best, and practical solvers may combine these ideas with other techniques. 

4.3.3 Choosing a Solver for Structured Convex Problems 

Linear programs, quadratic programs, and second-order cone programs have more structure than a general nonlinear optimization problem. Practical solvers use that structure rather than treating every problem in the same way. For example, linear objectives and constraints lead to different computations from quadratic objectives, and cone constraints require different operations from simple bounds. There are therefore several solver families. Common names include simplex, active-set, interior-point, and first-order methods. The important point is that they make different compromises: some perform expensive iterations but need relatively few of them, while others use cheap iterations but may require many more. Some are designed for high accuracy, whereas others are attractive for very large problems when a moderately accurate answer is sufficient. The best choice also depends on how the problem is used. A sparse problem should be solved without converting its matrices to dense form. A sequence of similar problems may benefit from a warm start using the previous solution. Real-time applications may prefer predictable and inexpensive iterations, while an offline design problem may justify a more costly high-accuracy solve. In many software packages, the modeling system converts the user-written problem into a standard form and selects a suitable solver automatically. This is convenient, but the returned vector should not be trusted without checking the solver status, constraint violations, and an optimality residual or gap. Poor scaling can also make an otherwise easy problem numerically difficult. Structured convex solvers also play an important role inside algorithms for general nonlinear Section 4.3 67 

constraints. Sequential quadratic programming (SQP), for example, keeps the constraints explicit. At the current iterate, it builds a quadratic model of the objective and linear models of the nonlinear constraints. The resulting quadratic program proposes a step, so every SQP iteration requires a suitable QP solver. A line search or trust region may shorten the proposed step when the local models are not yet reliable. Near a well-behaved solution, SQP can converge rapidly. The central lesson is that no numerical method is universally best. Problem type, size, sparsity, desired accuracy, and available computation all influence the choice. For this book, it is enough to recognize these tradeoffs and to use a reliable solver designed for the problem class at hand. 

4.3.4 Operator Splitting for Convex Quadratic Programs 

Chapter 3 showed the simplest algebraic approach to an equality-constrained quadratic program: write its stationarity and feasibility conditions and solve the KKT system in equation (3.39). For a small or medium-sized problem, this direct approach is often the natural choice. It computes the primal variables and equality multipliers simultaneously, and an accurate sparse factorization can provide a high-precision solution in one linear solve. The same strategy becomes less direct when the problem contains many inequality or bound constraints. The active inequalities are not known in advance, so one must identify an active set, introduce slack and barrier variables, or repeatedly solve modified KKT systems. Moreover, for a very large sparse problem, factoring the full indefinite KKT matrix can require substantial memory and computation. These costs are particularly important when a sequence of closely related QPs must be solved, as in model predictive control or sequential quadratic programming. Operator-splitting methods make a different tradeoff. They separate the coupled quadratic objective from constraints that admit inexpensive projections, then enforce agreement through a multiplier iteration. Each iteration is usually cheaper and can reuse a matrix factorization, exploit sparsity, and start from the previous solution. The price is that many iterations may be required for high accuracy. Thus a direct KKT solve and operator splitting solve the same optimality conditions but organize the linear algebra differently: direct methods emphasize accuracy in relatively few expensive solves, whereas splitting methods emphasize inexpensive, repeatable, and warm-startable steps. The representative derivation below shows how optimality conditions become such repeated computations. The simplified pattern is used by solvers such as OSQP; production implementations add scaling, regularization, acceleration, and adaptive parameter selection. Consider the standard convex QP minimize 

> x

12 xTP x + qTx

subject to ℓ ≤ Ax ≤ u, 

(4.67) where P ⪰ 0. The two-sided bounds include equalities, one-sided inequalities, and simple variable bounds as special cases. 68 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

Separating the objective from the bounds. Introduce an auxiliary vector z and rewrite the constraints as 

Ax = z, ℓ ≤ z ≤ u. (4.68) The variable x now carries the quadratic objective, while z carries the simple bounds. The equality 

Ax = z requires the two descriptions to agree. Let C = [ ℓ, u ]. Before constructing an algorithm, write the KKT conditions. Introduce λ for Ax − z = 0, α ≥ 0for the lower bound ℓ − z ≤ 0, and β ≥ 0 for the upper bound z − u ≤ 0. The ordinary Lagrangian is 

L(x, z, λ, α, β ) = 12 xTP x + qTx + λT(Ax − z) + αT(ℓ − z) + βT(z − u). (4.69) Stationarity in z and complementary slackness give 

−λ − α + β = 0 , (4.70) 

αi(zi − ℓi) = 0 , βi(ui − zi) = 0 . (4.71) Thus λi = 0 in the interior of an interval, λi ≤ 0 at a lower bound, and λi ≥ 0 at an upper bound. Eliminating α and β summarizes all three cases as 

λT(w − z) ≤ 0 for every w ∈ C. (4.72) The compact KKT system is therefore 

P x + q + ATλ = 0 , (4.73) 

Ax − z = 0 , (4.74) 

λT(w − z) ≤ 0 for every w ∈ C. (4.75) 

From the KKT system to an augmented Lagrangian. The three KKT conditions are coupled: changing x changes Ax , which affects both z and the multiplier. Operator splitting handles this coupling iteratively. It starts from the augmented Lagrangian

λ.Thus “FONC” describes how these equations are derived, while “KKT conditions” describes the complete optimality system, including the box constraint on z. Thus the quadratic penalty changes the path taken away from feasibility but does not change the desired primal–dual solution. Minimizing Lρ for an arbitrary fixed λ would not provide this guarantee: its minimizer may still have Ax ̸ = z. The multiplier-ascent step is what drives this disagreement to zero.

After minimizing in x and z, update the multiplier by gradient ascent. The relevant gradient is particularly simple: 

∇λLρ(x, z, λ ) = Ax − z. (4.91) Because λ is maximized, the gradient is added rather than subtracted. In principle, a separate ascent step size η > 0 could be used:

(4.92) The alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) is named for these alternating minimiza-tions in x and z, followed by an update of the Lagrange multiplier. The standard ADMM update chooses η = ρ, matching the dual step size to the coefficient of the quadratic penalty. This choice gives

4.3.5 Primal–Dual Interior-Point Methods 

The preceding subsection solved a convex QP by separating its objective and simple constraints into inexpensive subproblems. Interior-point methods make a different tradeoff: they perform more work per iteration in order to approach a high-accuracy solution in relatively few iterations. Rather than projecting onto the constraints or guessing an active set as in Chapter 3, they retain every inequality in the KKT system and approach complementarity continuously while keeping the slack variables and inequality multipliers strictly positive. A primal–dual interior-point method implements this idea by applying Newton’s method to a perturbed KKT system. Besides providing a direct solver for convex problems, this construction reinforces the primal–dual Newton viewpoint used again in the SQP method of the next subsection; in practice, interior-point methods can also solve the QP subproblems generated by SQP. To see the basic construction, consider the convex QP minimize 

> x

12 xTQx + cTx

subject to Ax = b, Gx ≤ h, Q ⪰ 0. (4.104) where G has m rows, one for each inequality. Introduce slacks s ≥ 0 so that 

Gx + s = h. (4.105) Let y be the equality multiplier and z ≥ 0 the inequality multiplier. With S = diag( s), the KKT conditions are 

Qx + c + ATy + GTz = 0 , (4.106) 

Ax − b = 0 , (4.107) 

Gx + s − h = 0 , (4.108) 

Sz = 0 , (4.109) 

s ≥ 0, z ≥ 0. (4.110) The nonlinear part is complementarity: for each constraint, sizi = 0. At the solution, either si > 0and the inequality is inactive with zi = 0, or zi > 0 and the constraint is active with si = 0. 

Perturbed complementarity and the central path. Instead of imposing complementary slackness immediately, an interior-point method solves the perturbed equations 

Sz = μ1, s > 0, z > 0, (4.111) Section 4.3 73

Their main cost is factoring a large sparse Newton system at each iteration, but they often reach 74 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

high accuracy in relatively few iterations. Modern primal–dual methods may begin with nonzero primal residuals; interior refers primarily to maintaining s > 0 and z > 0 while driving the KKT residuals to zero. 

4.3.6 Sequential Quadratic Programming 

Chapter 3 showed how the KKT conditions reduce an equality-constrained QP to a linear saddle-point system and how active constraints extend the same idea to inequality-constrained QPs. The preceding subsections then described numerical methods for solving such QPs without manually enumerating every possible active set. Sequential quadratic programming (SQP) uses a QP as a local model of a nonlinear constrained problem: solve the QP for a primal–dual correction, move to a new point, rebuild the model there, and repeat. The model changes at every iteration because the objective curvature and constraint Jacobians are evaluated at the current iterate; this repeated construction is the source of the word sequential .Consider the nonlinear program minimize  

> x

f (x)subject to h(x) = 0 ,g(x) ≤ 0,

(4.121) where x ∈ Rn, h : Rn → Rme collects the equality constraints, and g : Rn → Rmi collects the inequality constraints. Its Lagrangian is 

L(x, λ, μ ) = f (x) + λTh(x) + μTg(x), μ ≥ 0. (4.122) Here λ ∈ Rme contains the equality multipliers, while μ ∈ Rmi contains the nonnegative inequality multipliers. Under a suitable constraint qualification, a local solution satisfies the KKT conditions 

∇xL(x, λ, μ ) = 0 , h(x) = 0 ,g(x) ≤ 0, μ ≥ 0, μj gj (x) = 0 for every j. (4.123) Locally, after the active inequalities have been identified, SQP follows by applying the Newton iteration in equations (3.42) and (3.43) to the resulting smooth KKT equations. 

From a Newton correction to a QP. First suppose temporarily that the active inequalities are known. Collect them together with the equality constraints in a vector a(x), so the local active constraints are a(x) = 0, and collect their multipliers in y. Let 

C(x) = ax(x), Ck = C(xk) (4.124) be the active-constraint Jacobian. Thus Ckp is the first-order change in the active constraints caused by a step p, while CT

The full SQP subproblem. In practice, the active set is not known beforehand. SQP therefore retains all inequalities and lets the QP identify which linearized bounds are active. For a proposed 76 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

step p, the first-order constraint models are 

h(xk + p) ≈ h(xk) + hx(xk)p, (4.132) 

g(xk + p) ≈ g(xk) + gx(xk)p. (4.133) The full SQP subproblem is consequently minimize  

> p

∇f (xk)Tp + 12 pTBkp

subject to h(xk) + hx(xk)p = 0 ,g(xk) + gx(xk)p ≤ 0.

(4.134) The constant term f (xk) is omitted because it does not affect the minimizer. The objective is a local quadratic model, while the constraints are local affine models. The QP therefore chooses a direction that balances objective reduction against restoration of feasibility. 

Curvature model. The exact Lagrangian Hessian in equation (4.127) has the expanded form 

Bk = ∇2f (xk) + ∑

> i

> j

(μk)j ∇2gj (xk). (4.135) It includes constraint curvature because the equations being solved are the KKT equations, not merely ∇f (x) = 0. An active nonlinear constraint may therefore contribute substantially to the local curvature model. Exact second derivatives may be expensive, and the exact Lagrangian Hessian can be indefinite away from a solution. Common alternatives include a BFGS-type quasi-Newton approximation, a Gauss–Newton matrix for least-squares objectives, and a regularized model Bk + ρkI. If the inner solver requires a convex QP, Bk is chosen or modified so that Bk ⪰ 0. 

Obtaining the QP multipliers. The vectors λk and μk are estimates, not fixed problem data. They may be initialized to zero and are subsequently updated from the dual solution of each QP. These estimates enter the Lagrangian Hessian in equation (4.135), so the primal step and dual multipliers are both essential outputs of the subproblem. The QP KKT conditions include 

Bkpk + ∇f (xk) + hx(xk)T̂ λk + gx(xk)T̂ μk = 0 , (4.136) ̂

> (

gj (xk) + ∇gj (xk)Tpk 

> )

= 0 . (4.137) Thus a primal–dual QP algorithm computes ̂ λk and ̂ μk together with pk. Active-set methods obtain them from the equality-constrained KKT system for the final working set, primal–dual interior-point methods carry them as explicit iterates, and bound-form operator-splitting solvers maintain a dual variable for the constraint ℓk ≤ Akp ≤ uk. Solver sign conventions differ: for an upper-bound inequality of the form used here, its dual must be mapped to the convention ̂ μk ≥ 0. Most full-featured QP solver interfaces expose these dual variables, but not every interface does. An approximate first-order method may also return duals whose accuracy differs from that of the Section 4.3 77 

primal step. Moreover, sign conventions, presolve, and internal scaling affect how the reported duals map back to the original constraints. An SQP implementation should therefore request the dual solution and check its documented ordering, signs, and scaling. If only pk is available, the multipliers can often be recovered cheaply. Let JA contain the gradients of the QP inequalities judged active at pk. Set the inactive multipliers to zero and solve 

> [

hx(xk)T JT

> A
> ] [̂

(̂ μk)A

> ]

= −(Bkpk + ∇f (xk)). (4.138) Under LICQ, the active constraint gradients are linearly independent, so the QP multipliers are unique and this system can be solved by QR or a least-squares factorization. Recovery is less reliable when LICQ fails, the active gradients are nearly dependent, or the active set depends sensitively on the feasibility tolerance. A solver that directly supplies a consistent primal–dual solution is therefore preferable for SQP. 

Iteration, globalization, and stopping. Using the QP multipliers ̂ λk and ̂ μk, a consistent primal–dual update with a common damping parameter 0 < α k ≤ 1 is 

xk+1 = xk + αkpk, λk+1 = λk + αk(̂λk − λk), μk+1 = μk + αk(̂ μk − μk). (4.139) For a full step, αk = 1, the multiplier estimates are replaced directly by the QP multipliers. At the new point, the algorithm reevaluates f , h, g, and their derivatives, constructs a new QP, and repeats. Near a regular solution, the full Newton step αk = 1 is typically appropriate. Farther away, however, the local QP may predict objective improvement while worsening nonlinear feasibility, or vice versa. A line search can compare trial points using a merit function such as 

Mη(x) = f (x) + η∥h(x)∥1 + η∥max {0, g (x)}∥ 1 (4.140) and reduce αk until this combined measure of objective and constraint violation decreases sufficiently. Trust-region SQP instead restricts ∥p∥ and changes the trust-region radius according to the agreement between predicted and actual improvement. If the linearized constraints are inconsistent, the QP may be infeasible even though the nonlinear problem is feasible. Practical implementations address this using slack variables, elastic penalties, or a separate feasibility- restoration step. The algorithm terminates only when the KKT residuals are small: h(xk) and the positive parts of g(xk) measure primal infeasibility, ∇xL(xk, λ k, μ k)measures stationarity, the negative parts of μk measure dual infeasibility, and μk ⊙ g(xk) measures complementarity. Under standard regularity and second-order assumptions, exact-Hessian SQP has Newton-like rapid local convergence. Quasi-Newton variants obtain similar behavior when their curvature approximations become accurate. 78 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms 

# 4.4 Stochastic Gradient Descent 

Many data-driven problems minimize an average loss 

F (x) = 1

> N∑
> i=1

fi(x), (4.141) where fi is the loss associated with one data point. Full gradient descent computes all N component gradients before taking a step. When N is large, it can be faster to use a randomly selected data point or mini-batch and take more frequent, less accurate steps. Let gk denote the resulting gradient estimate. Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) uses the update 

xk+1 = xk − αkgk, (4.142) where αk > 0 is the learning rate. A standard analysis assumes that the estimate is correct on average and has bounded noise: 

E[gk | xk] = ∇F (xk), (4.143) 

E

> [

∥gk − ∇ F (xk)∥22 | xk

> ]

≤ σ2. (4.144) The first condition says that repeated random samples at the same point would produce the full gradient on average. The second prevents the estimates from being arbitrarily noisy. Averaging a larger mini-batch usually reduces this variance, at the cost of more computation per update. 

4.4.1 Why SGD Makes Progress 

Suppose that F is bounded below by F⋆ and has an L-Lipschitz gradient. Applying the descent lemma to the stochastic step d = −αkgk gives 

F (xk+1 ) ≤ F (xk) − αk∇F (xk)Tgk + Lα 2

> k

2 ∥gk∥22. (4.145) Taking conditional expectation and using equations (4.143) and (4.144) yields 

E[F (xk+1 ) | xk] ≤ F (xk) − αk

> (

1 − Lα k

2

> )

∥∇ F (xk)∥22 + Lα 2

> k

2 σ2. (4.146) This inequality displays the essential competition in SGD. The term involving ∥∇ F (xk)∥22 produces progress, while the final term is the price of gradient noise. The useful term is proportional to αk,whereas the noise term is proportional to α2

> k

. Thus sufficiently small learning rates make the noise less important than the expected descent. For example, if αk = α ≤ 1/L is constant, then equation (4.146) simplifies to 

E[F (xk+1 ) | xk] ≤ F (xk) − α

2 ∥∇ F (xk)∥22 + Lα 2

2 σ2. (4.147) Section 4.4 79 

Summing this inequality for k = 0 , . . . , K − 1 and using F (xK ) ≥ F⋆ gives 1

K

> K−1∑
> k=0

E

> [

∥∇ F (xk)∥22

> ]

≤ 2( F (x0) − F⋆)

αK + Lασ 2. (4.148) The first term decreases as more iterations are taken, but the second does not. Consequently, a constant learning rate usually brings SGD to a neighborhood of a stationary point and then causes it to fluctuate there. A smaller learning rate produces a smaller final neighborhood but slower initial progress. If the number of iterations K is known in advance, choosing α proportional to 1 /√K balances the two terms in equation (4.148) and gives an O(1 /√K) bound on the average squared gradient norm. Alternatively, one may gradually decrease the learning rate. The classical conditions 

> ∞∑
> k=0

αk = ∞,

> ∞∑
> k=0

α2 

> k

< ∞ (4.149) capture the desired balance: the steps remain large enough to keep moving, but the accumulated effect of the noise is finite. Under the assumptions above and standard regularity conditions, convergence theorems show that suitable averages of the gradient norm approach zero and that convergent limit points are stationary. For convex objectives, related arguments give convergence in objective value; stronger convexity permits faster rates such as O(1 /k ) with suitable decreasing learning rates. 

4.4.2 Practical Interpretation 

The analysis explains the main design choices. Larger mini-batches reduce σ2 but cost more per iteration. Larger learning rates move quickly but amplify noise. Decreasing the learning rate allows coarse progress early and more accurate refinement later. In practice, one monitors training and validation losses, gradient norms when available, and sensitivity to the learning-rate schedule rather than relying only on the iteration count. Many extensions modify the basic SGD update. Momentum smooths successive directions; RMSProp and Adam adapt the effective learning rate of each coordinate; and AdamW combines Adam with separately applied weight decay. These methods are important in modern machine learning, but they introduce additional state and tuning choices. The central idea remains the same: use inexpensive noisy gradient information while controlling its noise through batching and learning rates.

Short solution. Since ∇f (1 , 1) = (1 , 100), gradient descent gives x1 = (0 .99 , 0). The Hessian is 80 Chapter 4. Numerical Optimization Algorithms

Short solution. The update is xk+1 = (1 − aα )xk. Objective decrease requires |1 − aα | < 1, which is equivalent to 0 < α < 2/a .

Exercise 4.5. Suppose a convex objective is L-smooth with L = 10 and ∥x0 − x⋆∥2 = 2. Using equation (4.31), how many iterations suffice to guarantee f (xk) − f (x⋆) ≤ 0.01?

Exercise 4.6. For a 2-strongly convex and 10-smooth function, what contraction factor is guaranteed by gradient descent with step size 1 /L ?

Short solution. The factor is 1 − μ/L = 1 − 2/10 = 0 .8. Thus ∆ k ≤ 0.8k∆0.

Exercise 4.7. Use Armijo backtracking on f (x) = x2 at x = 1 with direction p = −∇ f (1) = −2, initial step α = 1, c = 10 −4, and reduction factor ρ = 1 /2. Which step is first accepted? 

Short solution. For α = 1, the trial point is −1 and the objective does not decrease, so the step is rejected. For α = 1 /2, the trial point is 0, which satisfies the Armijo condition. The first accepted step is therefore α = 1 /2. 

Exercise 4.8. For the quadratic function f (x) = 12 xTQx − bTx with Q ≻ 0, show that one Newton step from any starting point reaches the minimizer.

Exercise 4.10. Minimize f (x) = 12 (x−3) 2 over C = [0 , 1]. Verify the projected-gradient fixed-point condition at the solution for any α > 0. 

Short solution. The constrained minimizer is x⋆ = 1 and ∇f (1) = −2. Therefore x⋆ − α∇f (x⋆) = 1 + 2 α > 1, whose projection onto [0 , 1] is again 1. Thus Π C (x⋆ − α∇f (x⋆)) = x⋆.

Exercise 4.11. Apply soft thresholding to z = (3 , 0.4, −2) with threshold αλ = 1.

2 (x − 1) 2. (4.153) Find its minimizer and show how it approaches the constraint x = 1. 

Short solution. Setting the derivative to zero gives x + ρ(x − 1) = 0, so xρ = ρ/ (1 + ρ). The violation is 1 − xρ = 1 /(1 + ρ), which tends to zero as ρ → ∞ .

Exercise 4.13. The constrained problem min x≥0 x has solution x⋆ = 0. Find the minimizer of its logarithmic-barrier objective x − μ log x for x > 0. 

Short solution. The derivative is 1 − μ/x . Setting it to zero gives xμ = μ. Hence the barrier solution remains strictly positive and approaches 0 as μ ↓ 0. 

Exercise 4.14. Let f1(x) = 12 (x − 1) 2 and f2(x) = 12 (x + 1) 2, and define F = ( f1 + f2)/2. If one component is sampled uniformly, show that its gradient is an unbiased estimate of ∇F (x). 

Short solution. The sampled gradients are x − 1 and x + 1. Their mean is 12 (x − 1) + 12 (x + 1) = x = ∇F (x), (4.154) so the estimate is unbiased. 

Exercise 4.15. Suppose independent single-sample gradient estimates have variance σ2. What is the variance of their average over a mini-batch of size B?

Short solution. Independence makes the variances add, while averaging contributes a factor 1 /B 2.Thus the mini-batch variance is Bσ 2/B 2 = σ2/B .

Chapter summary. Gradient methods are inexpensive, curvature-based methods reduce it-eration count, projection and proximal operators handle constraints or nonsmooth terms, and stochastic methods scale to data-driven objectives. In practice, reliable optimization depends as much on step sizes, scaling, and residual checks as on the choice of algorithm. Part II

# Optimization in Function Spaces 

Chapter map. Functionals, first variations, Euler–Lagrange equations, mechanical systems, and constrained dynamics. 

The variables in the previous chapters were finite-dimensional vectors. Many problems in mechanics and control instead ask us to choose an entire curve. A robot trajectory, for example, is a function 

q : [ t0, t f ] → Rn (5.1) that specifies a configuration at every time. After choosing a time grid, its samples can be converted into an ordinary finite-dimensional vector. For example, choose N + 1 equally spaced times 

ti = t0 + ih, h = tf − t0

N , i = 0 , . . . , N, (5.2) and write qi = q(ti) ∈ Rn. The entire sampled trajectory is then represented by the stacked vector 

q =



q0

q1

...

qN

 ∈ Rn(N +1) . (5.3) Thus, sampling a two-joint robot at 101 times produces a vector containing 2(101) = 202 scalar entries. Derivatives and integrals can also be replaced by finite-dimensional approximations, such as ˙q(ti) ≈ qi+1 − qi

∫ tf

> t0

f (t, q, ˙q) dt ≈ h 

> N−1

∑

> i=0

(

ti, q i, qi+1 − qi

h

)

. (5.4) Here f is simply a generic scalar-valued function being integrated along the trajectory. The resulting problem is an optimization problem over the vector q, of the kind studied in Chapter 1. Function-space optimization instead studies the curve before choosing the grid. This viewpoint reveals the differential equations and boundary conditions hidden inside the discretized problem; a 84 Section 5.1 85 

grid can then be chosen later for numerical computation. The main idea is the same as a directional derivative. We perturb a candidate curve by a small function, compute the first-order change in the objective, and require that change to vanish for every admissible perturbation. The resulting Euler–Lagrange equation becomes an equation of motion when the objective is a mechanical action. Equality constraints then add reaction forces through Lagrange multipliers, while unilateral contact adds inequality and complementarity conditions.

Definition 5.1 (Functional) . A functional is a map that takes a function as input and returns a scalar. The integral in equation (5.5) is the main example used in this chapter. The allowed curves form the admissible set. For example, fixed endpoints impose 

q(t0) = q0, q(tf ) = qf . (5.6) We assume enough smoothness for the displayed derivatives and integrals to exist. Continuously differentiable curves are sufficient for the variation, and twice differentiable curves are used when the final equation contains ¨ q.

5.3.2 Endpoint Constraints and Transversality 

An endpoint may be neither fixed nor completely free. For example, a mobile robot may be required to finish somewhere on a docking line, without specifying its exact location on that line. Similarly, a robot arm may need to place its end effector somewhere on a target surface. In both cases the final configuration is allowed to move, but only in directions that preserve the endpoint constraint. Suppose the final configuration must satisfy 

c(q(tf )) = 0 , c : Rn → Rm. (5.30) The m components of c describe the target surface, or more generally the target manifold. Let

This condition is called a transversality condition . The name indicates that it determines how the stationary curve is allowed to meet the endpoint surface. Thus transversality expresses stationarity with respect to allowed endpoint motions; a geometric right angle follows only when bf is aligned with the terminal velocity.

# 5.4 Mechanical Systems from the Action 

5.4.1 Mechanical Lagrangians and Generalized Forces 

For a conservative mechanical system, define 

L(q, ˙q) = T (q, ˙q) − V (q), (5.52) where T is kinetic energy and V is potential energy. The functional 

S[q] =  

> ∫tf
> t0

L(q, ˙q) dt (5.53) is called the action. Physical motion makes the first variation of the action zero. It need not minimize the action globally; stationarity is the key fact. An applied generalized force τ (t) performs virtual work τ Tη. Including this work gives the forced equation 

ddt ∂L ∂ ˙q − ∂L ∂q = τ. (5.54) 

Example 5.7 (Mass on a spring) . For

m¨q + kq = τ. (5.56) If viscous damping exerts force −d ˙q and an actuator applies u, then 

m¨q + d ˙q + kq = u. (5.57) 

Example 5.8 (Simple pendulum) . For a point mass m on a massless rod of length ℓ,

T = 12 mℓ 2 ˙θ2, V = mgℓ (1 − cos θ). (5.58) Therefore 

mℓ 2 ¨θ + mgℓ sin θ = τ. (5.59) Section 5.4 93 

The nonlinear sine term comes directly from gravitational potential energy.

# 5.5 Advanced Constrained Dynamics and Physics Simulation 

Remark (For advanced readers) . This section is particularly intended for readers interested in a modern, optimization-based viewpoint of dynamics and physics simulation. The preceding sections developed the core function-space and Euler–Lagrange ideas. Here we go further by treating reaction forces, unilateral contact, and impacts as unknowns determined by constrained linear systems, KKT conditions, and complementarity problems. These formulations are central to many contemporary robotics and rigid-body simulators, where each time step requires solving an optimization-related algebraic problem in addition to integrating the equations of motion. 

5.5.1 Forward Dynamics and Time-Stepping Simulation 

Forward dynamics computes the acceleration from the current configuration q, velocity ˙ q, and applied generalized force τ . For the unconstrained mechanical system in equation (5.67), this means solving 

M (q)¨ q = τ − h(q, ˙q) − ∇ V (q). (5.89) Since M (q) ≻ 0, the acceleration is uniquely determined: ¨q = fFD (q, ˙q, τ ) := M (q)−1(τ − h(q, ˙q) − ∇ V (q)). (5.90) In computation, one solves the linear system with M (q) rather than forming its inverse. This is an instantaneous calculation: it gives the acceleration at the supplied state, not the entire future trajectory. A physics simulator turns this calculation into motion by numerically integrating the first-order system ˙q = v, ˙v = fFD (q, v, τ ). (5.91) Choose a time step ∆ t > 0, and let ( qk, v k) approximate the state at tk = t0 + k∆t. Given the applied force τk, first compute 

ak = fFD (qk, v k, τ k). (5.92) 

Explicit Euler then updates both position and velocity using the old state: 

qk+1 = qk + ∆ t v k, vk+1 = vk + ∆ t a k. (5.93) 

Semi-implicit Euler instead updates velocity first and uses that new velocity to advance position: 

vk+1 = vk + ∆ t a k, qk+1 = qk + ∆ t v k+1 . (5.94) The acceleration is still evaluated at ( qk, v k); this update requires no additional nonlinear solve. Both rules are first-order approximations, and their difference is the velocity used in the position update. Repeating the acceleration calculation and state update produces a simulated trajectory. Section 5.5 97 

With constraints, the same simulation workflow applies, but the acceleration calculation must also determine the unknown reaction forces. The following subsection explains this coupled forward-dynamics solve for equality constraints; later we extend it to unilateral contact and impacts. 

5.5.2 Dynamics with Equality Constraints 

Configuration Constraints and Reaction Forces 

Suppose the configuration is restricted by p smooth, time-independent equality constraints 

c(q) = 0 , c : Rn → Rp, (5.95) with p ≤ n. Such constraints are called holonomic because they are expressed directly in terms of the configuration. Define the constraint Jacobian 

J(q) = ∂c (q)

∂q ∈ Rp×n. (5.96) We assume that J(q) has full row rank on the constraint surface, so the p constraints are locally independent. The constraints can be incorporated directly into the variational principle. Because c(q(t)) = 0 must hold at every time, its multiplier is not a single vector but a time-dependent function 

λ : [ t0, t f ] → Rp. (5.97) Define the multiplier-augmented, also called extended , Lagrangian and its action by 

Laug (q, ˙q, λ ) = L(q, ˙q) + λTc(q), (5.98)

Setting η = 0 and allowing μ(t) to vary arbitrarily recovers the original constraint: 

c(q(t)) = 0 for every t. (5.102) Next, setting μ = 0 and integrating the ˙ η term by parts eliminates the endpoint terms. Since the interior perturbation η(t) is arbitrary, its coefficient must vanish. Together, the two variations give 

ddt ∂L ∂ ˙q − ∂L ∂q = τ + J(q)Tλ, c(q) = 0 . (5.103) The multiplier variation enforces feasibility, while the configuration variation supplies the equation of motion with the additional force J(q)Tλ.To interpret this force geometrically, consider a virtual displacement that is compatible with the original constraint. Unlike the unrestricted configuration perturbations used in the augmented formulation, a compatible displacement must be tangent to the constraint surface. Linearizing 

c(q) = 0 gives 

J(q)η = 0 . (5.104) The tangent space is therefore the null space of J(q). An ideal constraint reaction does no virtual work along these tangent directions, so it lies in their orthogonal complement: the normal space, which is the range of J(q)T. This is exactly the force produced by the multiplier: 

fc = J(q)Tλ, λ ∈ Rp. (5.105) For the mechanical Lagrangian L = T − V , the constrained Euler–Lagrange equation consequently takes the manipulator form 

M (q)¨ q + h(q, ˙q) + ∇V (q) = τ + J(q)Tλ, (5.106) where the multiplier λ determines the magnitude of each constraint reaction. The reaction force does no virtual work along any compatible displacement: (JTλ)Tη = λTJη = 0 . (5.107) Likewise, along a feasible trajectory it contributes no instantaneous power, because J ˙q = 0 implies (JTλ)T ˙q = 0 . (5.108) The position constraint must hold for all time. Differentiating it once gives the velocity constraint 

ddt c(q(t)) = J(q) ˙ q = 0 . (5.109) Differentiating once more gives the acceleration constraint 

ddt 

> (

˙qi (5.111) is the derivative of the constraint Jacobian along the trajectory. The term ˙J ˙q accounts for the curvature of the constraint surface; even a constant-speed feasible motion may require a normal acceleration. At a given state ( q, ˙q) and applied force τ , neither the acceleration ¨ q nor the reaction multiplier λ

is known. They must be determined together : the reaction force J(q)Tλ changes the acceleration, while the requirement that this acceleration satisfy the constraint determines the reaction force. Computing the unconstrained acceleration first and treating it as the final answer would generally violate the constraint. To solve for these coupled unknowns, define the nonconstraint generalized force 

r(q, ˙q, τ ) = τ − h(q, ˙q) − ∇ V (q). (5.112) Then the dynamics read M ¨q − JTλ = r. These n equations alone do not determine the n + p

unknown components of (¨ q, λ ). The p acceleration constraints supply the remaining equations, producing the coupled linear saddle-point system

To see why this is called a saddle-point system, hold q, ˙ q, and τ fixed and regard the acceleration 

a as the decision variable. Consider the equality-constrained quadratic problem minimize 

> a

12 aTM (q)a − r(q, ˙q, τ )Ta

subject to J(q)a + ˙J(q, ˙q) ˙ q = 0 .

La(a, λ ) = 12 aTM a − rTa − λT(Ja + ˙J ˙q). (5.115) The primal variable a is minimized, while the unrestricted multiplier λ is maximized. The first-order conditions are 

∇aLa = M a − r − JTλ = 0 , (5.116) 

∇λLa = −(Ja + ˙J ˙q) = 0 . (5.117) 100 Chapter 5. Optimization in Function Spaces 

Setting a = ¨ q produces exactly equation (5.113). The solution is therefore a saddle point of La: it is a minimum in the acceleration direction and a maximum in the multiplier direction. We now use a Schur-complement reduction to solve equation (5.113) by eliminating the accel-eration, solving a smaller system for the multipliers, and then recovering the acceleration by back-substitution. 

Schur-complement reduction. For a general block linear system

Thus elimination reduces the size of the linear solve without changing the original equations or uncoupling the underlying problem. We now apply this procedure to equation (5.113), with the block identification 

A = M, B = −JT, C = J, D = 0 , x = ¨ q, y = λ, b = r, d = − ˙J ˙q. (5.120) The acceleration and reaction force remain coupled, but we need not solve the full block system at once. We can eliminate the acceleration by expressing it in terms of the multiplier and substituting into the constraint. Throughout this calculation, q, ˙ q, and τ are fixed, so M , J, ˙J, and r are known. The first block equation, M ¨q − JTλ = r, gives ¨q = M −1r + M −1JTλ. (5.121) Substituting into the second block equation, J ¨q = − ˙J ˙q, removes ¨ q from the solve: 

JM −1r + JM −1JTλ = − ˙J ˙q. (5.122) Define the p × p matrix 

W (q) = J(q)M (q)−1J(q)T. (5.123) The reduced system is then 

W λ = − ˙J ˙q − JM −1r. (5.124) Once λ is found, back-substitution into equation (5.121) recovers ¨ q. This is an algebraic rearrange-ment of the same coupled problem, not a separate assumption about the reaction force. Section 5.5 101 

Consistent with the general formula, the Schur complement of M is 0 − JM −1(−JT) = W .Physically, W is the constraint-space inverse inertia : W λ is the change in constraint acceleration caused by the reaction force JTλ.The reduced system has a unique solution under our assumptions that M ≻ 0 and J has full row rank. To see why, recall that W = JM −1JT. Since M −1 is symmetric, W is symmetric as well. Moreover, full row rank means that the rows of J are linearly independent: no nonzero combination of them can vanish. Thus, for any nonzero z ∈ Rp, the vector u = JTz is nonzero. Because M −1 is positive definite, 

zTW z = ( JTz)TM −1(JTz) = uTM −1u > 0. (5.125) Therefore W is symmetric positive definite and hence invertible. The reduced equation determines a unique multiplier λ; back-substitution then determines a unique acceleration ¨ q.For numerical computation, the p × p reduced system can be solved using a Cholesky factorization of W . This reduction is particularly useful when there are far fewer constraints than coordinates (p ≪ n). There is no need to form M −1 explicitly. Instead, compute the free acceleration afree ∈ Rn

and the matrix Y ∈ Rn×p by solving 

M a free = r, M Y = JT. (5.126) Form W = JY , solve for the multiplier, and recover the acceleration: 

W λ = − ˙J ˙q − Ja free , ¨q = afree + Y λ. (5.127) Since afree = M −1r and Y = M −1JT, the recovered acceleration has a simple physical interpreta-tion: ¨q = afree + M −1JTλ. (5.128) The first term is the acceleration without constraint reactions; the second is the correction produced by the reaction force JTλ. Together they satisfy J ¨q = − ˙J ˙q. Equivalently, the quadratic formulation above shows that ¨ q is the feasible acceleration closest to afree in the mass-matrix metric, which measures a correction ∆ a by ∆ aTM ∆a.

Constraint drift. In exact continuous dynamics, consistent initial data satisfying c(q(0)) = 0 and J(q(0)) ˙ q(0) = 0 remain feasible. A numerical integrator, or time-stepping method , is an algorithm that approximates a continuous trajectory at discrete times 

tk = t0 + kh, (5.129) where h > 0 is the time-step length. Given approximations qk ≈ q(tk) and vk ≈ ˙q(tk), the algorithm computes an acceleration ak from the equations of motion and then advances the state. For example, the explicit Euler method uses 

qk+1 = qk + hv k, vk+1 = vk + ha k. (5.130) 102 Chapter 5. Optimization in Function Spaces 

These updates replace continuous motion over an interval by a finite approximation and therefore introduce a small discretization error at each step. Consequently, even if ak satisfies the acceleration constraint Ja k = − ˙Jv k, the updated state need not satisfy c(qk+1 ) = 0 and J(qk+1 )vk+1 = 0 exactly. The resulting position and velocity errors can accumulate over many time steps, gradually moving the computed trajectory away from the constraint manifold. This phenomenon is called constraint drift . A smaller time step or a higher-order integrator generally reduces the local error, but does not by itself guarantee that the constraints remain exactly satisfied. 

Projection. One remedy is to correct the state after each time step. Given a tentative position 

> ˜

qk+1 , position projection finds a nearby point on the constraint manifold, for example by solving minimize 

> q

12 ∥q − ˜qk+1 ∥22 subject to c(q) = 0 . (5.131) Because c may be nonlinear, this projection usually requires a small iterative solve. After obtaining the corrected position qk+1 , the tentative velocity ˜vk+1 can be projected onto the tangent space by requiring 

J(qk+1 )vk+1 = 0 . (5.132) The correction that changes velocity as little as possible in the mass metric is 

vk+1 = ˜vk+1 − M −1JT(JM −1JT)−1J ˜vk+1 , (5.133) where M and J are evaluated at qk+1 . Thus the same Schur-complement matrix W = JM −1JT

that determines reaction forces also appears in velocity projection. Projection removes drift directly, but adds one or more constraint solves to every time step. 

Baumgarte stabilization. Projection corrects the position and velocity after a time step. Baumgarte stabilization takes a different approach: it adjusts the acceleration before the update so that the motion gradually corrects any constraint error. To understand the idea, let 

e(t) = c(q(t)) (5.134) measure how far the current configuration is from satisfying the constraint. Here e has one component per constraint; it is a constraint residual, not necessarily a geometric distance. Its derivative ˙ e = J ˙q measures how quickly that residual is changing. The usual acceleration constraint imposes ¨ e = 0. If e and ˙ e are initially zero, this keeps them zero in exact continuous motion. But it does not repair an error that has already appeared. In fact, ¨e = 0 gives 

e(t) = e(t0) + ( t − t0) ˙ e(t0). (5.135) A position error therefore persists, and a velocity error makes it grow. This is why merely enforcing the acceleration constraint is not enough to remove numerical drift. The remedy is to ask the error to behave like a damped spring returning to its equilibrium at zero. Section 5.5 103 

Baumgarte stabilization prescribes ¨e + 2 α ˙e + β2e = 0 , α, β > 0. (5.136) Equivalently, ¨ e = −2α ˙e − β2e. The position term −β2e pulls the error back toward zero, while the velocity term −2α ˙e damps its motion to reduce overshoot. For example, for the scalar constraint 

c(q) = q = 0, a positive position error with zero velocity requests a negative acceleration; a negative error requests a positive one. These are corrections to the constraint error , not extra actuator commands or a replacement for the equations of motion. With α, β > 0, this continuous-time error equation makes both e and ˙ e decay to zero. The parameter β sets the natural frequency of the correction, and α/β is its damping ratio. A useful starting choice is α = β: the error dynamics are then critically damped, with no oscillatory modes. Smaller damping ( α < β ) allows decaying oscillations, while larger damping ( α > β ) gives an overdamped response. To use this idea in the dynamics solve, express the error derivatives in terms of the robot state: ˙e = J ˙q, ¨e = J ¨q + ˙J ˙q. (5.137) Substituting these into equation (5.136) and isolating J ¨q gives the stabilized acceleration constraint: 

J ¨q = − ˙J ˙q − 2αJ ˙q − β2c(q). (5.138) Compared with J ¨q = − ˙J ˙q, only two feedback terms have been added: one for position error and one for velocity error. When both errors are zero, these terms vanish and the original acceleration constraint is recovered. The equations of motion remain unchanged; acceleration and reaction force are still solved for together, using this modified constraint. Unlike projection, stabilization does not place the numerical state exactly on the constraint surface after every step. It encourages errors to decay, while discretization may continue to introduce small new errors. Larger gains request faster correction, but a finite-step integrator must resolve that faster motion. Gains that are too large for the chosen time step can therefore cause numerical instability. This separation between fast error correction and slower physical motion is a source of 

stiffness .The implementation uses the same block system as before, with only its constraint right-hand side changed. Let qk and vk approximate q(tk) and ˙ q(tk). At the beginning of a time step, evaluate 

Mk = M (qk), Jk = J(qk), rk = r(qk, v k, τ k), (5.139) and ˙Jk =

> n∑
> i=1

∂J ∂q i

(qk)( vk)i. (5.140) The Baumgarte-stabilized acceleration ak and reaction multiplier λk are obtained from the linear system [

One simple update is the semi-implicit Euler rule 

vk+1 = vk + ha k, (5.142) 

qk+1 = qk + hv k+1 . (5.143) Thus each step consists of evaluating the dynamics, solving one KKT system, and advancing the state. The name semi-implicit Euler , also called symplectic Euler , refers to the order of these two updates. First the old state ( qk, v k) is used to compute ak and the new velocity vk+1 . The position is then advanced using this new velocity rather than the old one. In contrast, fully explicit Euler updates both quantities from the old state: 

vk+1 = vk + ha k, qk+1 = qk + hv k. (5.144) The semi-implicit method is still straightforward to compute sequentially; no additional nonlinear equation must be solved for qk+1 . The word “implicit” only reflects that the position update depends on the already updated velocity. This small change often gives better stability and energy behavior for mechanical systems.

gy − ˙x2 − ˙y2)

. (5.154) The physical radial force is JTλ. Alternatively, substituting x = ℓ sin θ and y = −ℓ cos θ eliminates the constraint and recovers the pendulum equation. Multiplier coordinates retain the Cartesian variables and make the reaction force explicit. 

5.5.3 Inequality Constraints and Contact 

The equality-constrained dynamics above used unrestricted multipliers because an equality reaction may act in either normal direction. Contact is different: two rigid bodies may push one another apart, but they cannot pull one another together. This one-sided physical rule leads directly to inequality constraints, nonnegative multipliers, and KKT complementarity. 

Variational Multipliers and the KKT Analogy 

The following conditions are not restricted to static equilibrium. They are imposed at each time along a trajectory q(t), and the active contacts and their forces may change with time. Static contact is the special case ˙ q = ¨ q = 0. Let 

ϕ(q) ∈ Rm, ϕ(q) ≥ 0, (5.155) collect the gap functions for m possible contacts. All inequalities are componentwise. For contact 

i,

ϕi(q) > 0 means separation , ϕi(q) = 0 means touching , (5.156) whereas ϕi(q) < 0 would mean forbidden penetration. Define 

Jc(q) = ∂ϕ (q)

∂q ∈ Rm×n. (5.157) The transpose of row i of Jc points in the generalized direction that increases gap i.Let λi(t) ≥ 0 denote the magnitude of the outward normal force at contact i. With this physical sign convention, introduce the extended mechanical Lagrangian 

Lc(q, ˙q, λ ) = L(q, ˙q) + λTϕ(q). (5.158) 106 Chapter 5. Optimization in Function Spaces 

The variation of its multiplier term is 

δq

> (

λTϕ(q)) = λTJc(q)η = (Jc(q)Tλ)Tη. (5.159) Thus the multiplier contributes the generalized force Jc(q)Tλ, which points toward increasing separation. Including an applied generalized force τ gives 

ddt ∂L ∂ ˙q − ∂L ∂q = τ + Jc(q)Tλ. (5.160) The plus sign in Lc reflects this mechanical force convention. By comparison, an ordinary minimization problem with ϕ(q) ≥ 0 uses 

LKKT (q, μ ) = F (q) − μTϕ(q), μ ≥ 0, (5.161) because the constraint is written as −ϕ(q) ≤ 0. The two formulas serve different purposes: one represents an outward physical force in a stationary action principle, while the other is the standard KKT Lagrangian for a minimization problem. Because contact is unilateral, λ is not an unrestricted equality multiplier. The gap and force instead satisfy 

ϕ(q) ≥ 0, λ ≥ 0, λTϕ(q) = 0 . (5.162) These conditions are analogous to primal feasibility, dual feasibility, and complementary slackness. For each contact, 

ϕi(q) > 0 =⇒ λi = 0 , λi > 0 =⇒ ϕi(q) = 0 . (5.163) Separated bodies therefore exert no contact force, and a positive contact force can act only when the gap is closed. Combining the variational equation and complementarity gives the necessary position-level relations 

ddt ∂L ∂ ˙q − ∂L ∂q = τ + JT 

> c

λ, 0 ≤ λ ⊥ ϕ(q) ≥ 0. (5.164) The notation 0 ≤ λ ⊥ ϕ(q) ≥ 0 is shorthand for complementarity : for every contact i,

λi ≥ 0, ϕi(q) ≥ 0, λiϕi(q) = 0 . (5.165) Here λi is the normal contact force and ϕi(q) is the gap. The inequalities say that the force can push but not pull, and that the bodies cannot penetrate. The zero product says that force and gap cannot both be positive: an open gap requires zero force, while a positive force requires a closed gap. Both may be zero, so touching does not necessarily imply a positive force. The symbol ⊥

denotes the zero inner product λTϕ(q) = 0; because both vectors are componentwise nonnegative, this is equivalent to the zero-product condition for each contact. It does not refer to perpendicular directions in physical space. Section 5.5 107 

For the mechanical Lagrangian developed earlier, the first relation becomes 

M (q)¨ q + h(q, ˙q) + ∇V (q) = τ + Jc(q)Tλ. (5.166) These equations identify where contact forces may act and the direction in which they act, but they do not yet determine the magnitude of λ at a closed contact. If ϕi(q) = 0, position complementarity only requires λi ≥ 0. Moreover, the dynamics contain the n + m unknowns (¨ q, λ ) but provide only 

n equations. The missing conditions come from requiring the closed gaps not to accelerate into penetration. Differentiating the active gap constraints supplies those acceleration-level conditions and closes the forward-dynamics problem in the next subsection. Impacts require a separate velocity-level impulse law because velocity may jump discontinuously. 

From Position Constraints to Acceleration Constraints 

The position-level KKT conditions explain when a force may act, but a forward dynamics solver must determine the force at the current instant. First, distinguish an impact from persistent contact. At an impact time timp , the superscripts − and + denote values immediately before and 

after the impact, respectively: ˙q− = lim 

> t→t−
> imp

˙q(t), ˙q+ = lim 

> t→t+imp

˙q(t). (5.167) Here − and + identify the two sides of the event; they do not indicate the sign of the velocity. The configuration q remains continuous, but the velocity may jump. The normal velocity measures how quickly the two bodies approach or separate in the direction perpendicular to their contact surface. With our gap convention, it is the rate of change of the gap: 

vn = ˙ϕ(q) = Jc(q) ˙ q. (5.168) For each contact, vn < 0 means that the gap is shrinking (approach), vn > 0 means that it is growing (separation), and vn = 0 means that the gap is instantaneously unchanged. Zero normal velocity does not require zero total velocity: the bodies may still slide tangentially along the contact surface. For example, for a point above a horizontal floor with q = ( x, y )T and gap ϕ(q) = y,we have Jc = [0 1] and vn = ˙ y; the horizontal velocity ˙ x does not change the gap. Accordingly,

Eliminating Acceleration: The Linear Complementarity Problem 

Define the generalized force excluding contact by 

r(q, ˙q, τ ) = τ − h(q, ˙q) − ∇ V (q). (5.173) At a fixed state ( q, ˙q), the equations of motion are 

M (q)¨ q = r(q, ˙q, τ ) + Jc(q)Tλ. (5.174) Both the acceleration ¨ q and the contact force λ are unknown. To eliminate acceleration means to solve this equation algebraically for ¨ q and substitute the result into the normal-acceleration constraint. Since M (q) is positive definite, ¨q = M −1(r + JT

A solution identifies which contacts remain active and supplies contact forces consistent with nonpenetration. The important point is that the unknown acceleration no longer appears in the LCP: the problem is expressed entirely in the m

contact-force variables. Once λ is known, equation (5.175) recovers ¨ q.

Solving the LCP as a Convex Quadratic Program 

Because A ⪰ 0, the LCP in equation (5.178) can be solved through the convex quadratic program minimize 

> λ≥0

12 λTAλ + bTλ. (5.179) The gradient of its objective is 

∇λ 

> (

12 λTAλ + bTλ

> )

= Aλ + b = w. (5.180) The KKT conditions for the lower bound λ ≥ 0 are therefore 

λ ≥ 0, w ≥ 0, λTw = 0 , (5.181) which are exactly the LCP conditions. Numerically, one supplies the Hessian A, the linear term b,and the lower bound λ ≥ 0 to a convex QP solver. The solver determines the active contacts and their forces simultaneously. If A is positive definite, the contact force is unique. If A is singular, as can occur with redundant contact constraints, several multipliers may produce the same physical acceleration. After selecting a solution λ, the simulator recovers ¨ q from equation (5.175) and advances ( q, ˙q) using a numerical integrator. Thus a forward-dynamics step consists of a contact solve followed by a state update. 

Remark (Alternative KKT derivation in acceleration space) . The LCP can be understood directly from force balance and acceleration complementarity, so the following optimization interpretation is not needed for using it. It provides a second derivation. The free acceleration, obtained by setting λ = 0, is ¨qfree = M (q)−1r(q, ˙q, τ ). Example 5.9 (A mass resting on a floor) . Let y ≥ 0 be the height of a mass above a horizontal floor. At y = ˙ y = 0, 

m¨y = −mg + λ, 0 ≤ λ ⊥ ¨y ≥ 0. (5.189) If λ = 0, gravity gives ¨ y = −g, which violates primal feasibility. Therefore λ > 0, and complemen-tarity requires ¨ y = 0. The equation of motion then gives λ = mg . Thus the normal force is exactly the KKT multiplier needed to prevent penetration. 

Resolving rigid impacts. The acceleration-level conditions above describe sustained contact: the gap is closed and the normal velocity is already zero, Jc ˙q = 0. They do not by themselves handle a body that arrives at the contact with inward normal velocity Jcv− < 0. In the ideal rigid-contact model, penetration is forbidden, so this incoming velocity must change instantaneously. An ordinary force acting for zero time cannot produce a finite velocity change; instead, the model introduces a contact impulse p, the time integral of a very large contact force over the short impact interval. For a frictionless, perfectly inelastic impact, impulse–momentum balance and the unilateral contact conditions are 

M (q)( v+ − v−) = Jc(q)Tp, 0 ≤ p ⊥ Jcv+ ≥ 0. (5.190) Here v− and v+ are the velocities immediately before and after impact. The complementarity condition says that a contact receives an impulse only when its post-impact separating velocity is zero. From impulse–momentum balance, 

v+ = v− + M −1JT 

> c

p, (5.191) and therefore 

Jcv+ = Jcv− + Ap, A = JcM −1JT 

> c

. (5.192) Thus the impulse is found from the velocity-level LCP 0 ≤ p ⊥ Jcv− + Ap ≥ 0. (5.193) After this impact solve changes v− to v+, contacts with Jcv+ = 0 are maintained by the acceleration-level contact solve. A rigid time-stepping simulator may therefore distinguish an impact stage Section 5.5 111 

from the subsequent sustained-contact stage. 

Soft contact in modern simulation. Many modern simulators instead use a compliant, or 

soft , contact model. A small amount of penetration is allowed, and contact forces act over a finite time interval. The incoming normal velocity produces a damping response, while penetration produces a restoring response. Numerical integration then changes the velocity continuously across timesteps. Because the same force law handles the initial collision and the later resting contact, there is no separate instantaneous reset from v− to v+ and no need to solve the rigid impact LCP first. MuJoCo follows this soft-contact viewpoint. Its reference acceleration uses the normal velocity and gap in a spring–damper-like form, while a regularized contact optimization determines the admissible forces. The formulation below shows how this model is related to the rigid contact-space problem derived above Todorov [2011], Todorov et al. [2012]. 

Brief Connection to MuJoCo’s Soft Contact

For the frictionless normal constraints above, it is exactly the vector b.• Ω is the set of admissible constraint forces. For frictionless normal contact it is Rm

With the present gap convention, a scalar normal constraint has the schematic form (aref )i = −βi(Jc ˙q)i − kiϕi(q), βi > 0, ki ≥ 0. (5.197) 112 Chapter 5. Optimization in Function Spaces 

Thus closing velocity or penetration requests an outward corrective acceleration, while a resting contact with zero error has aref = 0. This is the same feedback idea used in Baumgarte stabilization: equation (5.136) prescribes ¨ e = −2α ˙e − β2e. Identifying the error with the gap, 

e = ϕi(q), gives the same spring–damper form with βi = 2 α and ki = β2. The distinction is that Baumgarte stabilization imposes this error acceleration as an equality, whereas here it serves as a reference for a soft, unilateral contact solve; the force restrictions and regularizer determine the acceleration actually realized. • R ≻ 0 adds a penalty 12 λTRλ on contact forces. This weakens exact enforcement of the reference acceleration and makes the force solution unique. The following explanation shows how a force penalty produces a soft contact response. 

Understanding the role of R. Consider one frictionless contact with A > 0 and scalar regularizer R = ρ > 0. If afree < a ref , the positive force needed to realize the reference without regularization is

Like a loaded spring, the contact supports weight by allowing compression; for fixed k, larger ρ

Chapter summary. A function can be optimized by perturbing it with a small function and computing the first variation. Integration by parts produces the Euler–Lagrange equation and the appropriate endpoint conditions. For a mechanical Lagrangian L = T − V , this equation yields the equations of motion, including the robot manipulator form. Equality constraints add reaction forces JTλ and lead to a KKT-like system for acceleration and multipliers. Unilateral contact adds nonnegative forces and complementary slackness; after acceleration or velocity is eliminated, frictionless contact becomes a linear complementarity problem. 6

# Continuous-Time Optimal Control 

Chapter map. Finite- and infinite-horizon control, cost-to-go functions, the Hamiltonian, costates, Pontryagin’s minimum principle, and numerical methods. 

Chapter 5 optimized an entire curve and showed how stationarity produces a differential equation. Optimal control adds an important restriction: the state trajectory cannot be chosen freely. It must be generated by a dynamical system, and the designer chooses a control input that influences that system. For a robot, the state might contain joint angles and velocities, while the control might be a vector of motor torques. The goal is to choose the complete control signal so that the resulting motion is safe, accurate, and economical. This viewpoint unifies many robotics tasks. Moving a manipulator between two configurations while limiting torque, landing a quadrotor with little fuel, steering a mobile robot around obstacles, and braking a vehicle in minimum time are all optimal control problems. The central result of this chapter is Pontryagin’s minimum principle (PMP). It introduces a costate and converts a constrained optimization over functions into coupled state, costate, and control conditions. The derivation uses the same perturbation and integration-by-parts ideas as Chapter 5. Before deriving the principle, we introduce the cost-to-go function and distinguish finite-horizon trajectory optimization from infinite-horizon feedback design. These ideas lead to the Riccati and dynamic-programming viewpoints developed in Chapters 7 and 8. Standard references include Kirk [2004], Lewis et al. [2012]. 

# 6.1 Problem Formulation 

6.1.1 Dynamics, Controls, and Trajectories 

Let 

x(t) ∈ Rn, u(t) ∈ Rm (6.1) 116 Section 6.1 117 

denote the state and control. A controlled system has dynamics ˙x(t) = f (t, x (t), u (t)), x(t0) = x0. (6.2) Once x0 and u(·) are specified, the state trajectory is determined by integrating this differential equation. Thus x(·) and u(·) are not independent optimization variables. We assume enough smoothness for the state equation and displayed derivatives to be well defined; smooth dynamics and piecewise-continuous controls suffice for this chapter. 

Example 6.1 (A torque-controlled robot) . Let q ∈ Rd be a manipulator configuration and v = ˙ q.With commanded joint torque u, its first-order state equation is 

x =

> [

qv

> ]

, ˙x =

> [

vM (q)−1(u − h(q, v ) − g(q))]

, (6.3) where M is the mass matrix, h contains velocity-dependent forces, and g(q) = ∇V (q) is gravity. The torque signal determines the motion through these equations. 

Example 6.2 (A planar mobile robot) . A unicycle model has state ( px, p y, θ ) and controls ( v, ω ): ˙px = v cos θ, ˙py = v sin θ, ˙θ = ω. (6.4) It captures the nonholonomic fact that the robot cannot translate instantaneously sideways. A pair ( x(·), u (·)) is admissible if it satisfies the dynamics, initial condition, regularity requirements, and all constraints.
