Costs should reflect the physical goal. Squared torque is a smooth measure of effort, but it is not literally electrical energy unless the actuator model supports that interpretation. Likewise, an obstacle penalty discourages collision but does not guarantee avoidance. 

6.1.3 The Cost-to-Go Function 

The optimization problem can be restarted at any intermediate time. Suppose the clock reads t

and the current state is x. The optimal cost-to-go , also called the value function , is 

V (t, x ) = inf 

> u(·)
> {

Φ(xu(tf ), t f 

> )

+ 

> ∫tf
> t

ℓ(s, x u(s), u (s)) ds 

> }

, (6.8) where xu satisfies ˙xu(s) = f (s, x u(s), u (s)), xu(t) = x, (6.9) and the infimum is over admissible controls on [ t, t f ]. In particular, the optimal value of the original problem is 

J⋆ = V (t0, x 0). (6.10) The distinction between J[u] and V (t, x ) is important. The functional J[u] evaluates one complete control signal from the specified initial condition. The value function instead asks a family of optimization problems: for every possible state x and starting time t, what is the smallest future cost still achievable? It compresses all optimal future behavior into one scalar field over time and state. The value also has a practical interpretation. A small value means that the remaining task can be completed cheaply; a large value means that the current state is difficult or expensive to recover from. Differences in V compare states by their future consequences rather than by geometric distance alone. For example, two robot states may be equally far from a target, but the state moving rapidly away from the target can have much larger cost-to-go. At the terminal time there is no running interval left, so 

V (tf , x ) = Φ( x, t f ). (6.11) If an optimizer exists for every feasible ( t, x ), the control selected at the current time defines an optimal feedback control law 

u = μ⋆(t, x ). (6.12) Starting from ( t0, x 0) and applying this law generates the particular optimal trajectory x⋆(·) and control u⋆(t) = μ⋆(t, x ⋆(t)). Thus the value function describes a whole family of optimal trajectories, whereas a single open-loop solution describes only one member of that family. Chapter 8 derives the equation satisfied by V ; here we use it primarily to clarify the meaning of optimal control and the costate. Section 6.1 119 

6.1.4 Infinite-Horizon Optimal Control 

Some tasks have a natural deadline or terminal event, but regulation and disturbance rejection are continuing tasks. For an autonomous system ˙x = f (x, u ), (6.13) an undiscounted infinite-horizon problem has the form 

V∞(x0) = inf  

> u(·)
> ∫∞
> 0

ℓ(x(t), u (t)) dt subject to ˙x = f (x, u ), x(0) = x0. (6.14) There is no terminal time or terminal cost. Instead, the running cost must encode the desired long-run behavior. In regulation problems, ℓ(x, u ) ≥ 0 usually vanishes at the desired equilibrium. A finite value then requires the controller to approach that equilibrium sufficiently fast. If the system cannot be stabilized, or if positive cost persists forever, the integral may be infinite. A discounted alternative is 

Vρ(x0) = inf  

> u(·)
> ∫∞
> 0

e−ρt ℓ(x(t), u (t)) dt, ρ > 0. (6.15) Discounting gives less weight to distant costs and can make the integral finite under weaker conditions. It is not merely a numerical trick, however: changing ρ changes the control objective and can make the controller more short-sighted. When the dynamics, cost, and constraints are time invariant, the infinite-horizon value depends only on the current state, and one seeks a stationary feedback law 

u = μ⋆

> ∞

(x). (6.16) The word stationary means that the same state produces the same control regardless of the clock time. It does not mean that the state is constant. Infinite-horizon problems still require appropriate assumptions for the value to be finite and for an optimal control law to exist. Unlike a finite-horizon problem, there is no fixed terminal time at which to impose a final boundary condition. The desired solution must instead be selected through appropriate long-run requirements, such as finite accumulated cost, convergence toward a target equilibrium, or closed-loop stability. The precise boundary conditions at infinity require care and are discussed later, after the necessary optimality conditions have been introduced. 

6.1.5 Finite Horizon, Infinite Horizon, and Feedback 

The horizon and the form of the solution are related, but they are different ideas. • A finite-horizon problem optimizes over [ t, t f ]. Its value V (t, x ) generally depends on both time and state because the remaining time and terminal cost matter. The corresponding feedback law μ⋆(t, x ) is generally time varying. • An infinite-horizon problem optimizes all future behavior. For an autonomous, time-invariant 120 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control 

problem, its value V∞(x) and optimal feedback μ⋆

> ∞

(x) are often stationary. The problem emphasizes long-run regulation or operation rather than arrival at a prescribed final time. • An optimal trajectory is the state–control pair produced for one initial condition. It is an open-loop plan: if a disturbance moves the robot away from that trajectory, the precomputed control signal does not by itself specify how to recover. • An optimal state-feedback controller is a rule defined over a set of possible states. It observes the current state and selects u = μ⋆(t, x ) for a finite-horizon problem, or u = μ⋆

> ∞

(x) for a stationary infinite-horizon problem. After a disturbance, it can therefore generate a new optimal or locally optimal trajectory from the disturbed state. Finite horizon does not mean open loop: finite-horizon dynamic programming produces the feedback law μ⋆(t, x ). Likewise, infinite horizon does not automatically mean feedback: one can solve an infinite-horizon problem for a single initial state and obtain only an open-loop extremal. Pontryagin’s principle naturally characterizes individual optimal trajectories, while the value-function viewpoint naturally produces feedback laws. Chapters 7 and 8 make this distinction concrete through Riccati equations and the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation. 

6.1.6 State and Control Constraints 

Practical systems may require 

u(t) ∈ U, x(t) ∈ X, (6.17) 

g(t, x (t), u (t)) ≤ 0, c(t, x (t), u (t)) = 0 , (6.18) and endpoint constraints 

ψ(x(t0), x (tf ), t f 

> )

= 0 . (6.19) Examples include torque and joint limits, obstacle clearance, contact, and a prescribed terminal pose. Control bounds 

umin ≤ u(t) ≤ umax (6.20) are essential in minimum-time problems; without them, the model may request unbounded force. State constraints are more subtle because they must hold at every time and may become active only over particular portions of a trajectory. We first study problems without active state-path constraints, while handling control constraints explicitly. Chapter 9 develops discretization-based methods better suited to complicated path constraints.

6.2.3 Statement of the Principle 

Theorem 6.3 (Pontryagin’s minimum principle, basic normal form) . Consider equation (6.6) with fixed times, fixed initial state, free terminal state, no active state-path constraints, and u(t) ∈ U .If (x⋆, u ⋆) is a sufficiently regular local optimum, then there exists a costate λ⋆ such that, almost everywhere, 

˙x⋆ = Hλ(t, x ⋆, u ⋆, λ ⋆) = f (t, x ⋆, u ⋆), (6.43) ˙λ⋆ = −Hx(t, x ⋆, u ⋆, λ ⋆), (6.44) 

u⋆(t) ∈ arg min 

> u∈U

H(t, x ⋆(t), u, λ ⋆(t)) , (6.45) 

λ⋆(tf ) = Φ x(x⋆(tf ), t f ). (6.46) The phrase “almost everywhere” allows the optimal control to have isolated switches or jump discontinuities. The state remains continuous, but a bounded optimal control can change value instantaneously at a switching time. A common class of such controls is introduced later in the chapter. These conditions should be read as one coupled system: 124 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control 

1. The state equation starts from the known initial condition x(t0) = x0 and naturally evolves forward in time. 2. The costate equation is usually supplied with a condition at the terminal time. For example, when the terminal state is free, λ(tf ) = Φ x(x(tf ), t f ). It therefore naturally evolves backward in time. 3. At each time, the minimizing condition determines u⋆(t) from the current values of x⋆(t) and 

λ⋆(t). Thus the state equation depends on the costate through the control, while the costate equation depends on the state. 4. The initial state alone is therefore insufficient for a forward integration, because the required control depends on the costate, whose initial value is generally unknown. Likewise, the terminal costate condition alone is insufficient for a backward integration, because its equation depends on the state trajectory. 5. The unknown boundary data must be adjusted until the forward state, backward costate, minimizing control, and all endpoint conditions agree simultaneously. Consequently, the equations cannot generally be solved by one independent forward integration followed by one independent backward integration. They form a two-point boundary-value problem: some data are specified at t0 and the remaining data are specified at tf . Resolving this forward–backward coupling is the main computational difficulty of the state–costate formulation. The state runs forward from x(t0) = x0, while the costate has a terminal condition and naturally runs backward. For an unconstrained interior control, Hamiltonian minimization reduces to 

Hu(t, x ⋆, u ⋆, λ ⋆) = 0 . (6.47) If H is strictly convex in u, this equation uniquely minimizes it. If H is linear in a bounded control, the solution usually lies on the boundary of U , producing bang–bang control. These conditions are necessary, not automatically sufficient. They identify extremals, which need not all be minima. 

Remark (Sign conventions) . Some texts define H = λTf − ℓ and state a maximum principle. We use H = ℓ + λTf , so the control minimizes H. The conventions are equivalent when used consistently. 

6.2.4 Extension to Infinite Horizons 

The finite-horizon theorem above does not apply to an infinite-horizon problem word for word, because there is no terminal time at which to impose λ(tf ) = Φ x. Nevertheless, its interior conditions extend in a natural way. For the undiscounted problem in equation (6.14), define 

H∞(x, u, λ ) = ℓ(x, u ) + λTf (x, u ). (6.48) Section 6.2 125 

Under suitable regularity and optimality assumptions, an infinite-horizon extremal satisfies the following conditions almost everywhere on every finite subinterval: ˙x⋆ = f (x⋆, u ⋆), (6.49) ˙λ⋆ = −H∞,x (x⋆, u ⋆, λ ⋆), (6.50) 

u⋆(t) ∈ arg min 

> u∈U

H∞(x⋆(t), u, λ ⋆(t)) . (6.51) Thus the state equation, costate equation, and pointwise Hamiltonian minimization remain. What changes is the boundary condition used to select the correct solution of these equations. There is no single transversality condition at infinity that is valid for every problem. Depending on the admissible trajectories and the notion of infinite-horizon optimality, one may obtain a limiting condition such as lim  

> t→∞

λ⋆(t)Tδx (t) = 0 (6.52) for an appropriate class of terminal variations. Under stronger assumptions, this may imply 

λ⋆(t) → 0, but that conclusion must be proved; it cannot be adopted as a universal terminal condition. This is one reason that infinite-horizon boundary-value computations require more care than simply replacing tf by infinity in the finite-horizon equations. For the discounted objective in equation (6.15), the running cost incurred at time t enters the objective with weight e−ρt . Applying the same multiplier argument as in the finite-horizon derivation therefore gives the augmented integrand 

e−ρt ℓ(x, u ) + pT(f (x, u ) − ˙x), (6.53) where p(t) is the costate associated with the discounted objective. The resulting present-value Hamiltonian is 

Hp(t, x, u, p ) = e−ρt ℓ(x, u ) + pTf (x, u ), (6.54) and the usual variation gives ˙p = −Hp 

> x

. (6.55) The term “present value” indicates that future costs, and hence their marginal value represented by p, are measured after discounting them to the initial time. Consequently, even for autonomous dynamics and running cost, Hp contains the explicit time-dependent factor e−ρt .It is often more convenient to express the marginal value of the state in undiscounted, time-t units. Define the rescaled costate 

λ(t) = eρt p(t). (6.56) Multiplying Hp by the positive factor eρt then gives the current-value Hamiltonian 

Hc(x, u, λ ) = eρt Hp(t, x, u, p ) = ℓ(x, u ) + λTf (x, u ). (6.57) Because eρt > 0, this rescaling does not change which control minimizes the Hamiltonian at a fixed time. Its advantage is that, for an autonomous problem, Hc has no explicit time dependence. 126 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control

6.2.5 The Costate as a Cost-to-Go Gradient 

The cost-to-go function in equation (6.8) assigns an optimal remaining cost to every time–state pair. To see how it is related to the costate, consider an optimal trajectory and pause it at time 

t. Perturb its state from x⋆(t) to x⋆(t) + δx , and then reoptimize the control over the remaining interval [ t, t f ]. If V is differentiable, the resulting change in optimal future cost is 

V (t, x ⋆(t) + δx ) − V (t, x ⋆(t)) = ∇xV (t, x ⋆(t))Tδx + o(∥δx ∥). (6.64) Now apply the first-variation calculation to this remaining-horizon problem, using t as its initial time. In equation (6.38), replace t0 by t. Since the state at this new initial time is perturbed by 

δx , its variation satisfies z(t) = δx . The first variation becomes 

δJ = (Φx − λ(tf ))Tz(tf ) + λ(t)Tδx (6.65) + 

> ∫tf
> t
> [

(Hx + ˙λ)Tz + HT 

> u

η

> ]

dt. (6.66) Each optimality condition removes one part of this expression: • the terminal condition λ(tf ) = Φ x removes the term involving z(tf ); • the costate equation ˙λ = −Hx removes the interior term involving z; and • the control stationarity or minimization condition removes the first-order contribution associated with adjusting the optimal control. For an unconstrained interior optimum, this is simply 

Hu = 0. Section 6.2 127 

This last point is an instance of the envelope principle: once the control is already optimal, its induced first-order adjustment does not contribute to the first-order change in the optimized cost. Consequently, the only remaining contribution is the boundary term at the perturbed starting state, 

δJ ⋆ = λ⋆(t)Tδx. (6.67) The optimized objective J⋆ on [ t, t f ] is precisely the value V (t, x ). Therefore equations (6.64) and (6.67) describe the same change in optimal cost. Because this equality holds for every small direction δx , their coefficients must agree. Hence, when V is smooth and the optimum is regular, the costate along an optimal trajectory satisfies 

λ⋆(t) = ∇xV (t, x ⋆(t)) . (6.68) Thus the costate is the local slope of the cost-to-go sampled along one optimal trajectory. It is a shadow price: a large component means that a small state error in that direction greatly changes the best achievable future cost. For a robot near a narrow passage, the costate of lateral position may be large because a small lateral error strongly affects future feasibility. Pontryagin’s principle computes this gradient along the chosen extremal without first constructing the entire function 

V (t, x ). This distinction is computationally important. Solving the state–costate boundary-value problem for one initial state produces one optimal trajectory and the values of ∇xV encountered along it. Computing the value function produces information for many possible initial states and can be used to define feedback away from the nominal trajectory, but it is much more expensive in high-dimensional state spaces. Endpoint restrictions determine how this backward sensitivity is initialized. For the free-terminal-state problem, equation (6.11) gives ∇xV (tf , x ) = Φ x(x, t f ), consistent with the terminal condition from Pontryagin’s minimum principle, λ(tf ) = Φ x.The word transversality refers to the boundary conditions obtained by requiring the endpoint part of the first variation to vanish. After imposing the costate equation, the terminal-state contribution has the form (Φx − λ(tf ))Tz(tf ), (6.69) where z(tf ) = δx (tf ) is the allowed first-order displacement of the terminal state. The condition on λ(tf ) depends on which terminal displacements are permitted. • Free terminal state. If there is no constraint on x(tf ), then z(tf ) may point in any direction. For equation (6.69) to vanish for every possible z(tf ), its coefficient must be zero. Therefore 

λ(tf ) = Φ x(x(tf ), t f ). (6.70) This is the standard terminal condition used when the terminal state is completely free. • Fixed terminal state. If x(tf ) = xf is prescribed, every admissible perturbation must satisfy 

z(tf ) = 0. Hence equation (6.69) vanishes automatically, regardless of λ(tf ), and the variational argument supplies no terminal value for the costate. This does not mean that λ(tf ) is irrelevant. Its unknown value, or equivalently the unknown initial value λ(t0), must be selected so that 128 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control 

integration of the coupled state–costate equations produces the required endpoint x(tf ) = xf .• Terminal state constrained to a manifold. Suppose ψ(x(tf )) = 0, where ψ : Rn → Rr. Linearizing this constraint shows that an admissible endpoint displacement must satisfy 

ψx(x(tf )) z(tf ) = 0 . (6.71) Thus z(tf ) may move tangent to the constraint manifold but not normal to it. For equation (6.69) to vanish for every such tangent displacement, the vector λ(tf ) − Φx must lie in the normal space spanned by the rows of ψx. Therefore, for some multiplier ν ∈ Rr,

λ(tf ) = Φ x + ψT 

> x

ν. (6.72) The multiplier ν measures the first-order cost of enforcing the terminal constraint. A fully fixed terminal state is the limiting case in which there are enough independent endpoint constraints to eliminate every terminal-state variation. 

Free final time. The preceding cases concern which terminal states are allowed at a fixed terminal time. A separate variation is needed when the optimizer may also choose the terminal time. Suppose first that the terminal state is free and that there is no additional terminal-time constraint. Perturb the endpoint from tf to tf + δt f .There are two contributions to the displacement of the terminal state. The trajectory itself may vary by z(tf ) at the original time tf , and the shift in time moves the state along its dynamics for an additional δt f . To first order, the actual terminal-state displacement is 

δx f = z(tf ) + ˙ x(tf )δt f = z(tf ) + f (tf , x (tf ), u (tf )) δt f . (6.73) Therefore the terminal cost changes by 

δΦ = Φ T

> [

Substituting this condition into the coefficient of δt f gives 

ℓ(tf ) + λ(tf )Tf (tf ) + Φ tf = H(tf ) + Φ tf . (6.79) The terminal time is free, so small positive and negative values of δt f are admissible. The coefficient must therefore vanish: 

H(tf ) + Φ tf (x(tf ), t f ) = 0 . (6.80) When Φ has no explicit time dependence, Φ tf = 0, and the condition reduces to H(tf ) = 0. Intuitively, at the optimal stopping time, the first-order cost of continuing for a little longer must be zero; otherwise, moving the endpoint slightly earlier or later would improve the objective. If the terminal state must lie on a constraint manifold, or if the terminal time itself is constrained, then z(tf ) and δt f are no longer independent. The corresponding endpoint multipliers must be included before the coefficients are set to zero. 

# 6.3 Worked Robotics and Control Problems 

The examples in this section use the same sequence repeatedly: 1. write the dynamics and performance objective; 2. form the Hamiltonian; 3. derive the state, costate, and pointwise control conditions; 4. use the endpoint conditions to determine the unknown constants; and 5. interpret the resulting control physically. The algebra is useful, but the larger purpose is to see how the form of the Hamiltonian predicts the qualitative structure of the solution. A quadratic control cost tends to produce a smooth interior control, whereas a Hamiltonian that is affine in a bounded control tends to produce saturation and switching. 

6.3.1 Minimum-Energy Motion of a Double Integrator 

Consider one translational coordinate of a robot with acceleration control: ˙q = v, ˙v = u. (6.81) This model describes a point mass after its mass has been absorbed into the control variable, or a single robot coordinate when acceleration can be commanded directly. The position q cannot jump, and the velocity v can change only through the acceleration u.Suppose the duration T is prescribed and both position and velocity are specified at the two endpoints. Among all feasible motions, we seek the one with minimum squared acceleration: minimize 

> u

12 

> ∫T
> 0

u(t)2 dt subject to q(0) = q0, v(0) = v0,q(T ) = qf , v(T ) = vf . (6.82) 130 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control 

The factor 1 /2 simplifies derivatives. The integral penalizes large accelerations throughout the motion, so it favors a smooth distribution of effort rather than a short, violent maneuver. Because the final state is fixed, the terminal costate is not prescribed; its unknown value must be chosen so that the state reaches ( qf , v f ). Boundary conditions. Both components of the state are fixed at the initial and terminal times:

The unknown costate data must instead be chosen so that the state trajectory satisfies the two terminal conditions. The equation ˙λq = 0 makes λq constant. It follows from ˙λv = −λq that λv, and hence u⋆ = −λv,is affine in time. Integrating the state equations then shows that the optimal velocity is quadratic and the optimal position is cubic. The formulas apply componentwise to a point robot in Rd. The optimization is convex: the dynamics and endpoint constraints are linear, and the control cost is strictly convex. Therefore the stationary trajectory derived above is the unique global minimizer. Cubic interpolation is common in robotics because it matches position and velocity while minimizing squared acceleration under this model. 

6.3.2 Minimum-Time Motion and Bang–Bang Control 

The previous example fixed the duration and minimized effort. We now reverse the priorities: effort is not charged directly, and the objective is to arrive as quickly as possible. A control bound is essential. Without it, the model could request an arbitrarily large acceleration and drive the 132 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control

Because the terminal state is fixed, no terminal values are prescribed for λq(T ) or λv(T ). Their unknown values must make the state reach ( qf , v f ). 

Free-final-time condition. The final time is free and there is no terminal cost. Therefore the additional transversality condition is 

H(T ) = 0 . (6.110) The equation ˙λq = 0 makes λq constant, and ˙λv = −λq then makes λv affine in time. The function 

λv(t) is called the switching function . Because it is affine, it crosses zero at most once unless it vanishes identically. Thus an ordinary extremal has at most one switch: use one extreme acceleration before the zero and the opposite extreme afterward. At an isolated switching time ts, one has λv(ts) = 0, so the control-dependent term λv(ts)u

vanishes. Every value u(ts) ∈ [−umax , u max ] therefore minimizes the Hamiltonian at that single instant. This apparent ambiguity has no effect on the motion: changing a bounded control at only one time does not change its time integral and hence does not change the resulting state trajectory. The signs of λv immediately before and after ts determine the two control values on the neighboring intervals. A different situation would arise if λv(t) = 0 throughout a nonzero time interval. Then first-order Hamiltonian minimization would leave the control undetermined on an entire interval. Such an interval is called a singular arc , and additional differentiation of the switching function is generally needed to determine the control. Singular arcs cannot occur in this example. Because λv is affine, vanishing on an interval would force λv ≡ 0 and λq ≡ 0 everywhere. The Hamiltonian would then equal H = 1, contradicting the free-final-time condition H(T ) = 0. Consider rest-to-rest travel from ( q, v ) = (0 , 0) to ( d, 0) with d > 0. The robot must first accelerate in the positive direction and then brake:

The free-final-time condition fixes this remaining scale. 

Determining the costate. At the terminal time, v(T ) = 0 and u(T ) = −umax . Hence 0 = H(T ) = 1 + λq(T )v(T ) + λv(T )u(T ) = 1 − λv(T )umax , (6.116) so

Meaning of the costate. The costate measures the first-order sensitivity of the optimal remaining travel time to the current state. Under the usual smoothness conditions, 

λq = ∂V ∂q , λv = ∂V ∂v , (6.119) where V (q, v ) is the minimum time still required to reach ( d, 0). Here λq < 0: moving the robot slightly farther toward the target decreases the remaining travel time. Before the switch, λv < 0, so a small increase in forward velocity also decreases the remaining time. After the switch, λv > 0: extra forward velocity increases the remaining time because the robot must spend longer braking. At the switching time, λv = 0, so the first-order value of an additional velocity perturbation changes sign. The contrast with the preceding example is fundamental. A quadratic effort term makes the Hamiltonian strictly convex in u and produces a smooth interior minimizer. A time objective makes the Hamiltonian affine in u, so the fastest solution uses all available actuator authority. Bang–bang behavior is therefore a consequence of the model and objective, not a numerical artifact. Additional speed, position, or comfort constraints can introduce further arcs on which those constraints are active. Section 6.3 135

6.3.4 Torque-Optimal Motion of a Manipulator 

The double integrator hides the configuration dependence and coupling present in a real robot. Return to the manipulator dynamics in equation (6.3). Consider the running cost 

ℓ(q, v, u ) = 12 (q − qd)TQ(q − qd) + 12 vTSv + 12 uTRu, R ≻ 0. (6.135) The three terms have distinct roles. The matrix Q ⪰ 0 penalizes configuration error, S ⪰ 0 penalizes velocity, and R ≻ 0 penalizes joint torque. Large entries of Q demand accurate positioning; large entries of R make the optimizer reluctant to use the corresponding actuators. A terminal cost or a fixed terminal state must also be specified according to the task, although the pointwise control calculation below is the same. Recall that the state and dynamics are

M (q)−1u. (6.143) The first is the immediate effort cost. The second measures how the torque changes acceleration and therefore future cost. For unconstrained torque, the pointwise stationarity condition is 0 = Hu = Ru + M (q)−Tλv, (6.144) and hence 

u⋆ = −R−1M (q)−Tλv. (6.145) Because the robot mass matrix is symmetric, M −T = M −1. Nevertheless, the transpose notation shows how the derivative of M −1u maps a velocity costate into actuator coordinates. 

Boundary conditions. The initial state is prescribed: 

q(t0) = q0, v(t0) = v0. (6.146) The terminal conditions depend on the task specification. If the terminal state is fixed, then 

q(tf ) = qf , v(tf ) = vf , (6.147) 138 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control 

and no terminal values are prescribed for λq(tf ) or λv(tf ); their unknown values must make the state reach the specified endpoint. If the terminal state is free and the problem includes a terminal cost Φ( q(tf ), v (tf ), t f ), then 

λq(tf ) = Φ q, λv(tf ) = Φ v. (6.148) In particular, a free terminal state with no terminal cost gives λq(tf ) = λv(tf ) = 0. If the final time is also free, the additional condition is 

H(tf ) + Φ tf = 0 . (6.149) The state equations, costate equations, control condition, and applicable boundary conditions together form a nonlinear two-point boundary-value problem. The control formula has a simple interpretation. If a component of M −Tλv is large and positive, increasing the corresponding torque greatly increases future cost, so the minimizing torque acts in the negative direction. The matrix R−1 scales this response: an actuator assigned a large effort penalty is used less aggressively. It is tempting to read equation (6.145) as a feedback law, but λv is not a measured robot state. It depends on the future objective and endpoint conditions and is known only after solving the full optimal-control problem. Solving the two-point boundary-value problem for one initial condition produces three linked time histories: 

x⋆(t), λ⋆(t), u⋆(t) = −R−1M (q⋆(t)) −Tλ⋆v(t). (6.150) This is an open-loop plan for the particular initial condition used in the optimization. For example, suppose an external force changes the robot’s configuration or velocity at some time td. The stored costate λ⋆v(td) belongs to the nominal state x⋆(td), not to the disturbed state. Continuing to apply the precomputed torque therefore does not specify how the robot should optimally recover. A feedback controller needs the costate, or equivalently the optimal control, as a function of the state near or away from the nominal trajectory. If a differentiable value function is known, then along an optimal trajectory 

λ(t) = Vx(t, x (t)) , (6.151) so the velocity component Vv gives the feedback law 

u⋆(t, x ) = −R−1M (q)−TVv(t, x ). (6.152) This example illustrates the typical role of PMP in nonlinear robot control. The state and costate equations, the Hamiltonian-minimizing torque, and the endpoint conditions must be solved together; the torque formula alone is not a complete solution. Quadratic effort produces an explicit pointwise control condition, but the required costate still carries global information about the future objective. Solving for one initial condition therefore gives an optimal open-loop motion, whereas constructing feedback requires a value function, a local approximation such as a Riccati method, or repeated online replanning. In contrast, λθ evolves because changing the heading rotates the direction in which the translational control acts.

# 6.4 Numerical Methods 

Closed-form solutions are exceptional. For realistic nonlinear systems, the PMP produces coupled state–costate boundary-value problems that must be solved numerically. This section first dis-tinguishes direct and indirect methods, then focuses on numerical methods for solving the PMP equations. 

6.4.1 Two Numerical Viewpoints 

An indirect method first derives the PMP conditions and then solves the state equation, costate equation, Hamiltonian minimization condition, and boundary conditions. The numerical unknowns may include costate boundary values, switching times, and endpoint multipliers. The result is an 

extremal : it satisfies necessary conditions but is not automatically a minimum. A direct method instead discretizes the original state and control trajectories, objective, and con-straints before optimization. It produces a finite-dimensional nonlinear program without explicitly deriving the costate equations. Direct methods, including transcription and direct collocation, are developed in Chapter 9 on discrete-time trajectory optimization. Here we concentrate on indirect methods. 

6.4.2 Single Shooting 

In single shooting , Hamiltonian minimization gives u = μ(t, x, λ ) and the unknown initial costate 

λ0 is guessed. One integrates ˙x = f (t, x, μ (t, x, λ )) , (6.172) ˙λ = −Hx(t, x, μ (t, x, λ ), λ ). (6.173) 142 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control 

For fixed endpoints, the shooting residual is 

F (λ0) = x(tf ; λ0) − xf . (6.174) Root finding adjusts λ0 until F = 0. For free terminal states, endpoint constraints, or free final time, the residual is enlarged with the corresponding transversality conditions. A typical iteration therefore consists of the following steps: 1. guesses the unknown initial costate and other boundary variables; 2. integrates the state–costate equations to tf ;3. evaluates the terminal residual; and 4. updates the guess using Newton, quasi-Newton, or another root solver. Single shooting has few numerical unknowns and can be highly accurate. However, a small error in λ0 may produce a large terminal error on a long or unstable trajectory. Control switching can also make the residual nonsmooth. 

6.4.3 Multiple Shooting 

Multiple shooting partitions the interval, 

t0 = τ0 < τ 1 < · · · < τ N = tf , (6.175) and assigns an independent initial value yj (τj ), with y = ( x, λ ), to each segment j = 0 , . . . , N − 1. The PMP equations are integrated separately on every segment, and continuity is enforced through 

yj (τ −

> j+1

) − yj+1 (τ +

> j+1

) = 0 , j = 0 , . . . , N − 2. (6.176) The endpoint conditions are imposed together with these matching equations. Short integrations reduce sensitivity and make multiple shooting more robust than single shooting, at the cost of additional variables and continuity constraints. 

6.4.4 Indirect Collocation 

Collocation avoids repeated integration from one boundary guess. The state–costate trajectory is approximated by piecewise polynomials on a time mesh. At selected collocation points tk,i , one enforces ˙yh(tk,i ) = G(tk,i , y h(tk,i )), y =

> [

xλ

> ]

, (6.177) where G contains the PMP state and costate equations after Hamiltonian minimization. Boundary and continuity conditions are imposed simultaneously. The polynomial coefficients or nodal values are then found by solving a nonlinear algebraic system. This remains an indirect method because PMP is derived before discretization. By contrast, direct collocation discretizes the original dynamics, objective, and constraints before deriving optimality conditions. Indirect collocation is often less sensitive than shooting, but it introduces Section 6.4 143 

more unknowns and requires every PMP condition to be encoded correctly. 

6.4.5 Infinite-Horizon Indirect Methods 

Infinite-horizon PMP retains the interior equations but replaces a finite terminal condition with asymptotic behavior. A common approximation truncates the horizon at a large T and imposes a terminal state, terminal cost, or stable state–costate relation near the desired equilibrium. The early trajectory should become insensitive to further increases in T .Local Riccati solutions often supply the terminal relation. For discounted problems, the current-value costate satisfies ˙λ = ρλ − Hc

> x

. (6.178) Discounting reduces the influence of distant costs but does not eliminate the need for a suitable tail condition. Infinite-horizon shooting is especially sensitive because an incorrect costate can excite unstable modes. 

6.4.6 Other Indirect Techniques 

Several auxiliary techniques improve the reliability of shooting and collocation. In continuation 

or homotopy , one first solves an easier nearby problem and then gradually changes the horizon, boundary data, cost weights, or constraints toward the desired problem, using each solution to initialize the next. For a bang–bang problem with a known switching structure, the switching times can instead be treated as unknowns and determined from the endpoint and switching conditions. Standard boundary-value and finite-difference solvers provide another option by discretizing the PMP equations globally and solving the resulting nonlinear equations. These approaches remain indirect because the optimality conditions are derived before discretization or parameterization. 

6.4.7 Direct Methods: Outlook 

Direct methods optimize a discretized version of the original control problem rather than the PMP equations. Common choices include control parameterization, direct multiple shooting, and direct collocation. They usually introduce more decision variables than indirect methods, but handle control bounds, obstacles, and other path constraints more naturally. Their detailed treatment is deferred to Chapter 9. For infinite-horizon problems, direct methods still require truncation at a large time T together with a terminal cost or terminal set that approximates the omitted tail. Without such a tail model, the optimizer may postpone corrective action beyond the artificial endpoint. 

6.4.8 Choosing and Checking an Indirect Method 

Single shooting is attractive when the number of unknown boundary values is small and the dynamics are not too sensitive. Multiple shooting is usually more robust on long or unstable 144 Chapter 6. Continuous-Time Optimal Control 

trajectories, while collocation is useful when the whole state–costate curve must be resolved simultaneously. Continuation can provide the initial guesses required by any of these methods. None of them guarantees that a computed extremal is a minimum: the PMP supplies necessary conditions, and a nonlinear problem may have several extremals. An indirect solution should therefore be checked by: • verifying the state and costate equations and all boundary conditions; • checking pointwise Hamiltonian minimization and active constraints; • refining the integration tolerance or collocation mesh; • varying the initial guess and, for infinite horizons, the truncation time; and • comparing the costs of distinct extremals or alternative numerical formulations. Agreement across these tests is stronger evidence than one successful solver run. 

Exercises. Exercise 6.4. Explain in words the difference between J[u] and the cost-to-go function V (t, x ). What boundary condition does V satisfy at t = tf ?

Short solution. J[u] evaluates one specified control signal from a specified initial condition. The value V (t, x ) is the minimum future cost over all admissible controls starting from each time–state pair ( t, x ). At the terminal time, V (tf , x ) = Φ( x, t f ). 

Exercise 6.5. For an autonomous, time-invariant problem, contrast the typical form of a finite-horizon optimal feedback law with that of an infinite-horizon law. Does a finite horizon necessarily mean open-loop control? 

Short solution. The finite-horizon law generally has the form u = μ⋆(t, x ) because the remaining time matters. A stationary infinite-horizon law has the form u = μ⋆

> ∞

(x). Finite horizon does not imply open loop: its value function also defines a time-varying state-feedback law. 

Exercise 6.6. Classify each of the following algorithms as direct or indirect: (i) derive the PMP equations and shoot on the initial costate; (ii) represent u by piecewise-constant samples and optimize those samples; and (iii) impose collocation equations on the state and costate equations obtained from PMP. 

Short solution. Case (i) is indirect because it solves derived necessary conditions. Case (ii) is direct because it parameterizes the original control problem before optimization. Case (iii) is indirect because the PMP equations were derived before they were discretized. 

Exercise 6.7. Why does a truncated infinite-horizon direct method benefit from a terminal cost approximating V∞(x(T ))? 

Short solution. The terminal cost approximates the cost omitted after the artificial endpoint T .Without it, the optimizer may postpone state regulation or other costly actions until just beyond the truncated horizon. Section 6.4 145

Exercise 6.11. Suppose H = c(t, x, λ )u + d(t, x, λ ) and u ∈ [−umax , u max ]. Minimize H with respect to u.

Short solution. Choose u = −umax if c > 0 and u = umax if c < 0. If c = 0, every admissible control minimizes the instantaneous Hamiltonian and further analysis is needed. The quantity c is the switching function.

Exercise 6.16. Explain why a trajectory satisfying the state and costate differential equations may still fail Pontryagin’s minimum principle. 

Short solution. It may violate endpoint conditions, feasibility constraints, or pointwise Hamiltonian minimization. Every part of the principle is required. 

Exercise 6.17. For fixed terminal state and x ∈ Rn, what is the dimension of the basic shooting residual F (λ0)? 

Short solution. The unknown initial costate and terminal-state residual both have dimension n,so F : Rn → Rn.

Chapter summary. Continuous-time optimal control chooses a control function whose state trajectory satisfies a differential equation. The cost-to-go function gives the best achievable future cost from every time–state pair and, when solved over a state domain, defines an optimal feedback law. Finite-horizon value functions generally depend on time and include a terminal condition; autonomous infinite-horizon problems often seek stationary value functions and feedback laws for continuing regulation. An optimal trajectory for one initial state is not the same object as a feedback controller defined for many states. A costate multiplier and integration by parts yield the Hamiltonian system: the state evolves forward, the costate carries the gradient of future cost backward, and the control minimizes the Hamiltonian pointwise. Endpoint freedom produces transversality conditions. Quadratic effort often gives smooth controls, whereas bounded controls in an affine Hamiltonian produce bang–bang solutions. These necessary conditions can be solved by indirect methods such as shooting or collocation of the state–costate equations. Direct methods instead parameterize or transcribe the original control problem into a finite-dimensional optimization problem. Both families can be applied to finite horizons; infinite horizons require truncation, asymptotic conditions, terminal-value approximations, or stationary structure. Section 6.4 147 

These ideas underlie the Riccati, dynamic-programming, trajectory-optimization, model-predictive-control, and policy-optimization methods developed later. 7

# Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

Chapter map. Finite- and infinite-horizon linear–quadratic regulation, Riccati equations, optimal state feedback, reference tracking, connections to PD, computed-torque, and PID control, and an outlook on estimation and LQG control. 

Chapter 6 developed necessary conditions for general continuous-time optimal control problems. Those conditions are powerful, but they usually lead to a nonlinear two-point boundary-value problem that must be solved numerically. This chapter studies an important exception: linear dynamics with a quadratic cost and unconstrained control. The resulting problem is called the 

linear–quadratic regulator , or LQR. Here regulation means driving the state toward a desired equilibrium and keeping it there. LQR is special for three related reasons. First, the state and costate equations are linear once the optimal control is substituted. Second, the cost-to-go is quadratic in the state. Third, the optimal control is a linear state-feedback law. All future consequences of the current state are summarized by a symmetric matrix P (t) that satisfies a Riccati differential equation. Instead of solving a new nonlinear optimization problem for every initial condition, one integrates this matrix equation once and obtains a feedback controller for every state. The linear–quadratic model is more than a classroom example. Here a nominal configuration or trajectory means the planned motion about which local deviations will be measured. Near such a robot configuration or trajectory, nonlinear dynamics can be approximated by linear dynamics, and a smooth objective can be approximated by a quadratic cost. LQR then supplies local feedback gains that correct perturbations around the nominal motion. The same structure also appears inside trajectory optimization, model predictive control, state estimation, and modern policy optimization methods. In particular, Chapter 9 studies iterative LQR (iLQR), a nonlinear trajectory-optimization method that repeatedly constructs and solves local linear–quadratic approximations. Standard references include Kirk [2004], Lewis et al. [2012], Bertsekas [2017]. 148 Section 7.1 149 

# 7.1 Finite-Horizon LQR 

7.1.1 Problem Formulation 

Consider the linear time-varying system ˙x(t) = A(t)x(t) + B(t)u(t), x(t0) = x0, (7.1) where x(t) ∈ Rn, u(t) ∈ Rm, A(t) ∈ Rn×n, and B(t) ∈ Rn×m. The system is linear because the right-hand side is linear in both the state and the control. If A and B are constant, the system is linear time-invariant. Allowing them to depend on time also covers local models constructed along a changing reference trajectory. For example, linearizing nonlinear dynamics ˙ z = f (z, v ) around a feasible nominal trajectory (¯ z(t), ¯v(t)) gives, to first order, 

δ ˙z ≈ A(t)δz + B(t)δv, A(t) = fz

> (

¯z(t), ¯v(t)), B(t) = fv

> (

¯z(t), ¯v(t)). (7.2) The Jacobian matrices change as the nominal state and input move through different operating conditions. In that setting, the variables x and u in equation (7.1) can be interpreted as the deviations δz and δv , and the resulting time-varying LQR gain corrects the system back toward the nominal trajectory. A more detailed derivation is given later in this chapter. The finite-horizon LQR objective is 

J[u] = 12 x(tf )TSf x(tf ) + 12 

> ∫tf
> t0

x(t)TQ(t)x(t) + u(t)TR(t)u(t)

Q(t) ⪰ 0, R(t) ≻ 0, Sf ⪰ 0. (7.4) The state penalty xTQx measures deviation from the desired equilibrium x = 0, the control penalty uTRu measures effort, and the terminal penalty x(tf )TSf x(tf ) measures the final error. The matrices are taken to be symmetric. Positive semidefiniteness of Q and Sf makes state error nonnegative, while positive definiteness of R makes every nonzero control input costly. The entries of these matrices express design priorities. A large weight on a position coordinate demands accurate positioning. A large control weight makes the controller reluctant to use the corresponding actuator. These comparisons must respect physical units: a numerical weight on meters cannot be interpreted in the same way as the same numerical weight on radians or newton-meters. State and input scaling should therefore be chosen deliberately. The desired state is written as zero only to simplify notation. Tracking a nonzero equilibrium or trajectory will be reduced to this regulation problem later in the chapter. For now, the complete problem is minimize

If an actuator saturates, the unconstrained LQR law can still be useful locally, but it is no longer the exact solution of the constrained problem. We begin with the PMP conditions from Chapter 6. The Hamiltonian is 

H(t, x, u, λ ) = 12 xTQx + 12 uTRu + λT(Ax + Bu ). (7.6) The state and costate equations are ˙x = Ax + Bu, (7.7) ˙λ = −Qx − ATλ, (7.8) and the free terminal state gives the terminal condition

u⋆(t) = −R(t)−1B(t)Tλ(t). (7.11) As in many examples from Chapter 6, the minimizing control is initially expressed as a function of the costate. The central step in LQR is to express that costate directly in terms of the current state. 

7.1.2 Quadratic Value Functions and the Riccati Equation 

The terminal condition in equation (7.9) is linear in x(tf ). The state–costate equations are also linear after substituting equation (7.11). Moreover, the value function is meant to describe the optimal cost from every possible state, not only along one trajectory. To see why a linear state–costate relation is natural, substitute the minimizing control into the state equation and collect the state and costate equations as

Because the optimal cost is nonnegative for every state, xTP (t)x ≥ 0 for every x. Thus the Riccati solution associated with this value function is positive semidefinite. This result clarifies all three central LQR objects. The matrix P (t) is the curvature of the quadratic cost-to-go. Along an optimal trajectory, the costate is its state gradient, 

∇xV (t, x ) = P (t)x = λ(t), (7.27) and the feedback law applies control in response to that gradient. A state direction with large curvature in P is expensive because even a small displacement in that direction produces a large increase in future cost. The symmetry of P is consistent with its role in a quadratic form. More formally, transpose the Riccati equation. When Q, R, and Sf are symmetric, P T satisfies the same differential equation and terminal condition as P . Uniqueness of the matrix differential equation therefore gives P T = P .In numerical work one should nevertheless check that roundoff has not produced a significant asymmetric component.

7.1.3 Optimal Linear Feedback 

The optimal law equation (7.20) is a feedback law, not merely an open-loop control sequence. At every time it observes the current state and recomputes the control from that state. If a disturbance moves the system away from its predicted trajectory, the control changes immediately according to the same gain matrix. The original dynamics ˙ x = Ax + Bu are called open loop when the input u is regarded as an externally specified signal. Under LQR, the input is instead determined from the measured state 154 Chapter 7. Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

by u = −K(t)x. Substitution eliminates u from the state equation: ˙x = Ax + B(−K(t)x)

= ( A − BK (t)) x. (7.37) The resulting equation is called the closed-loop system because the state affects the control and the control feeds back into the state dynamics. Once x(t0) = x0 is given, this differential equation determines the state trajectory and the feedback law then determines the corresponding control trajectory. The term −BK (t) is the controller’s modification of the uncontrolled dynamics A.Thus the matrix A − BK (t) describes the dynamics created jointly by the physical system, often called the plant , and the controller. If a disturbance changes the state, the law u = −K(t)x

automatically changes the input, thereby closing the correction loop. For a finite horizon, K(t) generally varies with time even when A, B, Q, and R are constant. The remaining time and terminal penalty affect how aggressively the controller should act. Consequently, the finite-horizon closed-loop matrix A − BK (t) is generally time varying as well. This finite-horizon result does not by itself imply asymptotic stability. Asymptotic stability asks whether x(t) → 0 as t → ∞ , whereas the controller above is designed only on the finite interval [ t0, t f ]. It minimizes the accumulated and terminal costs on that interval, but it need not drive the state exactly to zero at tf , and the control law has not yet been specified beyond 

tf . The infinite-horizon LQR problem studied later selects a stabilizing Riccati solution; under the stated stabilizability and detectability conditions, its constant closed-loop matrix A − BK is asymptotically stable.

Thus LQR automatically produces both position and velocity feedback. The position term drives the system toward the target, while the velocity term provides braking and damping. These roles emerge from the optimization; they do not have to be imposed as separate controller-design rules. The same finite-horizon construction can also stabilize a nonlinear robot locally. The idea is to linearize the dynamics along a feasible nominal trajectory and apply LQR to the resulting perturbation dynamics. This extension is developed after the exact linear reference-tracking construction in section 7.2.3. The weight matrices influence both performance and robustness, meaning the ability to tolerate small disturbances and modeling errors. Increasing Q relative to R generally produces faster correction and larger control effort. Increasing R produces gentler control and slower convergence. A large terminal weight makes final accuracy important, especially near tf . These are tendencies rather than independent tuning rules: coupled dynamics mean that changing one entry can affect several state and input coordinates. The discrete-time counterpart is developed from the Bellman recursion in sections 8.1.3 and 8.2.4. There the same quadratic-value structure produces the discrete Riccati recursion, its optimal feedback gain, and the infinite-horizon DARE. 

# 7.2 Infinite-Horizon and Tracking Problems 

7.2.1 The Algebraic Riccati Equation 

For a time-invariant system ˙x = Ax + Bu, (7.45) consider the infinite-horizon objective 

J[u] = 12 

> ∫∞
> 0
> (

x(t)TQx (t) + u(t)TRu (t)

> )

dt, Q ⪰ 0, R ≻ 0. (7.46) There is no terminal time, and the problem data do not depend on time. It is therefore natural to seek a stationary quadratic value function 

V∞(x) = 12 xTP x (7.47) with a constant matrix P . Setting ˙P = 0 in the differential Riccati equation gives 

ATP + P A − P BR −1BTP + Q = 0 . (7.48) This is the continuous-time algebraic Riccati equation , abbreviated CARE. A suitable solution gives the stationary gain 

K = R−1BTP (7.49) 156 Chapter 7. Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

and feedback law 

u⋆ = −Kx. (7.50) An algebraic equation may have several symmetric solutions, and not every solution represents the infinite-horizon optimum. The desired solution must be positive semidefinite and must make the closed-loop matrix 

Acl = A − BK (7.51) stable, meaning that every unforced closed-loop trajectory approaches zero. Such a solution is called the stabilizing solution of the CARE. Two structural properties explain when a stabilizing solution should exist. To interpret them, recall that an eigenvalue μ of A describes a natural system motion whose magnitude behaves like 

eRe( μ)t. The motion decays when Re( μ) < 0 and fails to decay when Re( μ) ≥ 0. The pair ( A, B ) is called stabilizable if there is some feedback matrix K for which every eigenvalue of A − BK has negative real part. Equivalently in physical terms, every motion that does not already decay must be accessible through the input channels in B so that feedback can stabilize it. Stabilizability is weaker than complete controllability: a mode may be unaffected by the actuators as long as that mode is already stable and therefore decays without control. For example, consider 

A =

> [

1 00 −1

> ]

, B =

> [

01

> ]

. (7.52) The first state grows as et but is not affected by the input, so no feedback can stabilize the system. The pair ( A, B ) is not stabilizable. If the cost penalizes this first state, its infinite-horizon cost is necessarily infinite for every initial condition with a nonzero first component. The second property concerns what the state cost can see. Every symmetric positive semidefinite matrix can be factored as 

Q = CT 

> q

Cq. (7.53) For example, one may choose the symmetric matrix square root Cq = Q1/2, which satisfies 

Q1/2Q1/2 = Q. Then 

xTQx = ∥Cqx∥22. (7.54) Thus Cqx collects the state combinations penalized by the objective. A state motion is invisible to the state cost when Cqx(t) = 0 for all time. The pair ( Cq, A ) is called detectable if every such invisible motion is already stable. In other words, every motion that fails to decay must eventually produce a nonzero state penalty. Detectability does not require every stable motion to be penalized; an unpenalized mode is harmless to the stability conclusion if it naturally decays. Detectability matters because an optimizer has no reason to suppress a motion that contributes no state cost. For example, let 

A =

> [

1 00 −1

> ]

, B = I, Q =

> [

0 00 1

> ]

. (7.55) The system is stabilizable because both states can be controlled, but the growing first state is Section 7.2 157 

invisible to Q. Starting from x(0) = [1 0] T, the choice u = 0 has zero cost even though x1(t) = et

diverges. Hence minimizing the stated objective does not force a stabilizing controller. If Q ≻ 0, by contrast, every nonzero state direction is penalized and detectability is automatic. Under R ≻ 0, stabilizability of ( A, B ), and detectability of ( Cq, A ), the CARE has a unique symmetric positive semidefinite stabilizing solution P . The gain K = R−1BTP then makes every eigenvalue of A − BK have negative real part, so the closed-loop state approaches zero. These conditions allow stable uncontrollable or unpenalized motions, but exclude precisely the nondecaying motions that the actuators cannot control or the cost cannot see. This chapter uses the conditions as interpretation rather than developing their full linear-systems theory.

One numerical route to the stabilizing CARE solution is to solve the finite-horizon Riccati equation backward with increasingly distant terminal times. Away from the terminal boundary, P (t) often approaches a constant matrix. This illustrates why the infinite-horizon gain is stationary: when the terminal time is sufficiently far away, shifting the clock does not change the remaining optimization problem.

c1∥x∥22 ≤ W (x) ≤ c2∥x∥22, ˙W (x) ≤ − c3∥x∥22. (7.62) Consequently, ˙W ≤ − c3

A scalar function that is positive away from an equilibrium and decreases along every nonzero trajectory is called a Lyapunov function . It plays a role similar to physical energy: if the stored quantity must decrease and can vanish only at the target, the state cannot remain away from the target. Under the conditions above, the optimal cost-to-go itself serves as a Lyapunov function. This is a deep connection between optimization and stability: the same matrix P measures future cost, determines the feedback gain, and certifies closed-loop behavior. For the optimal closed-loop trajectory, integrating equation (7.61) from 0 to a finite time T gives

Some state directions may have no direct state penalty. They can still be regulated because they influence penalized directions or require control effort. Detectability rules out an unstable direction that is completely invisible to the cost. Without such a condition, a controller could ignore an unstable motion because the objective never assigns it a cost. The stability conclusion is tied to the model and to unconstrained control. Large modeling Section 7.2 159 

errors, actuator saturation, delays, or neglected dynamics can invalidate the predicted closed-loop behavior. LQR is often robust to modest errors, but the Riccati equation by itself does not certify an arbitrarily uncertain physical system. 

7.2.3 Reference Tracking 

Regulation drives the state toward zero. Tracking drives it toward a nonzero reference. The cleanest case is a constant equilibrium pair ( xr, u r) that satisfies 0 = Ax r + Bu r. (7.67) Define the state and input errors 

e = x − xr, v = u − ur. (7.68) Because the reference satisfies the dynamics, ˙e = ˙ x = A(e + xr) + B(v + ur)= Ae + Bv + ( Ax r + Bu r) = Ae + Bv. (7.69) The tracking objective is now written as a regulation objective in the error coordinates. For example, the infinite-horizon cost becomes 

Jtrack = 12 

> ∫∞
> 0
> (

eTQe + vTRv 

> )

dt. (7.70) This cost penalizes deviation from the desired state and deviation from the input required to hold that state. Because the error dynamics ˙ e = Ae + Bv have exactly the same form as the original regulation dynamics, the same CARE and feedback gain give 

v⋆ = −Ke. (7.71) Returning to the original variables using v = u − ur and e = x − xr gives 

u − ur = −K(x − xr) =⇒ u = ur − K(x − xr). (7.72) When x = xr, the feedback term is zero and the controller applies exactly ur; the equilibrium condition then gives ˙ x = Ax r + Bu r = 0. When the state is displaced, the term −K(x − xr)supplies a corrective input. Indeed, substituting the law into the error dynamics gives ˙e = ( A − BK )e. (7.73) Therefore, if K is the stabilizing infinite-horizon LQR gain, then e(t) → 0 and hence x(t) → xr.The two terms in the controller have different jobs. The feedforward input ur makes the desired state dynamically sustainable, while the feedback term corrects deviations caused by initial error or disturbances. If ur is omitted, the controller applies zero input at x = xr; unless Ax r = 0, the state immediately moves away from the target and a steady-state tracking error can result. For 160 Chapter 7. Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

example, a robot arm generally needs a nonzero torque to hold a configuration against gravity. Not every desired state is an equilibrium. If no input ur satisfies equation (7.67), then exact constant tracking is incompatible with the model. One must change the reference, use a moving reference that satisfies the dynamics, or accept the best attainable tracking error. Optimization cannot make an infeasible steady state dynamically feasible. The same coordinate change handles a time-varying feasible reference. Suppose ( xr(t), u r(t)) satisfies ˙xr = A(t)xr + B(t)ur. (7.74) Then the errors e = x − xr and v = u − ur satisfy ˙e = A(t)( e + xr) + B(t)( v + ur) − ˙xr

= A(t)e + B(t)v. (7.75) With a quadratic tracking cost 

J = 12 e(tf )TSf e(tf ) + 12 

> ∫tf
> t0
> (

eTQe + vTRv 

> )

dt, (7.76) the optimal law is 

u(t) = ur(t) − K(t)(x(t) − xr(t)). (7.77) This controller combines an open-loop nominal motion with closed-loop error correction. For the linear model, the error dynamics used in its derivation are exact. The same architecture can be extended locally to a nonlinear robot by linearizing its dynamics along the reference trajectory. 

Local tracking for a nonlinear robot. Consider the nonlinear dynamics ˙x = f (t, x, u ) (7.78) and suppose a feasible nominal trajectory (¯ x(t), ¯u(t)) satisfies ˙¯x = f (t, ¯x, ¯u). (7.79) Define the perturbations from this trajectory by 

δx = x − ¯x, δu = u − ¯u. (7.80) Expanding f to first order around the nominal state and input gives 

f (t, ¯x + δx, ¯u + δu ) ≈ f (t, ¯x, ¯u) + fx(t, ¯x, ¯u)δx + fu(t, ¯x, ¯u)δu. (7.81) Here fx and fu are the Jacobian matrices of partial derivatives with respect to the state and control. Subtracting the nominal dynamics and neglecting terms that are quadratic or higher order in the perturbations gives 

δ ˙x ≈ A(t)δx + B(t)δu, A(t) = fx(t, ¯x, ¯u), B(t) = fu(t, ¯x, ¯u). (7.82) Section 7.2 161 

This perturbation model has the same form as the linear tracking-error dynamics above, but it is now only a first-order approximation. After choosing a quadratic penalty on δx and δu ,finite-horizon LQR gives 

δu ⋆ = −K(t)δx, (7.83) or, in the original coordinates, 

u(t) = ¯ u(t) − K(t)(x(t) − ¯x(t)). (7.84) Thus ¯ u(t) generates the nominal motion, while the feedback term corrects deviations from it. This is the nonlinear, locally valid counterpart of equation (7.77). In practice, the nominal pair (¯ x, ¯u) must be found before this feedback law can be constructed. For a simple motion it may be available analytically, but for a nonlinear robot it is often computed by a trajectory optimization method subject to the nonlinear dynamics, endpoint conditions, actuator limits, and collision constraints. The optimization returns a planned state trajectory ¯ x(t) together with a feedforward control trajectory ¯ u(t). Chapter 9 develops such trajectory-optimization methods, including iLQR. One could execute the plan open loop by applying only u(t) = ¯ u(t). This assumes that the robot begins exactly at the planned state and that the model is exact. If a disturbance, estimation error, or modeling error moves the robot away from ¯ x(t), the precomputed input ¯ u(t) does not change and therefore does not actively return the system to the plan. A practical trajectory-tracking controller instead solves the Riccati equation backward for K(t) and applies equation (7.84). On the nominal trajectory the feedback correction is zero, so the controller applies ¯ u(t). Away from the trajectory, the term −K(t)( x(t) − ¯x(t)) changes the input in a direction intended to reduce the deviation. This feedback execution is generally more reliable than open-loop playback because it continually reacts to the measured state. The improvement is local rather than unlimited. The matrices A(t) and B(t) describe only the first-order dynamics near the nominal trajectory. After a large disturbance, neglected nonlinear terms may dominate, the LQR input may exceed actuator limits, or the correction may violate state and obstacle constraints. The feedback law may then recover slowly, converge to an unintended motion, or fail entirely. Large deviations therefore require a controller designed to operate over a larger region, or a new trajectory obtained through replanning or model predictive control. 

7.2.4 PD, Computed-Torque, and PID Control from LQR 

Many familiar feedback laws for mechanical systems can be derived by applying LQR after changing from the physical state and input to tracking-error coordinates. The high-level construction has three design steps, followed by an analysis of the resulting physical controller. First, define an error state e that is zero on the desired motion, and seek an input coordinate ν for which the error dynamics have the linear form ˙e = Aee + Beν. (7.85) 162 Chapter 7. Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

The new variable ν is commonly called a virtual input . It is not an additional physical actuator. It is a convenient input coordinate used during controller design. When the transformation is simply 

u = uff + ν, (7.86) 

uff is the feedforward input required to generate the reference motion, and ν may more specifically be called the input correction . For a nonlinear mechanical system, the map from ν to the physical input u can also contain inverse-dynamics terms that cancel the modeled nonlinearities. Second, choose a quadratic cost on e and ν and solve the resulting LQR problem. This gives a linear law in the transformed coordinates, 

ν⋆ = −Ke. (7.87) Third, substitute this law into the input transformation to recover a controller expressed in the original state and actuator coordinates. For the simple decomposition in equation (7.86), the physical command is 

u = uff − Ke. (7.88) The feedforward term creates the nominal motion, while the feedback term corrects departures from it. The resulting design should then be analyzed in the coordinates in which it was constructed. Under the ideal transformed model, the closed-loop error dynamics are ˙e = ( Ae − BeK)e. (7.89) The eigenvalues of Ae − BeK determine stability, convergence rates, and oscillatory modes in this linear error model. One must also check what was assumed when deriving the transformation: whether the reference is feasible, whether model cancellation is accurate, and whether the mapped physical input respects actuator limits. Thus the LQR interpretation does more than recover the form of a familiar controller. It connects its gains to an explicit tradeoff between tracking error and corrective effort, while making the limits of the derivation visible.

Example 7.5 (Computed-torque control for an n-joint manipulator) . For a nonlinear mechanical system, the LQR-derived PD law can be applied after canceling the modeled dynamics. Consider a fully actuated manipulator with generalized coordinates q ∈ Rn, joint torques τ ∈ Rn, and dynamics

Thus a stable PID loop can reject the bias: the integral state retains the constant value needed to produce the canceling input even after the tracking error has returned to zero. The optimal-control interpretation does not remove the practical limitations of PID. Actuator saturation can cause the integral state to grow while the requested input cannot be delivered, a phenomenon called integral windup; anti-windup logic is then needed. Direct numerical differenti-ation of a noisy position signal can make the derivative term noisy, so velocity estimation or a filtered derivative is normally used. Finally, computed-torque cancellation is only as accurate as the robot model. LQR or LQI determines gains for the model used in the design, while robustness to large uncertainty, hard input limits, and unmodeled dynamics requires additional analysis. 

# 7.3 Noise, State Estimation, and LQG: An Outlook 

The LQR law u = −Kx assumes that the controller knows the current state exactly. This assumption is convenient but rarely literal. A robot may measure joint angles directly while inferring velocities from imperfect sensor data; a mobile robot may receive camera or range measurements rather than its full position and orientation; and unmodeled forces may disturb the motion. The controller must therefore do two jobs: estimate the state from the available measurements and use that estimate to choose the control. This section proceeds in two stages. We first ignore disturbances and sensor noise and derive a deterministic observer whose estimation error converges to zero. We then introduce random process and measurement noise, replace exact error convergence by a statistical objective, and obtain the Kalman–Bucy filter. Combining that filter with LQR leads to LQG control. 

7.3.1 Deterministic State Estimation and Observers 

Begin with the deterministic linear system and sensor model ˙x = Ax + Bu, (7.129) 

y = Cx, (7.130) where y(t) ∈ Rp is the sensor measurement and C ∈ Rp×n maps the state to the quantities reported by the sensors. The equation y = Cx is called the measurement model . For example, a robot may measure joint positions while its velocity components remain unmeasured. The controller therefore knows y, but it may not know the complete state x required by the LQR law u = −Kx .The controller instead maintains a state estimate ˆ x, where the hat indicates an estimated quantity, and applies feedback using the estimate. An observer is a dynamical system that updates ˆ x from the known input u and measured output y. Define the estimation error by 

ex = x − ˆx. (7.131) 168 Chapter 7. Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

We want this error to obey the stable linear dynamics ˙ex = ( A − LC )ex, (7.132) where the observer gain L ∈ Rn×p is chosen so that every eigenvalue of A − LC has negative real part. Because ˙ ex = ˙ x − ˙ˆx, enforcing equation (7.132) gives ˙ˆx = ˙ x − (A − LC )( x − ˆx)= Aˆx + Bu + L(Cx − C ˆx)= Aˆx + Bu + L(y − C ˆx). (7.133) Every quantity on the right-hand side is available to the observer: it uses the current estimate ˆ x,the applied input u, and the measured output y. Thus the desired error dynamics lead directly to an implementable dynamical system for the estimate. The term Aˆx + Bu predicts how the estimate should move according to the model. The predicted sensor output is C ˆx, so 

y − C ˆx (7.134) is the innovation , or the discrepancy between the measured and predicted outputs. The term 

L(y − C ˆx) converts this discrepancy into a correction of the state estimate. Substitution of the observer into ˙ ex = ˙x − ˙ˆx recovers equation (7.132). Consequently, if A − LC is stable, then 

ex(t) → 0 and ˆ x(t) → x(t) even when the initial estimate is incorrect. A stabilizing observer gain exists when the measurements reveal every unstable motion of the system, a property called detectability of ( C, A ). In the deterministic setting, the eigenvalues of 

A − LC determine how rapidly the estimation error decays. Noise changes this design problem because a gain that responds strongly to measurements can also respond strongly to sensor noise. 

7.3.2 Random Disturbances and Noisy Measurements 

Augment the deterministic model with process and measurement noise: ˙x = Ax + Bu + w, (7.135) 

y = Cx + v, (7.136) Here w(t) ∈ Rn represents unmodeled forces and errors in the state dynamics, while v(t) ∈ Rp

represents sensor error. Thus w changes the physical state, whereas v changes the reported measurement. For a random vector z, E[z] denotes its mean and Cov( z) = E

> [

(z − E[z])( z − E[z]) T]

. (7.137) is its covariance. Its diagonal entries are variances, and its off-diagonal entries describe correlations. The standard LQG model assumes that w and v are zero-mean, independent Gaussian white Section 7.3 169 

noises, independent also of the initial state uncertainty. Their covariance intensities are Σw ⪰ 0, Σv ≻ 0, (7.138) which measure the rates at which the two noises inject uncertainty. Ideal continuous-time white noise is not an ordinary signal with a finite value at each instant. A rigorous model represents its accumulated effect using stochastic increments: 

dx = ( Ax + Bu ) dt + Σ 1/2 

> w

dW t, (7.139) 

dy = Cx dt + Σ 1/2 

> v

dV t, (7.140) where Wt and Vt are independent Wiener processes, also called Brownian motions. To avoid intro-ducing stochastic calculus here, we retain the common engineering shorthand in equations (7.135) and (7.136). Together with the expected quadratic objective 

E[J] = E 

> [

12 x(tf )TSf x(tf ) + 12 

> ∫tf
> t0
> (

xTQx + uTRu 

> )

dt 

> ]

. (7.141) this model defines linear–quadratic–Gaussian control, or LQG. The expectation averages over possible noise histories. A full treatment uses stochastic calculus; see Brockett [1983] for an application-oriented introduction. The key LQG result is that state estimation and LQR control can be designed separately: the LQR gain is computed as if the full state were known, and the resulting law is applied to the Kalman estimate as u = −K ˆx. This separation principle is explained in section 7.3.4. Persistent noise makes the usual undiscounted infinite-horizon total cost generally infinite, so one instead uses a finite-horizon, discounted, or long-run average formulation. 

7.3.3 Statistical State Estimation and the Kalman–Bucy Filter 

The estimation problem can be stated directly as a mean-square optimization problem. At time t,the observer has access to the measurement history 

Yt = {y(s) : t0 ≤ s ≤ t}. (7.142) Among all estimates a = a(Yt) that can be constructed from these measurements, we seek the one that minimizes the expected squared error: ˆx(t) = arg min 

> a=a(Yt)

E

> [

∥x(t) − a∥22 | Y t

> ]

. (7.143) The conditioning means that the observed history is treated as known, while the expectation averages over the state values that remain possible after those measurements. 170 Chapter 7. Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

To solve equation (7.143), define the conditional mean 

m(t) = E [x(t) | Y t] . (7.144) For any candidate a = a(Yt), write x − a = ( x − m) + ( m − a). The defining property of the conditional mean gives E[x − m | Y t] = 0, so the cross term vanishes when the square is expanded: 

E

> [

∥x(t) − a∥22 | Y t

> ]

= E

> [

∥x(t) − m(t)∥22 | Y t

> ]

+ ∥m(t) − a∥22. (7.145) The first term does not depend on a, and the second is nonnegative and vanishes only when 

a = m(t). Hence the solution of the estimation problem is ˆx(t) = E [x(t) | Y t] . (7.146) Equation (7.146) identifies the optimal estimate, but it does not yet give a differential equation for computing it. Under the linear–Gaussian assumptions, the conditional distribution of x(t) remains Gaussian and is completely characterized by its mean and covariance. We use the same observer structure as in the deterministic case, ˙ˆx = Aˆx + Bu + L(y − C ˆx). (7.147) In the deterministic design, L was chosen to make A − LC stable and to set the rate at which the estimation error decays. With random process and measurement noise, we instead choose the time-varying gain L(t) to minimize the expected squared estimation error. To obtain this gain, we first derive how the estimation error and its covariance depend on L.For ex = x − ˆx, substitution of the noisy plant, measurement, and observer equations gives ˙ex = ˙ x − ˙ˆx

= ( Ax + Bu + w) − [Aˆx + Bu + L(y − C ˆx)] = A(x − ˆx) + w − L [Cx + v − C ˆx]= ( A − LC )ex + w − Lv. (7.148) Because equation (7.146) minimizes the error for each measurement history, it also minimizes the unconditional mean-square error, since 

E[∥ex(t)∥22] = E

> [

E[∥ex(t)∥22 | Y t]

Equations (7.147), (7.162), and (7.163) form the Kalman–Bucy filter , the continuous-time minimum-mean-square estimator. The physical meaning of the filter is a repeated prediction–correction process. In equation (7.147), the term Aˆx + Bu predicts the state motion using the model and the known input. The difference 

y − C ˆx (7.164) is the innovation : the part of the latest measurement not explained by the prediction. The gain L

converts this output discrepancy into a correction of the state estimate. The covariance Σ measures the uncertainty in the estimate, and its diagonal entries are the error variances. In equation (7.163), the terms AΣ + Σ AT propagate existing uncertainty through the dynamics, Σ w adds uncertainty from process disturbances, and the negative term −ΣCTΣ−1 

> v

CΣrepresents uncertainty removed by the measurements. Thus greater process uncertainty makes the filter rely more strongly on measurements, whereas greater sensor uncertainty reduces their influence. This balance is also visible in L = Σ CTΣ−1 

> v

. A large state-estimation uncertainty Σ produces a stronger measurement correction, while a large measurement-noise covariance Σ v produces a weaker correction. The linear–Gaussian structure allows all earlier measurements to be summarized by the current pair (ˆ x, Σ); the complete history Yt need not be processed again whenever a new measurement arrives. The known input Bu cancels from the error dynamics because it is included in both the physical system and the observer. Finally, if w = v = 0, equation (7.148) reduces to the deterministic observer dynamics in equation (7.132). Section 7.3 173 

7.3.4 The Separation Principle in LQG 

If the state were measured exactly, the LQR solution would be u = −Kx . In the present setting, however, the controller does not know x; it knows only the sensor measurements received so far. The Kalman–Bucy filter summarizes those measurements by the conditional estimate ˆ x. This suggests the implementable control law 

u(t) = −K(t)ˆ x(t). (7.165) It is tempting simply to replace the unknown state by its best estimate, but the fact that ˆ x

minimizes estimation error does not by itself prove that this substitution minimizes the control cost. Estimation and control are different optimization problems, so the combined law requires a separate argument. To state that argument precisely, a measurement-based controller may use the history 

Yt = {y(s) : t0 ≤ s ≤ t}, (7.166) but it may not use the unknown true state. Thus u(t) must be a function of Yt; such a control is said to be adapted to the measurements. The separation principle states that, among all adapted controls for the LQG problem, the law in equation (7.165) is optimal. The LQR gain can therefore be designed as if the state were known, the Kalman filter can be designed from the sensor and noise models, and the two can then be combined. The form u = −K ˆx is also called certainty equivalence : the full-state law is left unchanged except that the unknown state is replaced by its conditional estimate. 

Theorem 7.6 (Finite-horizon LQG separation) . Consider the linear–Gaussian model in equa-tions (7.135) and (7.136) and the expected quadratic cost in equation (7.141) . Let P (t) solve the LQR differential Riccati equation in equation (7.18) , and define 

K(t) = R−1BTP (t). (7.167) 

Let ˆx(t) = E[x(t) | Y t] be the estimate produced by the Kalman–Bucy filter. Then the control in equation (7.165) uniquely minimizes the expected cost, over all controls adapted to Yt for which the expected cost is finite. Proof. The proof has two steps. First, we rewrite the expected cost as a term that is independent of the control plus a nonnegative square. Second, we minimize that square using only the information available from the measurements. For the first step, define Z(t) = x(t)TP (t)x(t)/2 and apply the Itô product rule along dx =(Ax + Bu )dt + Σ 1/2
