Because R ≻ 0, the remaining integral is nonnegative and is uniquely minimized by u = −K ˆx. This control is admissible because ˆ x is determined by the measurement history Yt. Thus the independently computed LQR gain and Kalman estimate produce the optimal measurement-based control. The proof gives a physical interpretation of certainty equivalence. If the state were known, LQR would make the completed-square residual u + Kx equal to zero. When the state is hidden, that residual cannot be cancelled for every possible value of x. The best admissible choice in the conditional mean-square sense is to cancel its predictable part, u + K ˆx. The remaining term depends on the unavoidable estimation uncertainty, not on the control policy. Section 7.3 175 

The two designs therefore use different data: K depends on ( A, B, Q, R, S f ), whereas L depends on ( A, C, Σw, Σv, Σ0). Neither Riccati equation contains the other gain. Optimality and stability are related but distinct questions. The theorem above proves that 

u = −K ˆx minimizes the expected quadratic cost. A separate noise-free argument shows that controller and estimator stability can also be checked independently. For the simplest explanation, consider the time-invariant infinite-horizon case, in which the controller and estimator Riccati equations have produced constant gains K and L. Since ˆ x = x − ex, the state and estimation error satisfy ˙x = ( A − BK )x + BKe x, (7.172) ˙ex = ( A − LC )ex. (7.173) Therefore [

˙x

˙ex

> ]

=

> [

A − BK BK 

0 A − LC 

> ] [

xex

> ]

. (7.174) The lower-left block is zero, so this matrix is block triangular . A block-triangular matrix has the eigenvalues of its diagonal blocks. Therefore the combined system has the controller eigenvalues of A − BK and the estimator-error eigenvalues of A − LC . The choice of L does not change the controller eigenvalues, and the choice of K does not change the estimator-error eigenvalues. Thus stability can be checked independently: the noise-free combination is stable when both 

A − BK and A − LC are stable. This block-triangular calculation does not by itself prove optimality; that claim follows from the expected-cost decomposition above. With persistent noise, individual trajectories do not converge exactly to zero; instead, one asks whether the state and estimation-error covariances remain bounded. The separation principle is exact for the unconstrained linear–quadratic Gaussian problem consid-ered here. It does not say that estimation and control are always independent. With non-Gaussian noise, the Kalman–Bucy filter is generally no longer the exact conditional-mean estimator, although some form of separation may still survive. Nonlinear dynamics, constraints, control-dependent sensing, nonquadratic or risk-sensitive objectives, and other information patterns can destroy the no-dual-effect property and couple estimation with control. In such problems, an action may be valuable not only because it moves the robot but also because it produces more informative future measurements. For reference tracking, the estimated-state controller becomes 

u = ur − K(ˆ x − xr). (7.175) The feedforward input supports the reference, the Kalman filter estimates the current state, and the LQR gain corrects the estimated tracking error. This estimator–controller architecture remains a standard baseline for robotic systems because every component has a clear mathematical and physical role. 

Exercises. Exercise 7.7. For the finite-horizon LQR problem, state the dimensions of P , K, and BTP . Why 176 Chapter 7. Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

must Kx have the same dimension as u?

Short solution. P ∈ Rn×n, BTP ∈ Rm×n, and K = R−1BTP ∈ Rm×n. Therefore Kx ∈ Rm,matching the control dimension.

Exercise 7.9. Use equation (7.26) to explain why the LQR extremal is a global minimum rather than merely a trajectory satisfying PMP. 

Short solution. The cost equals xTP (t)x/ 2 plus an integral of a quadratic form weighted by R ≻ 0. The integral is nonnegative for every admissible control and vanishes only for u = −R−1BTP x .No other control can have smaller cost.

Exercise 7.11. For the double integrator in equation (7.38), identify the position and velocity feedback gains in equation (7.44). 

Short solution. The position gain is kp(t) = p12 (t)/r , and the velocity gain is kv(t) = p22 (t)/r .Thus u = −kpq − kvv.

Exercise 7.12. Suppose Q, R, and Sf are all multiplied by the same scalar c > 0. How do P , K,and the optimal control change? 

Short solution. The objective is multiplied by c, so its minimizer does not change. The Riccati solution becomes cP , while ( cR )−1BT(cP ) = R−1BTP . Hence K and the optimal control are unchanged, although the value is multiplied by c.

Exercise 7.15. For a constant reference xr, why is a feedforward input ur required? What equation must ( xr, u r) satisfy? 

Short solution. The feedforward input must balance the plant dynamics at the desired state. The pair must satisfy Ax r + Bu r = 0. Without this balance, zero tracking error is not a constant solution of the closed-loop dynamics. 

Exercise 7.16. Let a feasible reference trajectory satisfy ˙ xr = A(t)xr + B(t)ur. Derive the error dynamics for e = x − xr and v = u − ur.

Short solution. Subtracting the reference dynamics from the plant dynamics gives ˙e = A(t)e + B(t)v. (7.180) Thus the tracking problem has the same form as regulation in the error coordinates.

Exercise 7.18. Suppose the double integrator is affected by an unknown constant acceleration d,so that ˙ ev = δu + d. Explain how the LQI law in equation (7.126) can maintain zero steady-state position error. 178 Chapter 7. Linear Quadratic Optimal Control 

Short solution. At a steady state with eq = ev = 0, the input correction is δu = −kiη. The acceleration is zero when −kiη + d = 0, so the integral state settles to η = d/k i and supplies the constant input needed to cancel the disturbance. 

Exercise 7.19. Derive the observer error equation for ˙ˆx = Aˆx + Bu + L(y − C ˆx).

Exercise 7.20. Explain separately why the LQR gain K and Kalman gain L can be designed independently for expected-cost optimality and for closed-loop stability. 

Short solution. For optimality, conditioning the completed-square identity on the measurement history leaves the control-dependent term ( u + K ˆx)TR(u + K ˆx), which is uniquely minimized by 

u = −K ˆx. The remaining estimation-error term is independent of the control because the Kalman covariance does not depend on u. For stability, the noise-free matrix in equation (7.174) is block triangular, so its eigenvalues are those of A − BK and A − LC . Thus the two stability conditions can also be checked independently. 

Exercise 7.21. Why is the usual undiscounted infinite-horizon expected cost generally infinite when persistent process noise is present? 

Short solution. Persistent noise continually excites the state, so the expected instantaneous quadratic cost generally approaches a positive steady value rather than zero. Integrating that positive value over an infinite interval gives an infinite total cost. 

Chapter summary. Linear dynamics and quadratic costs produce an unusually complete optimal control solution. For the finite-horizon problem, PMP gives a linear relation between the costate and state, λ = P x . The matrix P (t) satisfies the differential Riccati equation backward from the terminal cost, and the optimal law is the time-varying feedback u = −R−1BTP x . Completing the square proves that this law is the unique global minimizer and shows that the value function is V (t, x ) = xTP (t)x/ 2. For time-invariant infinite-horizon regulation, the Riccati differential equation becomes the algebraic Riccati equation. Its stabilizing solution gives a stationary feedback law and a quadratic Lyapunov function for the closed-loop system. Reference tracking is obtained by applying LQR to error coordinates and retaining the feedforward input needed to generate the reference. Around a nonlinear robot trajectory, the same construction gives local feedback based on linearized dynamics. For double-integrator error dynamics, the optimal feedback has the familiar PD form. Computed-torque control applies this optimal PD law after canceling modeled mechanical dynamics, while augmenting the state with integrated tracking error produces an LQI design with PID form and rejection of constant biases. Section 7.3 179 

When the state is not known exactly, an observer estimates it from noisy measurements. The Kalman–Bucy filter evolves an uncertainty covariance through a second Riccati equation. In the LQG setting, the separation principle allows the estimator and controller to be designed independently and combined as u = −K ˆx. These ideas provide the most important exactly solvable example of optimal feedback control and prepare the quadratic example in the HJB framework of Chapter 8. 8

# Dynamic Programming and the HJB Equation 

Chapter map. The principle of optimality, Bellman recursion, continuous-time Hamilton– Jacobi–Bellman equations, optimal feedback, linear–quadratic control, numerical value iteration, and the relationship between dynamic programming and Pontryagin’s minimum principle. 

Chapter 6 approached optimal control by perturbing a candidate trajectory. The resulting state, costate, and Hamiltonian conditions describe what must hold along an optimal motion from a specified initial condition. Chapter 7 showed that the linear–quadratic problem has additional structure: a single Riccati equation produces a feedback law for an entire region of states. This chapter develops a more general route to feedback. Its central object is the value function , or 

optimal cost-to-go : the smallest future cost achievable from each time–state pair. The cost-to-go under a particular control need not be optimal; the value function is the infimum of these costs over all admissible controls. Once the value function is known, the optimal action can be determined from the current time and state. The dynamics predict how each candidate action changes the state and, consequently, how the value changes. The controller chooses the action that minimizes the immediate cost plus the optimal future cost of the resulting state. If the immediate cost is independent of the action, this amounts to choosing the action that produces the greatest decrease in value. In general, the decrease in value must be balanced against the cost of producing it. This decision can be made without solving the full trajectory-optimization problem again. The key question is how to compute the value function. The central insight is the principle of optimality . Imagine cutting an optimal trajectory at some intermediate time. From the state reached at that time, the remaining part of the trajectory must be an optimal solution of the remaining problem. If a cheaper continuation existed, we could keep the first part unchanged and replace only the remainder, producing a cheaper complete trajectory and contradicting optimality. This observation makes the value function recursive. The optimal cost from the current state 180 Section 8.1 181 

equals the cost accumulated over a short first interval plus the optimal cost-to-go from the state reached at the end of that interval. Thus, once later values are known, earlier values can be computed by solving shorter local minimization problems. Computing the value function through this backward recursion is the core idea of dynamic programming .In discrete time, this principle produces the Bellman recursion. In continuous time, applying it over an interval that shrinks to zero produces the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation, abbreviated HJB. Solving HJB over a state domain yields an optimal feedback law rather than a single open-loop control signal. The price is computational: HJB is generally a nonlinear partial differential equation in time and state, and its numerical cost grows rapidly with the state dimension. This tradeoff motivates the local and approximate methods developed in later chapters. Standard references include Bertsekas [2017], Kirk [2004], Lewis et al. [2012]. 

# 8.1 The Principle of Optimality 

8.1.1 Cost-to-Go and Optimal Substructure 

Consider the finite-horizon system ˙x(s) = f (s, x (s), u (s)), x(t) = x, (8.1) with terminal time tf , running cost ℓ, terminal cost Φ, and admissible controls u(s) ∈ U . Starting at time t from state x, the cost of a control signal is 

Jt,x [u] = Φ (x(tf ), t f

ℓ(s, x (s), u (s)) ds. (8.2) The value function is the best cost achievable from that time and state: 

V (t, x ) = inf  

> u(·)

Jt,x [u]. (8.3) The terminal condition is 

V (tf , x ) = Φ( x, t f ), (8.4) because no running interval remains at tf .The word value does not mean the value of the state. It means the optimal numerical value of the remaining objective. The value function assigns a cost to each possible situation. A low value means that the task can still be completed cheaply; a high value means that the current state has expensive future consequences. Two states that are geometrically close may have very different values. A vehicle moving toward an obstacle and the same vehicle at the same position moving away from it have different states and can have very different future costs. Choose any intermediate time τ ∈ (t, t f ). Every complete control can be viewed as two pieces: a 182 Chapter 8. Dynamic Programming and the HJB Equation 

first piece on [ t, τ ] and a continuation on [ τ, t f ]. The cost separates in the same way:

. (8.5) Once the first piece reaches x(τ ), the expression in brackets is itself a control problem beginning from ( τ, x (τ )). Its smallest possible value is V (τ, x (τ )). 

Theorem 8.1 (Principle of optimality) . For every intermediate time τ ∈ [t, t f ], the value function satisfies 

V (t, x ) = inf     

> u(·)on [t,τ ]
> {∫ τt

ℓ(s, x (s), u (s)) ds + V (τ, x (τ ))}

. (8.6) 

Consequently, the continuation of an optimal control after time τ must be optimal for the state reached at time τ .Proof. Take any complete admissible control. Its continuation after τ cannot cost less than 

V (τ, x (τ )), because the value is the infimum over all continuations from that state. Therefore its complete cost is at least the quantity on the right-hand side of equation (8.6). Taking the infimum over complete controls gives one inequality. Conversely, select a first control segment and then append a continuation whose cost is arbitrarily close to V (τ, x (τ )). Concatenating the two segments gives an admissible complete control with cost arbitrarily close to the right-hand side. Taking the infimum over first segments gives the reverse inequality. If optimal controls exist, “arbitrarily close” can be replaced by an optimal continuation. This result is a consistency condition across time. It is not a greedy rule. The controller does not minimize the immediate running cost alone. It minimizes immediate cost plus the value of the state that remains. A robot may temporarily spend more energy to move into a region from which collision avoidance or final positioning becomes much cheaper. The principle can also be understood by contradiction. Suppose an optimal trajectory reaches some state x(τ ) but then follows a continuation that is not optimal from that state. Replacing only the continuation by a cheaper one leaves the earlier trajectory unchanged and reduces the total cost. That contradicts optimality of the original trajectory. This replacement argument is the source of both Bellman recursion and HJB. 

8.1.2 Why the State Must Summarize the Past 

The value in equation (8.3) depends on the current state rather than the complete past trajectory. This is valid only when the state contains all information from the past that can affect future dynamics, costs, and constraints. We call such a state a sufficient state .For a mechanical system, position alone is usually not sufficient. Future motion also depends on Section 8.1 183 

velocity, so a state such as x = ( q, ˙q) is required. If actuator temperature limits future torque, temperature must also be included. If the running cost penalizes changes in control, the previous control can be added to the state. Once these variables are included, two trajectories arriving at the same augmented state have the same set of possible futures and the same cost-to-go. This observation is more than bookkeeping. Dynamic programming fails if two histories are assigned the same state even though they permit different future behavior. The remedy is to enlarge the state until the future depends only on the present state and future controls. The state may then be viewed as a compressed memory of everything relevant from the past. When V is known, it defines feedback because the best current decision can be recomputed from the current state. If a disturbance moves the system away from the trajectory expected earlier, the controller evaluates the decision at the new state. By contrast, a previously computed open-loop signal does not change unless a new optimization is solved.

. (8.18) Thus quadratic value functions reproduce themselves under Bellman recursion. The coefficient pk

measures how expensive state error is when N − k steps remain, and the feedback gain converts that future cost into corrective action. This is the discrete-time counterpart of the Riccati structure in Chapter 7. Bellman recursion separates a long decision sequence into repeated one-step problems. It does not eliminate optimization; it reorganizes it. Instead of optimizing all controls simultaneously for one initial state, it computes the best continuation from every state at every stage. That broader answer is why dynamic programming produces feedback and why it can require much more computation. 

# 8.2 The Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman Equation Section 8.2 185 

8.2.1 From a Short Time Step to HJB 

We now apply the principle of optimality over a short continuous-time interval [ t, t + h], where 

h > 0 is small. Setting τ = t + h in equation (8.6) gives 

V (t, x ) = inf  

> u(·) on [ t,t +h]
> {∫t+ht

ℓ(s, x (s), u (s)) ds + V (t + h, x (t + h))}

. (8.19) The first term measures what is paid immediately. The second assigns the optimal remaining cost to the state reached after the short interval. Assume for now that V is continuously differentiable and that the dynamics and running cost are smooth. Hold the control equal to a constant value u over the short interval. To first order in h,

x(t + h) = x + hf (t, x, u ) + o(h), (8.20)  

> ∫t+ht

ℓ(s, x (s), u ) ds = hℓ (t, x, u ) + o(h). (8.21) The notation o(h) denotes terms that vanish faster than h, meaning that o(h)/h → 0 as h → 0. A first-order Taylor expansion of the value gives 

V (t + h, x (t + h)) = V (t, x ) + hV t(t, x )+ h∇xV (t, x )Tf (t, x, u ) + o(h), (8.22) where Vt is the time derivative and ∇xV is the gradient with respect to the state. Substituting these expansions into equation (8.19) gives, for a constant control u over the short interval, 

V (t, x ) = inf 

> u∈U (t,x )
> {

V (t, x ) + hℓ (t, x, u ) + hV t(t, x ) + h∇xV (t, x )Tf (t, x, u ) + o(h)}. (8.23) The common term V (t, x ) appears on both sides because, over a vanishingly short interval, the system accumulates almost no cost and moves only an infinitesimal distance. Canceling this term and dividing by h yields 0 = inf 

> u∈U (t,x )
> {

ℓ(t, x, u ) + Vt(t, x ) + ∇xV (t, x )Tf (t, x, u ) + o(h)

h

> }

. (8.24) As h → 0, the final term vanishes. Only the current value of the control remains: its effect over the short interval is summarized by the instantaneous dynamics f (t, x, u ) and running cost ℓ(t, x, u ). Under conditions ensuring that the infimum is attained, it may be written as a minimum, giving 0 = Vt(t, x ) + min 

> u∈U (t,x )
> {

ℓ(t, x, u ) + ∇xV (t, x )Tf (t, x, u )

> }

. (8.25) The minimization is pointwise: at each fixed pair ( t, x ), it compares all admissible current controls. The term Vt records how the remaining optimal cost changes merely because time advances, while 

∇xV Tf records how it changes because the selected control moves the state. The minimizing 186 Chapter 8. Dynamic Programming and the HJB Equation 

control balances these changes against the immediate running cost ℓ.Together with equation (8.4), this is the finite-horizon Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation . It is a partial differential equation because the unknown V (t, x ) depends on time and all state coordinates, and the equation contains derivatives with respect to those variables. It is generally nonlinear because of the minimization and because the dynamics can be nonlinear. Define the Hamiltonian 

H(t, x, u, p ) = ℓ(t, x, u ) + pTf (t, x, u ), (8.26) where p ∈ Rn is a placeholder for a state gradient. This is the same Hamiltonian form used in Chapter 6. The HJB equation can then be written compactly as 0 = Vt(t, x ) + min  

> u∈U (t,x )

H(t, x, u, ∇xV (t, x )). (8.27) The term ∇xV Tf predicts how the future value changes because the chosen control moves the state. Thus HJB balances two rates: the running cost paid now and the rate at which the motion changes the remaining optimal cost. A control that has small immediate cost can still be poor if it moves the system toward states with rapidly increasing value. The terminal cost supplies data at tf , and HJB propagates that information backward through time. For this reason finite-horizon value functions and feedback laws are generally time varying. Near the terminal time, little time remains to correct an error, so the best action can differ from the action at the same state earlier in the horizon. 

8.2.2 A Verification Theorem 

The short-time derivation motivates HJB. The following result explains why a smooth solution actually certifies optimality. 

Theorem 8.3 (HJB verification) . Suppose a continuously differentiable function W (t, x ) satisfies 

0 = Wt(t, x ) + min 

> u∈U (t,x )
> {

ℓ(t, x, u ) + ∇xW (t, x )Tf (t, x, u )

> }

, (8.28) 

and W (tf , x ) = Φ( x, t f ). Suppose also that an admissible feedback u = μ⋆(t, x ) attains the displayed minimum and produces a well-defined trajectory. Then 

W (t, x ) = V (t, x ), (8.29) 

and μ⋆ is an optimal feedback law. Proof. For any admissible control u(s) and its state trajectory, the minimum in equation (8.28) is no larger than the expression evaluated at that control. Hence 

Wt + ∇xW Tf (s, x, u ) + ℓ(s, x, u ) ≥ 0. (8.30) Section 8.2 187 

Along the trajectory, the chain rule gives 

dds W (s, x (s)) = Wt + ∇xW T ˙x = Wt + ∇xW Tf (s, x, u ). (8.31) Therefore 

ℓ(s, x (s), u (s)) ≥ − dds W (s, x (s)) . (8.32) Integrating from t to tf and using the terminal condition gives 

Jt,x [u] = Φ( x(tf ), t f ) +  

> ∫tf
> t

ℓ(s, x, u ) ds 

≥ W (tf , x (tf )) + W (t, x ) − W (tf , x (tf )) = W (t, x ). (8.33) Thus no admissible control can cost less than W (t, x ). When u = μ⋆(t, x ) attains the HJB minimum, every inequality above becomes an equality. Its cost is exactly W (t, x ), so W = V and the feedback is optimal. The verification theorem is stronger than a stationarity test along one trajectory. A function satisfying HJB over a domain provides a lower bound on the cost of every admissible control from every state in that domain, and a minimizing feedback attains that bound. The difficulty is that finding such a function is usually much harder than checking necessary conditions along one curve. 

8.2.3 Recovering the Optimal Feedback Law 

Once the value function is known, the optimal feedback follows from the pointwise minimization in HJB: 

μ⋆(t, x ) ∈ arg min 

> u∈U (t,x )
> {

ℓ(t, x, u ) + ∇xV (t, x )Tf (t, x, u )

> }

. (8.34) The optimization is pointwise because t, x, and ∇xV (t, x ) are fixed while choosing the current input. All future consequences have already been compressed into V .The gradient has a useful physical meaning. For a small state displacement δx ,

V (t, x + δx ) − V (t, x ) ≈ ∇ xV (t, x )Tδx. (8.35) Thus the ith component of the gradient measures the marginal change in optimal future cost caused by a small change in the ith state coordinate. The feedback selects a velocity f (t, x, u )through state space while accounting for how strongly the value rises or falls in that direction. For a control-affine system ˙x = f0(t, x ) + G(t, x )u (8.36) with running cost 

ℓ(t, x, u ) = L(t, x ) + 12 uTR(t, x )u, R(t, x ) ≻ 0, (8.37) 188 Chapter 8. Dynamic Programming and the HJB Equation 

the terms depending on u are 12 uTRu + ∇xV TGu. (8.38) For unconstrained control, differentiation with respect to u gives 

Ru + GT∇xV = 0 , (8.39) and hence 

μ⋆(t, x ) = −R(t, x )−1G(t, x )T∇xV (t, x ). (8.40) This formula generalizes the LQR law. The value gradient plays the role of the costate, while GT

extracts the part of that gradient that the actuators can influence. If controls are constrained, the unconstrained formula may be infeasible. The correct law is still the minimizer in equation (8.34). For a scalar input with u ∈ [umin , u max ] and quadratic effort, the constrained minimizer is the projection 

u⋆ = Π [umin ,u max ]

> (

−R−1GT∇xV

> )

, (8.41) where Π clips a scalar to the indicated interval. Saturation therefore appears naturally from Hamiltonian minimization rather than being added after the optimal law is derived.

from the terminal boundary. Setting Pk = Pk+1 = P in equation (8.62) gives

The continuous-time algebraic Riccati equation in Chapter 7 and the DARE play analogous roles, but the appropriate equation is determined by whether the plant and controller are formulated in continuous or discrete time. These examples also explain why the general HJB equation is difficult. LQR works because the class of quadratic functions is preserved by the Bellman update. For nonlinear systems or nonquadratic costs, the value function need not belong to any simple finite-dimensional family, so an entire function over state space must be represented.

stationary HJB equation 

0 = min 

> u∈U (x)

8.2.6 Minimum Time and a Nonsmooth Value Function 

The classical HJB derivation assumed that the value function is smooth, but optimal value functions can develop corners even for very simple dynamics. Consider the speed-limited integrator ˙x = u, |u| ≤ 1, (8.71) and let the task end when the state first reaches the target x = 0. With the running cost ℓ = 1, the accumulated cost is exactly the travel time. The minimum time from an initial state x is 

V (x) = |x|, (8.72) because the state can move toward the origin at unit speed, and no admissible control can reach it faster. Away from the target, the stationary HJB equation is 0 = min  

> |u|≤ 1

{1 + Vx(x)u}

= 1 − | Vx(x)|. (8.73) For x > 0, Vx = 1, and the minimizing control is u = −1. For x < 0, Vx = −1, and the minimizing control is u = 1. Thus the optimal control always points toward the target. The two feedback branches meet at x = 0, where |x| has left derivative −1 and right derivative 1, but no single derivative. This change of optimal direction creates the corner in the value function. The HJB equation is imposed away from the target, so V (x) = |x| is a classical solution on R \ { 0}.At the target, the task has ended and the value is specified by the boundary condition 

V (0) = 0 . (8.74) No control decision or differential equation is required after the target is reached. More generally, 

V is regarded as a solution of the HJB boundary- value problem in the viscosity sense. Roughly, a viscosity solution need not be differentiable everywhere. Instead, the HJB inequalities are tested using smooth functions that touch the value function locally from above or below. This generalized Section 8.3 193 

notion preserves the Bellman principle and identifies the correct value function even in the presence of corners. This lack of differentiability is not a defect in the control problem. It records a change in the active optimal strategy. Similar corners arise when different routes around an obstacle have equal cost, when an actuator enters or leaves saturation, or when several target points are equally good. At a smooth point, the value gradient defines one local direction of increasing cost. At a corner, several one-sided slopes or optimal strategies meet. A full theory of viscosity solutions is beyond this chapter, but the Bellman principle remains meaningful, and the grid methods described next can approximate nonsmooth value functions without differentiating them explicitly at every point. 

# 8.3 Computation and Limitations 

8.3.1 Grid-Based Value Iteration 

Except in structured examples such as LQR, HJB rarely has a closed-form solution. A direct numerical idea is to store the value on a grid of states and apply an approximate Bellman update backward in time. This approach makes the connection between the discrete and continuous theories especially clear. Divide the horizon into times 

tk = t0 + k∆t, k = 0 , . . . , N, tN = tf , (8.75) and choose state grid points xi. Over one small time step, forward Euler integration approximates the dynamics by 

x+ = xi + ∆ t f (tk, x i, u ). (8.76) The next state x+ will usually lie between grid points. Let I[Vk+1 ]( x+) denote an interpolation :an estimate of the value at x+ formed from nearby stored values. In one dimension, linear interpolation connects adjacent samples by a straight line. In several dimensions, the same idea is applied within the surrounding grid cell. The approximate Bellman update is 

Vk(xi) = min  

> u∈U d(xi)

{∆t ℓ (tk, x i, u ) + I[Vk+1 ] ( xi + ∆ tf (tk, x i, u )) } , (8.77) with terminal initialization 

VN (xi) = Φ( xi, t f ). (8.78) Here Ud(xi) is either the original control set, if the inner minimization can be solved continuously, or a finite collection of sampled controls. The minimizing control is stored as the approximate feedback μk(xi). The computation proceeds as follows: 1. assign the terminal cost to every state-grid point; 194 Chapter 8. Dynamic Programming and the HJB Equation 

2. at time tN −1, test candidate controls at each state point, predict the next state, interpolate its terminal value, and keep the smallest total; 3. repeat for tN −2, t N −3, . . . , t 0; and 4. during closed-loop operation, interpolate the stored feedback or repeat the one-step minimization using the stored value. The algorithm propagates cost information backward from the terminal condition. A large terminal penalty near an undesirable final state affects nearby states one step earlier, then states two steps earlier, and so on. In a minimum-time problem, low values spread outward from the target like an arrival-time wave. The feedback at each state points toward a neighboring state with an attractive combination of immediate and future cost. The update in equation (8.77) is sometimes called a semi-Lagrangian update. The name means that the grid is fixed, but each candidate control follows the dynamics forward to an off-grid next state before interpolation. Expanding the interpolated value for small ∆ t recovers the HJB equation, so the update is a discrete approximation of the same optimality principle. Several design choices affect the computed solution: • A smaller time step reduces integration error but increases the number of backward stages. • A finer state grid reduces spatial error but increases memory and computation. • A coarse control sample can miss the true minimizing action. • Interpolation must not create artificial values that make unsafe or infeasible states appear attractive. • The boundary of the computational domain requires a deliberate rule. One may impose a known boundary cost, forbid transitions outside the domain, or enlarge the domain so relevant trajectories remain inside. State constraints can be represented by assigning an infinite or very large value to forbidden states, but a large finite penalty is only an approximation. If collision must be impossible, the numerical transition rule should explicitly reject controls whose trajectories cross an obstacle rather than merely checking the endpoint of a large time step. 

Example 8.4 (A grid update for minimum-time motion) . For the integrator in equation (8.71), choose u ∈ {− 1, 1} and let each time step cost ∆ t. Away from the target, the update is 

Vk(xi) = ∆ t + min  

> u∈{− 1,1}

I[Vk+1 ]( xi + ∆ t u ). (8.79) The minimizing control selects the neighboring direction with smaller future arrival time. On a sufficiently fine grid, the computed value approaches |x| and the stored feedback points toward the origin. Section 8.3 195 

8.3.2 The Curse of Dimensionality 

Grid methods are conceptually simple but scale poorly. Suppose each state coordinate is represented by M grid points. An n-dimensional state then requires 

M n (8.80) value samples at every time layer. This exponential growth with dimension is called the curse of dimensionality .For M = 101, a two-dimensional grid contains 10 ,201 points, a four-dimensional grid contains more than 10 8 points, and a six-dimensional grid contains more than 10 12 points. Storing one double-precision value at each six-dimensional point would already require several terabytes, before storing a feedback law, obstacles, or additional time layers. A manipulator with d joints usually has at least 2 d state variables when positions and velocities are included, so a uniform grid becomes impractical even for moderate d.The control search can add another exponential factor. If each of m input coordinates is sampled at Mu values, then a full control grid contains M mu candidate actions. A finite-horizon calculation with N time steps therefore has work proportional, in a basic implementation, to 

N M nM mu . (8.81) Interpolation and collision checking increase the cost further. This limitation motivates several alternatives. When the model has special structure, as in LQR, the value can be represented by a small matrix rather than a grid. Local trajectory methods approximate the value near a nominal motion; Chapter 9 develops this idea through iterative LQR. Model predictive control repeatedly solves a finite-horizon problem only near the currently visited state rather than filling an entire state domain. Approximate dynamic programming represents the value or policy by a parameterized function fitted from samples. These approaches sacrifice some combination of global coverage, exactness, or guarantees in exchange for tractability. The curse of dimensionality does not make HJB irrelevant. The equation still states what an optimal value function must satisfy, guides the design of approximations, explains the structure of optimal feedback, and provides benchmarks for low-dimensional systems. 

8.3.3 HJB versus Pontryagin’s Minimum Principle 

Dynamic programming and PMP are not competing definitions of optimality. They describe the same problem at different scales. HJB studies the optimal cost from every state, while PMP studies necessary conditions along one candidate optimal trajectory. When the value function is smooth, their variables are connected directly. Let x⋆(t) be generated by a smooth HJB-minimizing feedback, and define 

λ(t) = ∇xV (t, x ⋆(t)). (8.82) 196 Chapter 8. Dynamic Programming and the HJB Equation 

Along the optimal feedback, HJB reads 

Vt + H(t, x, u ⋆, V x) = 0 . (8.83) Assume for simplicity that the control minimizer is interior and smooth, so Hu = 0. Differentiate equation (8.83) with respect to x. The dependence of H on p = Vx contributes Vxx Hp, and the dependence through u⋆(t, x ) contributes Huu⋆x = 0. Therefore 

Vxt + Hx + Vxx Hp = 0 . (8.84) Since Hp = f = ˙ x⋆, the total derivative of equation (8.82) is ˙λ = Vxt + Vxx ˙x⋆

= Vxt + Vxx Hp = −Hx. (8.85) The HJB minimization also gives 

u⋆ ∈ arg min 

> u∈U

H(t, x ⋆, u, λ ), (8.86) and the terminal condition gives 

λ(tf ) = ∇xΦ( x⋆(tf ), t f ). (8.87) Thus the state equation, costate equation, Hamiltonian minimization, and terminal costate condition of PMP all follow along a smooth HJB-optimal trajectory. The identity λ = ∇xV gives the costate a concrete interpretation. It is the sensitivity of optimal future cost to a perturbation of the current state. PMP propagates that sensitivity along one trajectory. HJB constructs the entire field of sensitivities over a state domain. Aspect Pontryagin’s principle Dynamic programming and HJB Primary object One state–costate trajectory A value function over time and state Typical result An open-loop extremal for one initial condition A feedback law for all represented states Optimality role Generally necessary conditions A smooth verified solution certifies global optimality on its domain Main computation Ordinary differential equations with split boundary conditions A nonlinear partial differential equation or Bellman recursion Main difficulty Boundary-value sensitivity and multiple extremals Exponential growth with state dimension PMP is often attractive for a high-dimensional system when only a few initial conditions matter, because it avoids constructing a function over the full state space. HJB is attractive when a low-dimensional problem requires a global feedback policy, explicit treatment of many initial states, or a value map for planning and safety analysis. Hybrid strategies are common: a global or coarse Section 8.3 197 

value approximation guides motion, while local trajectory optimization or LQR supplies accurate control near a nominal path. Neither method removes modeling choices. A globally solved HJB equation is only optimal for its stated dynamics, cost, constraints, and domain. A PMP trajectory is only relevant if it satisfies all constraints and corresponds to a minimum rather than another extremal. In both viewpoints, the quality of the controller begins with the quality of the problem formulation. 

Exercises. Exercise 8.5. Explain why the continuation of an optimal trajectory must be optimal from every intermediate state it reaches. Why does this statement not imply that the optimal control minimizes the running cost at each instant? 

Short solution. If a cheaper continuation existed, replacing the original tail by that continuation would reduce the complete cost and contradict optimality. The controller minimizes current cost plus optimal future cost, not current cost alone, so it may accept a larger immediate cost to reach a better future state. 

Exercise 8.6. A robot has position q and velocity v, but a proposed dynamic-programming model uses only q as its state. Explain why this state is generally insufficient. Give one additional example of a variable that may need to be included in an augmented state. 

Short solution. Two trajectories can reach the same position with different velocities and therefore have different possible futures and costs. The state should include v. Other examples include actuator temperature, battery charge, or the previous control when changes in control are penalized.

Short solution. After choosing u at state x, the first cost is ℓk(x, u ) and the next state is Fk(x, u ). The smallest remaining cost is Vk+1 (Fk(x, u )). Minimizing their sum gives

Exercise 8.16. Assuming a smooth value function and an interior minimizing control, show how the PMP costate equation follows from λ = ∇xV .

Short solution. Differentiate HJB with respect to x. Because Hu = 0 at the interior minimizer and Hp = f ,

Exercise 8.17. Why can HJB produce a feedback law directly, while a single PMP solution usually produces only one open-loop trajectory? 

Short solution. HJB computes V (t, x ) and its minimizing action over a domain of states, so the control can be evaluated at whichever state is currently observed. A single PMP solve computes state, costate, and control only along the extremal associated with one initial condition. 

Chapter summary. Dynamic programming organizes optimal control around the value function, the smallest future cost achievable from each time–state pair. The principle of optimality states that every continuation of an optimal trajectory must be optimal from the state at which that continuation begins. This principle requires a sufficient state that summarizes all past information relevant to future dynamics and costs. In discrete time it gives the Bellman recursion, which combines one-stage cost with the value of the next state and computes feedback by backward induction. Applying the same recursion over a short continuous-time interval and taking the limit gives the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation. HJB balances immediate running cost against the rate at which the chosen dynamics change future value. A smooth solution satisfying the terminal condition provides an optimality certificate: every admissible control has cost at least equal to the value, and a feedback that attains the Hamiltonian minimum achieves that bound. For control-affine dynamics with quadratic effort, the optimal action is proportional to the negative value gradient. In the linear–quadratic case, a quadratic value ansatz reduces HJB to the differential Riccati equation and recovers the continuous-time LQR feedback law. Applying the discrete-time Bellman recursion to linear dynamics and quadratic costs similarly yields the backward Riccati difference equation. Its time-invariant fixed point is the discrete-time algebraic Riccati equation. Autonomous infinite-horizon problems lead to stationary HJB equations, while discounting adds the term ρV . Value functions need not be smooth; minimum-time motion and competing routes can create corners where the active optimal strategy changes. Grid-based Bellman updates can approximate both smooth and nonsmooth values, but their storage and computation grow exponentially with state dimension. This curse of dimensionality motivates Riccati methods, local trajectory approximations, model predictive control, and parameterized value approximations. Finally, HJB and Pontryagin’s principle are linked by λ = ∇xV along a smooth optimal trajectory. PMP propagates value sensitivity along one candidate motion, whereas HJB constructs the full field of optimal costs and feedback decisions over a state domain. The next chapter uses local dynamic-programming approximations to optimize nonlinear trajectories without solving a global HJB equation. Part III 

# Trajectory Optimization and Model Predictive Control 

> 201

# 9

# Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

Chapter map. Nonlinear finite-horizon control problems, single and multiple shooting, direct transcription, adjoint gradients, iterative linear–quadratic methods, differential dynamic programming, contact-aware robotics examples, constraints, and practical implementation. 

The preceding chapters developed several complementary views of optimal control. Pontryagin’s principle gives necessary conditions along a candidate trajectory. Linear–quadratic control turns a special class of problems into a Riccati recursion and a feedback law. Dynamic programming characterizes the globally optimal value function through Bellman recursion or the HJB equation. Each viewpoint is powerful, but a practical gap remains. Robotic systems are usually nonlinear, their tasks have finite horizons, and their states and inputs are constrained. For such problems, the global Riccati structure of LQR is absent, while a grid-based solution of HJB is generally impractical. Trajectory optimization addresses this gap by concentrating computation near one motion. Rather than computing the optimal action from every possible state, it searches for a state–control sequence that performs a specified task from a specified initial condition. Examples include swinging up a pendulum, moving a manipulator around obstacles, landing a vehicle, and planning a dynamically feasible walking motion. The resulting plan may be used directly, tracked by a separate controller, or repeatedly recomputed in model predictive control. The apparent simplicity of this idea hides an important difficulty. A control chosen early in the horizon changes every later state, so the decision variables are strongly coupled through time. Unstable dynamics can amplify a small perturbation, constraints can make an otherwise attractive motion infeasible, and a nonlinear problem may contain several qualitatively different local solutions. A useful algorithm must therefore exploit the temporal structure rather than treating the problem as a generic large-scale optimization. This chapter develops two complementary ways to solve a discretized control problem. General direct methods formulate a finite-dimensional nonlinear program and exploit its sparse constraints. Structured shooting methods, especially iterative linear–quadratic regulation (iLQR), repeatedly 202 Section 9.1 203 

solve a local time-varying LQR approximation. Both are direct methods; they differ in how they represent the trajectory and solve the local optimization problem. iLQR is particularly revealing because it combines ideas from the previous chapters: it uses local linearization, quadratic value approximations, a Riccati-like backward pass, and a nonlinear forward rollout. Standard references include Bertsekas [2017], Kirk [2004], Lewis et al. [2012]. The next chapter places these methods inside a feedback loop through model predictive control (MPC), which repeatedly solves a finite-horizon trajectory optimization problem as new state measurements become available. 

# 9.1 Direct Versus Indirect Numerical Methods 

The central distinction is the order in which optimality conditions and discretization are introduced: indirect: continuous control problem −→ PMP conditions 

−→ boundary-value or root solve ,

direct: continuous control problem −→ discretized optimization problem 

−→ numerical minimization .

(9.1) Both routes ultimately use numerical computation, but they solve different finite-dimensional problems. Chapter 6 introduced indirect methods . They first apply Pontryagin’s minimum principle to obtain the state equation, costate equation, Hamiltonian minimization condition, and boundary conditions. Only then are these necessary conditions solved numerically. The unknowns may include an initial costate, endpoint multipliers, and switching times. A converged solution is an extremal satisfying necessary conditions; additional checks are needed to determine whether it is a local minimum. The methods in this chapter are primarily direct . They first choose a finite time grid and discretize the original dynamics, objective, and constraints. The resulting finite-dimensional optimization problem is solved for sampled states and controls without explicitly deriving the continuous-time costate equations. Necessary conditions still appear internally—for example, nonlinear-programming multipliers are discrete analogues of costates—but the user formulates the original control problem rather than its PMP boundary-value equations. The distinction between direct and indirect methods specifies which equations are solved, but it does not uniquely determine how they are represented numerically. Either route can use shooting, multiple shooting, transcription or collocation, parameterized trajectories, or grid-based discretizations. For example, shooting integrates the relevant equations and adjusts a finite set of unknowns, whereas transcription retains sampled variables and enforces the equations through algebraic constraints. A parameterized method instead represents a control or trajectory with a finite set of coefficients. In an indirect method these techniques are applied to the PMP state–costate conditions; in a direct method they are applied to the original dynamics, costs, and constraints. No representation is uniformly best. Shooting can be compact and accurate, but may be sensitive 204 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

Table 9.1: Indirect and direct numerical optimal control. Aspect Indirect method Direct method Formulated from PMP necessary conditions original dynamics, cost, and constraints Typical unknowns costate boundary values, multipliers, switching times sampled controls and, in transcription, sampled states Numerical task root finding or a boundary-value solve nonlinear minimization Path constraints require extra multipliers, arcs, and switching conditions imposed directly as finite-dimensional constraints over long or unstable trajectories. Transcription and grid-based methods introduce more variables but expose sparsity and handle path constraints naturally. Low-dimensional parameterizations reduce the number of unknowns, but can exclude useful trajectories if the chosen basis is too restrictive. The appropriate choice depends on the dynamics, horizon, constraints, required accuracy, derivative information, and available initialization. This chapter emphasizes direct shooting, direct transcription, and structured local methods while noting that other combinations are often equally valid. 

# 9.2 Finite-Horizon Trajectory Problems 

Trajectory optimization begins by replacing a continuous motion over an interval by a finite sequence of states and controls. This replacement is not merely a numerical convenience: it determines which quantities the optimizer may change and how dynamic consistency is enforced. We therefore begin with the construction of the discrete problem before discussing algorithms for solving it. 

9.2.1 Dynamics, Costs, and Constraints 

Suppose a continuous-time model is written as ˙x(t) = fc

> (

t, x (t), u (t)). (9.2) Choose grid times t0 < · · · < t N and hold the control constant, or represent it by a low-order polynomial, on each interval. Numerically integrating equation (9.2) from tk to tk+1 defines a transition map 

xk+1 = fk(xk, u k). (9.3) For example, with step length ∆ tk = tk+1 − tk, forward Euler gives

xk+1 = fk(xk, u k), k = 0 , . . . , N − 1, x0 = xinit , (9.5) where xk ∈ Rn, uk ∈ Rm, and the initial state is given. A standard finite-horizon objective is

The terminal cost measures the quality of the final state. The discrete stage cost may itself approximate a continuous integral. If the continuous running cost is L(t, x, u ), a left-endpoint rule gives 

ℓk(xk, u k) = ∆ tkL(tk, x k, u k), (9.8) and higher-order quadrature may be paired with the integration scheme. Thus both the dynamics and the objective inherit discretization error. Refining the grid should change the computed motion less and less; otherwise the apparent optimum may be an artifact of the chosen time step. State and input restrictions may be written as 

gk(xk, u k) ≤ 0, hk(xk, u k) = 0 , k = 0 , . . . , N − 1, (9.9) 

gN (xN ) ≤ 0, hN (xN ) = 0 . (9.10) These relations can encode actuator limits, collision avoidance, contact conditions, or a required terminal configuration. A pair ( X, U ) is dynamically feasible when it satisfies equation (9.5); it is fully feasible when it also satisfies the path and terminal constraints. These constraints are imposed at the grid points; satisfying them there does not by itself guarantee safety between samples. Continuous-time validation or grid refinement may therefore be needed, especially for collision avoidance. A common tracking objective has the quadratic form

Trajectory optimization usually produces an open-loop nominal sequence ( ¯X, ¯U ). Later in this chapter, the iLQR and DDP sections augment this sequence with local feedforward and feedback 206 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

corrections. The idea is closely related to the reference-trajectory tracking construction in Chapter 7: the nonlinear dynamics are linearized along a nominal motion, and a time-varying feedback law responds to deviations from that motion. Here, however, the nominal trajectory and its local corrections are improved together as part of the trajectory-optimization iteration. The resulting feedback is local to the current nominal trajectory rather than defined over the entire state space. It is useful to distinguish three objects that will recur throughout the chapter. The sequence ( ¯X, ¯U ) is the current nominal trajectory . A perturbation ( δX, δU ) describes a candidate local change. The updated sequence is accepted only after it is evaluated using the original nonlinear dynamics, costs, and constraints. This separation between nominal model, local approximation, and nonlinear trial motion is central to shooting, iLQR, and DDP. The following examples illustrate the common ingredients before the numerical methods are developed in detail.

With limited torque, the optimizer may need to discover that temporarily moving away from the target can build the energy needed to reach it. This makes the problem nonlinear and nonconvex even though the effort penalty is quadratic.

In a shooting or iLQR implementation, one often begins with smooth obstacle penalties and then increases their weight, or uses the resulting trajectory to initialize a constrained solver. This example illustrates the typical role of trajectory optimization in robotics: it coordinates the whole body over time rather than selecting a sequence of independent inverse-kinematics solutions. 

9.2.2 Why the Problem Is Difficult 

If the states at different stages were independent, each stage could be optimized separately. The dynamics prevent this decomposition: changing uk changes xk+1 , which changes xk+2 , and so on through the terminal state. The quality of an action therefore depends not only on its immediate cost, but also on every future state that it influences. This temporal coupling becomes explicit when the dynamics are differentiated. Along a nominal trajectory, define 

Ak = fx,k (¯ xk, ¯uk), Bk = fu,k (¯ xk, ¯uk). (9.18) If only ui is perturbed and the initial state is fixed, the resulting state perturbations satisfy, to first order, 

δx i+1 = Biδu i, (9.19) 

δx i+2 = Ai+1 Biδu i, (9.20) 

δx j = Aj−1 · · · Ai+1 Biδu i, j > i. (9.21) The product is the identity when j = i + 1. Thus a single control perturbation propagates forward through a product of dynamics Jacobians and enters every subsequent stage cost. Applying the chain rule to equation (9.6) gives 

∇uî J = ℓu,i +  

> N−1∑
> j=i+1
> (

∂x j

∂u i

> )T

ℓx,j + 

> (

∂x N

∂u i

> )T

Φx. (9.22) Here ̂ J denotes the cost after eliminating the states through the dynamics, and cost gradients are column vectors. Forming these long sensitivity products separately for every pair ( i, j ) would be expensive and can be numerically ill-conditioned. Their repeated structure, however, makes it possible to collect future effects through an efficient backward adjoint or value-function recursion. Nonlinearity creates a second difficulty: the optimization landscape may contain several qualita-tively different local solutions. In pendulum swing-up, for example, one initialization may lead to a clockwise motion and another to a counterclockwise motion, while a symmetric or poorly chosen initialization may remain near the downward equilibrium. Discretization, cost weights, constraint representations, and numerical scaling can all change which solution the algorithm finds. These observations motivate two algorithmic principles. First, a method should exploit the chain structure of the dynamics rather than treat the trajectory as an unstructured collection of variables. 208 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

Second, it should test each proposed step on the original nonlinear problem before accepting it. Most nonlinear solvers obtain a step from a local linear or quadratic model around the current trajectory. Because this model neglects higher-order terms, it is reliable only within a sufficiently small neighborhood. A large step may reduce the approximate objective while increasing the true cost, violating constraints, or entering a region where the approximation is poor. Line searches, trust regions, and predicted-versus-actual improvement tests provide the required safeguard. Direct transcription and iLQR implement these two principles in different ways. 

# 9.3 Direct Optimization Methods 

9.3.1 Single Shooting 

In single shooting , the controls are the optimization variables. Given U , the states are generated by rolling the dynamics forward from the fixed initial condition. This defines a reduced objective ̂

J(U ) = J(X(U ), U ), (9.23) and the dynamics are satisfied automatically at every iterate. The computational process is therefore simple to state. Starting from a trial control sequence, simulate forward to obtain X(U ), evaluate the cost, compute a search direction in control space, and repeat. The main question is how to differentiate ̂ J without separately propagating the influence of every uk to every later state. To see how an early control affects later states, define the Jacobians along a nominal trajectory by 

Ak = ∂f k

This is the optimization analogue of exploding or vanishing sensitivities and helps explain why long-horizon shooting can be poorly conditioned. The gradient can be computed without explicitly forming every product in equation (9.26). Computationally, this backward sweep is reverse-mode differentiation through the forward dynamics rollout. 210 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

Once the gradient is available, even a basic update has the form 

U + = U − α∇̂J(U ), (9.36) with a line search for α. Quasi-Newton methods replace the identity in this update by an approximation of the inverse reduced Hessian. The important point is that the adjoint computes the gradient in O(N ) stages rather than forming the O(N 2) collection of state–control sensitivity blocks. Single shooting is compact and satisfies the discrete dynamics at every iterate. One adjoint sweep supplies the gradient for the entire control sequence, but does not remove the long-horizon sensitivity described in equation (9.26). Unstable dynamics can amplify early control changes, while strongly contracting dynamics can suppress their effect on distant objectives. The resulting reduced problem may require coordinated control changes and very small line-search steps. Single shooting therefore works best when the horizon is moderate and the forward rollout remains reasonably well-conditioned. 

9.3.2 Direct Transcription and Multiple Shooting 

Single shooting represents the trajectory through the control sequence alone: the states are generated by one forward rollout and are therefore eliminated from the optimization variables. Direct transcription takes the opposite view. It retains every sampled state, together with every control, and asks the nonlinear-programming solver to enforce dynamic consistency. These two formulations provide useful endpoints for understanding the tradeoff between problem size and numerical conditioning. Multiple shooting, introduced after their comparison, lies between them. 

Direct transcription. In direct transcription , both states and controls are optimization variables. The dynamics become equality constraints: 

dk(X, U ) = xk+1 − fk(xk, u k) = 0 , k = 0 , . . . , N − 1. (9.37) The resulting nonlinear program is minimize  

> X,U

Φ( xN ) +  

> N−1∑
> k=0

ℓk(xk, u k)subject to x0 = xinit ,dk(X, U ) = 0 , k = 0 , . . . , N − 1,gk(xk, u k) ≤ 0, hk(xk, u k) = 0 , k = 0 , . . . , N − 1,gN (xN ) ≤ 0, hN (xN ) = 0 .

(9.38) There are ( N + 1) n + N m scalar state and control variables before eliminating the fixed initial state, and N n scalar dynamic-defect equations. Unlike a shooting rollout, an intermediate iterate may have dk̸ = 0 and therefore need not represent a motion that the system can execute. The solver reduces the objective and the defect residuals together; a usable trajectory is recovered only Section 9.3 211 

when the defects and the remaining constraint violations are within tolerance. 

Sequential quadratic programming. A popular way to solve equation (9.38) is sequential quadratic programming (SQP). Stack the retained variables in temporal order, 

z = ( x0, u 0, x 1, u 1, . . . , x N −1, u N −1, x N ), (9.39) and write the transcription compactly as minimize  

> z

J(z) subject to c(z) = 0 , g(z) ≤ 0. (9.40) Here J(z) = Φ( xN ) + ∑N −1 

> k=0

ℓk(xk, u k), c(z) collects the initial-state condition, dynamic defects, and other equalities, and g(z) collects the path and terminal inequalities. The general SQP construction, including its QP subproblem, multiplier updates, curvature models, and globalization strategies, is developed in section 4.3.6. Applied to equation (9.40), each SQP iteration builds a quadratic model using Lagrangian curvature, linearizes the dynamic defects and other constraints, and solves the resulting structured QP for a trajectory correction. The essential issue specific to trajectory optimization is not the SQP derivation itself, but the temporal sparsity of this QP and its KKT system. 

Why sparsity makes SQP practical. Retaining every state creates a large problem, but its derivatives are local in time. Dynamic defect dk depends only on ( xk, u k, x k+1 ), so 

∂d k

∂(xk, u k, x k+1 ) =

> [

−Ak −Bk I

> ]

. (9.41) Stacking the defect rows produces the block-banded Jacobian 

> 

−A0 −B0 I

−A1 −B1 I

. . . . . . . . . 

> 

. (9.42) Stage costs and path constraints have a similar local structure. The KKT systems inside the quadratic-programming solver are therefore sparse and nearly block-banded rather than dense. Sparse factorization, Riccati-style recursions, or condensing exploit this chain structure without coupling every time step to every other time step. For fixed state and control dimensions, the computational work and memory can often grow approximately linearly with the horizon, although the exact cost depends on inequality handling and factorization fill-in. This sparsity is what makes direct transcription practical despite its larger number of explicit variables. 

Collocation. Direct transcription requires a finite-dimensional equation connecting neighboring states. A simulation step such as forward Euler is one option; collocation derives this equation by approximating the state within each interval and enforcing the differential equation at selected 212 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

points. Starting from the exact integral relation 

xk+1 − xk = 

> ∫tk+1
> tk

fc(t, x (t), u (t)) dt, (9.43) and applying trapezoidal quadrature with a zero-order-held control gives 

xk+1 − xk − ∆tk

2 [fc(tk, x k, u k) + fc(tk+1 , x k+1 , u k)] = 0 . (9.44) Unlike a one-sided Euler defect, this equation couples both endpoints and has second-order integration accuracy under standard smoothness assumptions. Because xk+1 appears inside fc,the equation is implicit and is enforced by the nonlinear-programming solver rather than by an explicit forward rollout. Higher-order Hermite–Simpson or Runge–Kutta collocation schemes introduce internal stage variables and enforce additional residuals. They provide more accurate continuous-time approximations per interval, at the cost of more variables and denser local blocks. 

Comparison with single shooting. When they use the same transition map and cost dis-cretization, single shooting and direct transcription solve the same discretized control problem, but expose different variables to the optimizer. After fixing x0, single shooting optimizes only the 

N m control variables and obtains all states from the rollout map X = X(U ). Direct transcription instead retains N n + N m state and control variables and imposes N n dynamic-defect equations. The additional variables do not add physical degrees of freedom: once all defects vanish, the states are again determined by the initial condition and the controls. They change how the numerical solver reaches that feasible trajectory. The compactness of single shooting is attractive. Every iterate satisfies the discrete dynamics exactly, and evaluating the objective requires only a forward simulation. Its main weakness is that an early control can influence a distant state only through a long product of dynamics Jacobians. Although an adjoint pass computes the gradient with respect to the entire control sequence efficiently, it does not remove this sensitivity. Long or unstable rollouts can therefore produce an ill-conditioned reduced problem, and state or path constraints are imposed only through the states generated by the rollout. Direct transcription replaces these long-range dependencies by local sparse constraints. Each defect couples only ( xk, u k, x k+1 ), so the optimizer can change a distant state directly and then coordinate neighboring changes to restore dynamic consistency. This is often advantageous for long horizons, unstable dynamics, contact events, and problems with many state constraints. The cost is a larger constrained problem: intermediate iterates may be dynamically infeasible, defect residuals must be monitored, and efficient solution depends on sparse KKT or SQP linear algebra. Thus the practical choice is not between an exact and an approximate dynamics model. It is a choice between eliminating the dynamics and retaining them as structured constraints. 

Multiple shooting as a compromise. Multiple shooting retains states only at selected segment boundaries. Choose 0 = i0 < i 1 < · · · < i S = N (9.45) Section 9.3 213 

and introduce zs ≈ xis , with z0 = xinit . Starting from zs, simulate the original dynamics over segment s using its controls Us = ( uis , . . . , u is+1 −1). If Ψ s(zs, U s) denotes the simulated endpoint, neighboring segments are joined by the continuity constraints 

zs+1 − Ψs(zs, U s) = 0 , s = 0 , . . . , S − 1. (9.46) The method therefore preserves dynamically feasible rollouts inside each segment while permitting temporary gaps between segments. Linearizing a continuity constraint gives 

δz s+1 − Fsδz s − GsδU s = −cs, Fs = ∂Ψs

∂z s

, Gs = ∂Ψs

∂U s

, (9.47) where cs = zs+1 − Ψs(zs, U s) is the current gap. The sensitivity Fs contains Jacobian products only across one segment rather than the full horizon. Longer segments use fewer variables and approach single shooting; shorter segments reduce the sensitivity length but introduce more boundary states and continuity constraints. With one segment, the formulation reduces to single shooting after eliminating its endpoint state. With one time step per segment, it becomes essentially direct transcription. Segment length can therefore be chosen to balance compactness against conditioning. 

9.3.3 Feasibility, Initialization, and Scaling 

A dynamically feasible initial guess is easy to obtain for shooting: choose a control sequence and simulate. Its resulting motion may nevertheless be far from the task or violate state constraints. Transcription permits a more task-informed state guess, such as interpolating between initial and desired configurations, but this guess generally has nonzero defects. Neither type of initialization is universally superior. A useful strategy is often to solve an easier problem first and gradually introduce difficulty. One may shorten the horizon, relax terminal requirements, reduce obstacle penalties, or begin with a nearby target. This practice is called 

continuation or homotopy .Scaling is equally important. If position errors are measured in millimeters, angles in radians, forces in newtons, and time in seconds, raw derivatives may have very different magnitudes. Nondimensionalization or explicit variable and constraint scaling can substantially improve condi-tioning. Cost weights should express the desired tradeoff after this scaling rather than compensate accidentally for inconsistent units. For example, introduce characteristic scales Sx and Su and normalized variables 

> ˜

x = S−1

x, ˜u = S−1 

> u

u. (9.48) The normalized dynamics have Jacobians 

> ˜

Ak = S−1 

> x

AkSx, ˜Bk = S−1 

> x

BkSu. (9.49) Choosing scales so typical normalized variables are of order one helps prevent the linear systems inside Newton, SQP, and Riccati-type steps from being dominated by units rather than by the 214 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

Table 9.2: Common trajectory-optimization formulations. # 9.4 Iterative Linear–Quadratic Methods 

The formulations above can be handled by generic nonlinear-programming algorithms. Iterative linear–quadratic methods take a more specialized route: they preserve the time ordering of the control problem and solve each local model by a backward dynamic-programming recursion followed by a forward simulation. This structure is the source of their efficiency. 

9.4.1 Local Models Around a Nominal Trajectory 

Iterative LQR repeatedly solves a local approximation of the nonlinear problem. Begin with a nominal control sequence ¯U and roll out the dynamics to obtain a dynamically feasible nominal trajectory ¯X. Define deviations 

δx k = xk − ¯xk, δu k = uk − ¯uk. (9.50) Linearizing the dynamics gives 

δx k+1 ≈ Akδx k + Bkδu k. (9.51) This relation follows from the Taylor expansion 

fk(¯ xk + δx k, ¯uk + δu k) = fk(¯ xk, ¯uk) + Akδx k + Bkδu k + O(∥(δx k, δu k)∥2). (9.52) Because the nominal rollout is feasible, ¯ xk+1 = fk(¯ xk, ¯uk). Subtracting this identity produces equation (9.51). If the nominal sequence had a nonzero dynamic defect, an additional affine term would remain. Section 9.4 215

The condition δx 0 = 0 reflects the fixed initial state; it can instead be replaced by a prescribed initial deviation. Although the objective is written as a sum of stage terms, the dynamics couple the stages. A change in δu k alters δx k+1 and therefore all subsequent running costs and the terminal cost. The iLQR backward pass solves this coupled problem efficiently by summarizing the remaining cost in a quadratic value function.

9.4.3 The Nonlinear Forward Pass 

The backward pass optimizes only a local model. Its proposed policy must be tested on the original nonlinear dynamics. For a step length α ∈ (0 , 1], set xnew 0 = xinit and apply 

unew  

> k

= ¯ uk + αk k + Kk(xnew  

> k

− ¯xk), (9.75) 

xnew  

> k+1

= fk(xnew  

> k

, u new  

> k

). (9.76) If the new cost is sufficiently smaller, the rollout becomes the next nominal trajectory. Otherwise, 

α is reduced and the forward pass is tried again. This line search prevents a locally accurate quadratic model from taking an unreliable large step. The feedforward and feedback terms play different roles. Multiplying kk by α controls how far the nominal control is moved. The feedback term is not normally scaled because it is the local response to whatever state the nonlinear rollout actually reaches. Thus the forward pass evaluates a policy, not merely a vector addition in the stacked control variables. One iLQR iteration therefore consists of: 1. rolling the nonlinear dynamics forward to obtain ( ¯X, ¯U ); 2. differentiating the dynamics and costs along that trajectory; 3. performing the backward recursion to compute ( kk, K k); 4. line-searching with nonlinear forward rollouts; and 5. accepting the improved trajectory and repeating until convergence. The feedback term in equation (9.75) matters even though the initial state is fixed. As the feedforward controls change, the nonlinear rollout departs from the nominal states used in the backward pass. Feedback corrects these departures and usually makes the trial trajectory much more reliable than a purely open-loop update. 

9.4.4 Regularization and Predicted Improvement 

Far from a local minimum, Quu may fail to be positive definite. Then the quadratic model does not define a unique minimizing control step. A common remedy is to use 

Qreg  

> uu

= Quu + μI, μ > 0, (9.77) with μ large enough to make Qreg  

> uu

positive definite. Another option is to regularize the value Hessian before forming the Q coefficients. Large μ produces conservative steps; successful steps permit μ to decrease, while rejected steps cause it to increase. Regularized gains need consistent value updates. The Schur-complement formulas in equa-tions (9.73) and (9.74) assume that the gains minimize the same quadratic model being propagated. Section 9.4 219

A small positive ratio means the step helped less than expected; a negative ratio means the cost increased. This ratio is useful only when the predicted decrease is positive and not numerically negligible. An implementation may accept only steps with ρ above a small threshold, decrease μ after consistently accurate steps, and increase μ after rejection. This couples line search and regularization into a trust mechanism for the local model.

Vx and Vxx are passed to the preceding stage in the backward recursion. DDP uses the same nonlinear forward rollout and line search as iLQR. The difference has a useful optimization interpretation. Omitting the dynamics-curvature con-tractions makes iLQR similar to a Gauss–Newton method: it keeps curvature generated by the cost and by first-order state sensitivities, but not every term in the exact Hessian of the reduced objective. DDP is closer to Newton’s method because it retains those missing terms. Near a smooth local solution, this can make its model more accurate and its convergence faster. The added accuracy has a price. The tensors ( fi)xx , ( fi)ux , and ( fi)uu are expensive to form and store for a high-dimensional robot. In practice, automatic differentiation can compute the required tensor contractions without materializing every tensor. DDP also still requires regularization whenever its Quu is not positive definite; exact second-order information does not guarantee that a nonconvex local model is suitable for an unrestricted step. iLQR requires fewer dynamics derivatives, while DDP can be useful when improved local accuracy justifies the additional cost. The regularization and value-update precautions above apply to both methods. To see why only the Hessian blocks change, write the future value composition as V +(f (x, u )). Its first derivative is the ordinary chain rule

# 9.5 Constraints and Practical Implementation 

The derivation so far assumes smooth dynamics, smooth costs, and unconstrained local control updates. Robotic applications rarely satisfy all three assumptions. The following subsections explain how constraints and contact modify the basic algorithms and then collect the numerical checks needed in a robust implementation. 

9.5.1 Input and State Constraints 

Simple box constraints 

umin ≤ uk ≤ umax (9.91) can be incorporated into each local control minimization. Instead of applying equation (9.72) directly, one solves the small box-constrained quadratic problem min  

> δu

QT 

> u

δu + δu TQux δx + 12 δu TQuu δu subject to umin ≤ ¯u + δu ≤ umax . (9.92) If Quu ≻ 0 and the unconstrained candidate lies strictly within the input bounds, no bound is active and the solution is exactly the affine law in equation (9.72): 

δu ⋆ = −Q−1 

> uu

(Qu + Qux δx ) = k + Kδx, umin < ¯u + k + Kδx < u max . (9.93) If the candidate violates an input bound, solve equation (9.92) using a quadratic-programming (QP) algorithm. Simply clipping the unconstrained control can leave the feedback gain inconsistent with the active bounds. State constraints are harder because they couple current actions to future feasibility. Smooth penalties or barrier functions can be added to the cost, but penalties permit violation and barriers require a feasible interior. Augmented-Lagrangian and sequential quadratic programming methods provide stronger constraint handling. When collision, contact, and terminal constraints dominate the problem, direct transcription is often more natural than basic iLQR. For a scalar inequality g(x, u ) ≤ 0, a quadratic penalty adds 

ℓpen (x, u ) = ℓ(x, u ) + η

2 max {0, g (x, u )}2. (9.94) 222 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

For several inequalities, sum one such term per constraint. Increasing η discourages violation but can make the Hessian poorly conditioned. The squared positive-part penalty is continuously differentiable, but its second derivative generally jumps at g = 0; a second-order solver must handle this change or use a smooth approximation. A logarithmic barrier instead uses 

ℓbar (x, u ) = ℓ(x, u ) − τ log[ −g(x, u )] , g(x, u ) < 0, (9.95) with τ > 0. Reducing τ weakens the barrier bias, but every trial trajectory must remain strictly feasible for the barrier to be defined. For an equality hk(xk, u k) = 0, the augmented-Lagrangian approach adds a multiplier term and a quadratic penalty to the original stage cost: 

> ˜

ℓk(x, u ; νk, η ) = ℓk(x, u ) + νT 

> k

hk(x, u ) + η
