νk, with one entry for each equality at that stage. Equality multipliers can have either sign. 

Choosing and updating the parameters. A simple initialization is ν(0)  

> k

= 0 and a moderate positive penalty η(0) . There is no universal numerical value for η: first scale the constraint residuals to comparable magnitudes, then choose a penalty that makes meaningful violations costly without overwhelming the original objective. If a nearby problem has already been solved, its multipliers can provide a warm start. At outer iteration r, hold ν(r) 

> k

and η(r) fixed while the inner trajectory solver approximately minimizes the augmented objective. After obtaining the updated trajectory ( X(r+1) , U (r+1) ), update 

ν(r+1)  

> k

= ν(r) 

> k

+ η(r)hk(x(r+1)  

> k

, u (r+1)  

> k

). (9.97) Keep η unchanged if the constraint residuals decrease sufficiently; otherwise increase it by a factor greater than one and repeat the inner solve. These are outer-loop updates, not updates at every iLQR backward pass or line-search trial. Starting with an excessively large penalty can make the local quadratic models poorly conditioned. The multiplier update accumulates a correction for persistent constraint violation, so feasibility need not be pursued solely by sending the penalty to infinity. Under suitable convergence assumptions, the multipliers approach the KKT multipliers of the constrained optimum. In practice, stop only when both the constraint residuals and the augmented-objective stationarity residuals are small; a low augmented cost alone does not establish feasibility. 

9.5.2 Trajectory Optimization Through Contact Dynamics 

Contact-rich motions are naturally viewed as hybrid systems : continuous motion within a contact mode is interrupted by events such as touchdown, liftoff, or changes between sticking and sliding. Impacts may reset velocity, and different contact modes impose different dynamics and constraints. Section 9.5 223 

Optimizing a motion can therefore require choosing not only controls and states, but also the contact sequence and transition times. If the contact sequence is known, a hybrid trajectory formulation can optimize the motion within each phase and enforce transition conditions between phases. This simplifies the search, but cannot discover motions requiring a different sequence. Contact-implicit trajectory optimization instead retains contact forces or impulses as decision variables and imposes contact consistency alongside the dynamics. Unilateral contact and complementarity conditions express that separated bodies cannot exert a normal contact force, allowing contact activation to emerge from the optimization rather than from a prescribed schedule. 

A representative contact-implicit problem. Consider Euclidean generalized coordinates 

q, with v = ˙ q, and contact with stationary surfaces. For candidate contact j, let ϕj (q) be the signed gap, positive when the bodies are separated. Introduce the normal and tangential contact impulses pn,j,k and pt,j,k over interval k, and stack them in pk. Impulses are integrated contact forces, so they can represent both sustained contact and impact within a time step. An illustrative backward-Euler transcription for inelastic contact is minimize  

> Q,V,U,P

Φ( qN , v N ) +  

> N−1

∑

> k=0

ℓk(qk, v k, u k)subject to q0 = qinit , v0 = vinit ,qk+1 = qk + ∆ tkvk+1 ,Mk+1 (vk+1 − vk) = ∆ tkrk+1 + JT

> k+1

pk,ϕj (qk+1 ) ≥ 0, pn,j,k ≥ 0,pn,j,k ϕj (qk+1 ) = 0 ,

∥pt,j,k ∥2 ≤ μj pn,j,k ,pT

> t,j,k

wt,j,k +1 = −μj pn,j,k ∥wt,j,k +1 ∥2,gk(qk, v k, u k) ≤ 0, hN (qN , v N ) = 0 .

(9.98) Here Q = ( q0, . . . , q N ), V = ( v0, . . . , v N ), P = ( p0, . . . , p N −1), and U is the control sequence. The interval constraints hold for k = 0 , . . . , N − 1, and the contact constraints hold for every candidate contact j. The quantities Mk+1 = M (qk+1 ), Jk+1 = J(qk+1 ), and rk+1 = r(qk+1 , v k+1 , u k) denote the mass matrix, stacked contact Jacobian, and noncontact generalized forces. The tangential relative velocity is wt,j,k +1 = Jt,j (qk+1 )vk+1 , and μj ≥ 0 is the friction coefficient. The functions 

gk and hN encode task-specific bounds and terminal requirements. This is one discretization of contact-implicit optimization, not a unique contact model; see Posa et al. [2014]. The normal-contact conditions are often abbreviated as 0 ≤ pn,j,k ⊥ ϕj (qk+1 ) ≥ 0.

They encode a choice: either the gap is positive and the normal impulse is zero, or the gap is zero and a nonnegative normal impulse is possible. Thus the optimizer selects contact modes without explicit binary variables. The two friction constraints express Coulomb friction with maximum 224 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

dissipation: during sliding, the tangential impulse lies on the friction-cone boundary and opposes tangential velocity; during sticking, it can lie anywhere inside the cone. 

Why the constraints are difficult. The momentum equation is nonlinear because M , J, and 

r depend on the unknown state, and JTp couples configuration and contact impulse. The gap functions are generally nonlinear geometric quantities. Complementarity adds product equalities such as pn,j,k ϕj (qk+1 ) = 0, whose feasible set is nonconvex even when the gap is affine. For example, both ( ϕ, p n) = (1 , 0) and (0 , 1) satisfy complementarity, but their midpoint does not. The friction-cone inequality alone is convex in the impulses for fixed μj ; the dissipation equality couples impulses to velocity and is nonsmooth at zero tangential velocity. Together, these conditions create a nonlinear contact-constrained problem, not a QP with simple input bounds. Such formulations are commonly called mathematical programs with complementarity constraints 

(MPCCs). Contact-implicit optimization therefore does not eliminate the hybrid nature of the problem. Initialization, complementarity relaxation or continuation, and time-grid refinement remain important. A computed motion should be checked for contact consistency, friction feasibility, and sensitivity to discretization. Smooth contact models, such as those discussed in Chapter 5, offer another route to local trajectory optimization, but trade numerical smoothness against faithful impact behavior. For algorithmic details, see the complementarity-based direct method of Posa et al. [2014] and the contact-implicit orthogonal-collocation method of Patel et al. [2019]. Both extend direct transcription to contact-rich motion without prescribing the complete contact sequence. The key distinction from basic iLQR is that contact-mode changes require appropriate hybrid, contact-implicit, or regularized modeling rather than an assumption of smooth dynamics across every transition. 

9.5.3 Derivatives and Model Checks 

Analytic derivatives are fast and interpretable but can be tedious for a large robot model. Automatic differentiation evaluates derivatives of the implemented dynamics and costs and is usually preferable to differentiating a separate symbolic model. Finite differences remain useful for verification, although they require careful step sizes and can be noisy. A simple directional check compares 

f (x + ϵv, u ) − f (x, u )

ϵ with fx(x, u )v (9.99) for several random directions v and decreasing ϵ. Similar checks apply to cost gradients and Hessian-vector products. Incorrect derivatives often appear as consistently rejected line searches or a large mismatch between predicted and actual cost decrease. A stronger check examines the Taylor remainder. If A = fx(x, u ) is correct, then 

r1(ϵ) = ∥f (x + ϵv, u ) − f (x, u )∥ = O(ϵ), (9.100) Section 9.5 225 

whereas subtracting the linear prediction should give 

r2(ϵ) = ∥f (x + ϵv, u ) − f (x, u ) − ϵAv ∥ = O(ϵ2). (9.101) For generic directions with nonzero leading terms, a log–log plot of these residuals should have slopes one and two over an appropriate range of step sizes. For extremely small ϵ, floating-point cancellation eventually dominates, so the test should use a range of step sizes rather than a single finite difference. Nonsmooth simulation requires special care. Contact switches, saturation, lookup-table disconti-nuities, and angle-wrapping operations may invalidate ordinary derivatives. Smoothing, hybrid formulations, or nonsmooth optimization methods may be necessary. Automatic differentiation faithfully differentiates the code path that was executed; it does not make a discontinuous model smooth. 

9.5.4 Numerical Design Choices 

Several practical choices often matter as much as the formal algorithm. 

Discretization. A smaller sampling interval can improve integration accuracy, but increases the number of stages and computation for a fixed physical horizon. Higher-order integration can improve accuracy without as many stages, but its derivatives must also be computed correctly. The optimization and the final controller should use compatible models and sampling conventions. 

Cost design. Very large terminal weights encourage accuracy but can create poor conditioning; they do not impose an exact terminal constraint. Very small control weights can demand unrealistic actions and make Quu nearly singular. Smooth geometric costs, such as periodic angle errors, are usually preferable to coordinate expressions with artificial discontinuities. 

Initialization. Useful guesses can come from a simpler model, a previous solution, a motion library, or a gradual continuation in task difficulty. In receding-horizon control, shifting the previous solution by one time step provides a natural warm start. 

Termination. No single test is sufficient. Useful criteria include small cost reduction, small feedforward steps, small gradient norms, acceptable constraint violation, and agreement between predicted and actual improvement. A solver should also report failure when regularization grows excessively or no line search step is accepted. 

Complexity. With dense state and control matrices, an iLQR backward pass is linear in the horizon length and polynomial in n and m. It avoids solving one dense problem in all controls at once. Direct transcription also scales linearly in horizon at the sparsity-pattern level, but its sparse linear algebra has different constants and handles general constraints more directly. 226 Chapter 9. Discrete-Time Trajectory Optimization 

9.5.5 Choosing a Method 

Single shooting is attractive for small, smooth, well-conditioned problems. Multiple shooting improves robustness on unstable or long-horizon dynamics. Direct transcription is well suited to problems with many state and path constraints. iLQR and DDP are especially effective for smooth nonlinear dynamics with modest control dimension and costs that admit useful local quadratic models. These methods are not mutually exclusive. An iLQR solution can initialize a constrained transcrip-tion, while a coarse transcription can provide a motion around which iLQR constructs fast local feedback. The right formulation is the one that exposes the structure most important for the task. 

Exercises. Exercise 9.3. Contrast indirect single shooting from Chapter 6 with direct single shooting in this chapter. What is guessed or optimized in each method, and what equations are integrated? 

Short solution. Indirect shooting guesses unknown boundary data such as the initial costate, integrates the PMP state–costate equations, and uses root finding to satisfy terminal conditions. Direct shooting optimizes a sampled control sequence, integrates only the original state dynamics, and minimizes the discretized cost. The indirect result is an extremal of the continuous-time necessary conditions. The direct optimizer seeks a local minimum of the discretized problem, although convergence by itself may establish only a stationary candidate.

fk(xk, u k) = 0. There are ( N + 1) n + N m scalar variables, or N n + N m free variables if the fixed initial state is eliminated.

Exercise 9.10. Explain why one unregularized iLQR backward–forward iteration solves an unconstrained finite-horizon LQR problem when the line search accepts α = 1. 

Short solution. For linear dynamics and quadratic costs, the iLQR local models are exact rather than approximations. The backward pass is precisely the affine Riccati recursion, and the forward pass applies its exact minimizing policy. Hence no further local-model correction is required. 

Exercise 9.11. Why is the feedback term Kk(xnew  

> k

− ¯xk) useful during an iLQR line search even though x0 is fixed? 

Short solution. The changed feedforward controls and nonlinear dynamics make later trial states differ from the nominal states. The feedback term responds to these deviations using the local value model, keeping the rollout closer to the improvement predicted by the backward pass.

Short solution. DDP retains contractions of the next value gradient with second derivatives of the dynamics, such as ∑

> i

> x

Exercise 9.14. Show that the DDP curvature terms in equations (9.83) to (9.85) vanish when the dynamics are affine in state and control. What does this imply about iLQR and DDP for an LQR problem? 

Short solution. Affine dynamics have ( fi)xx = ( fi)ux = ( fi)uu = 0 for every component i. Therefore the DDP Q matrices reduce to the iLQR matrices. For linear dynamics and quadratic costs, both methods use the exact local problem and reproduce the finite-horizon LQR solution. 

Exercise 9.15. Why can prescribing a contact sequence simplify trajectory optimization, and what flexibility does a contact-implicit formulation provide? 

Short solution. A prescribed sequence fixes the order of hybrid modes, so the optimizer only needs to determine the motion and transition times within that sequence. A contact-implicit formulation imposes contact consistency without fixing the complete sequence, allowing different contact patterns to emerge at the cost of a more difficult nonconvex problem. 

Exercise 9.16. Why can a direct-transcription iterate have low objective value but still fail to represent a usable motion? 

Short solution. The independently optimized states may violate the dynamic defects. Until 

xk+1 = fk(xk, u k) is satisfied to adequate tolerance, the listed states cannot be produced by applying the listed controls to the system. 

Exercise 9.17. For the pendulum swing-up cost in equation (9.15), explain why initializing iLQR with θk = 0, ωk = 0, and uk = 0 can be problematic. 

Short solution. At the downward equilibrium, the derivative of 1 + cos θ is − sin θ = 0. The symmetric zero trajectory therefore has no first-order signal choosing clockwise or counterclockwise motion. A small asymmetric control initialization, a reference trajectory, or continuation can break the symmetry. Section 9.5 229 

Chapter summary. Trajectory optimization searches for a finite state–control sequence rather than a global value function. The controls are coupled through the dynamics: an early action changes every later state and can strongly affect terminal performance. Unlike the indirect methods of Chapter 6, the direct methods in this chapter discretize the original control problem before optimization rather than first deriving and solving the PMP boundary-value equations. Direct single shooting eliminates states by forward simulation and can compute gradients efficiently with a backward adjoint recursion, but long products of dynamics Jacobians may cause poor conditioning. Multiple shooting and direct transcription introduce state variables and enforce dynamic continuity with sparse equality constraints. They are often preferable when horizons are long, dynamics are unstable, or state and path constraints are central. Their intermediate iterates may be dynamically infeasible, so defect residuals must be checked alongside objective value. iLQR exploits the same temporal structure through a different route. It linearizes the dynamics, quadratizes the costs, and performs a Riccati-like backward pass to obtain feedforward improve-ments and local feedback gains. A nonlinear forward rollout and line search test the proposed policy on the original system. Regularization ensures that the local control minimization is well posed, while predicted-versus-actual improvement reveals when the local model is trustworthy. DDP refines this construction by retaining second-order dynamics terms, producing a more Newton-like local model at the cost of additional derivatives and regularization. Robotic examples show why several formulations are useful. Manipulator motion planning combines nonlinear dynamics, torque bounds, and geometric collision constraints. Contact-rich motions have hybrid dynamics, with contact transitions and possible impact resets. A prescribed mode sequence simplifies planning, while contact-implicit transcription allows contact patterns to emerge from the optimization. Both require careful initialization, contact-consistency checks, and appropriate handling of nonsmooth behavior. No formulation removes nonconvexity. Initialization, discretization, scaling, derivatives, and constraint handling remain decisive. Nevertheless, exploiting the chain structure makes substantial nonlinear control problems tractable. The next chapter places these finite-horizon solvers inside a feedback loop: model predictive control repeatedly reoptimizes the trajectory as new state measurements arrive. 10 

# Model Predictive Control 

Chapter map. Receding-horizon feedback, prediction and control horizons, linear and nonlinear MPC, state and input constraints, terminal ingredients, recursive feasibility, nominal stability, warm starts, state estimation, real-time implementation, a robot braking example, representative robotics applications, and whole-body NMPC for humanoid locomotion. 

Chapter 9 developed algorithms for computing finite state–control trajectories. A computed trajectory is a plan based on an assumed initial state, model, and set of constraints. During execution, however, a robot may encounter an unexpected load, a moving obstacle, or a new target. Executing the entire control sequence without correction ignores this new information. 

Model predictive control (MPC) turns finite-horizon optimization into feedback. At each sampling instant, the controller estimates the current state and optimizes a sequence of future controls. It applies only the first control, then measures again and solves a new problem over a shifted horizon. This repeated plan–act–measure–replan cycle is also called receding-horizon control. Replanning allows the robot to respond to changes in its state, task, and environment. It can also compensate for observed deviations caused by disturbances or model error. The effectiveness of this feedback depends on sensing, prediction quality, computation time, and available control authority; repeated optimization alone does not guarantee robustness. MPC is therefore a control architecture, not a particular numerical solver. The shooting, transcrip-tion, SQP, and iLQR methods of Chapter 9 can all serve as its computational engine. The central questions are how far ahead to predict, how to preserve feasibility and stability, and how to close the loop when computation and prediction are imperfect. Standard references are Mayne et al. [2000], Rawlings et al. [2017]; the discussion below emphasizes the connections to the preceding chapters and to robotic implementation. 

# 10.1 Receding-Horizon Control 

230 Section 10.1 231

We first explain the ideal setting, then account for state estimation, disturbances, and computation delays. At sample k, distinguish the actual state xk from a predicted state xi|k. 10.1.2 Prediction and Control Horizons 

The prediction horizon is the number N of future model transitions. With uniform sampling, it corresponds to physical duration Tp = N h . Increasing N while keeping h fixed lets the controller see farther ahead, but increases the optimization size. Decreasing h while keeping Tp fixed improves temporal resolution but also increases the number of stages. These are different design changes and should not be confused. The horizon should capture the important consequences of the first action. For braking, this includes the time needed to dissipate momentum. For manipulator motion, it may include a turn around an obstacle or a future actuator limitation. A horizon that is too short can make a locally cheap action attractive even when it leads toward a difficult future state. A terminal cost helps represent consequences beyond the horizon, but an arbitrary terminal weight does not automatically provide a correct long-term model. The control horizon Nu ≤ N describes how many future control moves are independently parame-terized. One simple restriction is 

ui|k = uNu−1|k, i = Nu, . . . , N − 1. (10.4) Other parameterizations use piecewise-constant blocks or a prescribed local feedback law after the independently optimized moves. This reduces the number of decision variables, but restricts the feasible control sequences. It can therefore change both performance and feasibility. The control horizon is not the number of controls executed before measuring again. Standard MPC still executes only one move. Executing several moves between optimization updates is possible, but reduces the frequency at which new information enters the feedback loop. Finally, distinguish a receding horizon from a shrinking horizon . In receding-horizon regulation, 

N usually stays fixed and the terminal sample moves from k + N to k + 1 + N . For a task with a fixed deadline kf , the horizon may instead shrink as Nk = kf − k. The latter keeps the same terminal time and is a different finite-horizon feedback problem. Section 10.2 233 

10.1.3 MPC as Feedback 

For a fixed model, reference, and constraint specification, the optimizer defines an implicit state-feedback law, denoted by κN . Given a state x, this law returns the first optimized input from the finite-horizon problem initialized at x:

κN (x) = u⋆

> 0

(x), xk+1 = f (xk, κ N (xk)). (10.5) Here N is the horizon length, and the index 0 identifies the first input in the optimized sequence. The policy is defined wherever the optimization is feasible, with a selection rule if the minimizer is not unique. Although each prediction may use an open-loop control sequence, the implemented controller is feedback: each new input depends on the latest state through a new optimization. This distinction connects MPC to Chapter 8. Dynamic programming seeks a value function and feedback law over a state domain. MPC instead evaluates a finite-horizon optimization at the states actually encountered. It avoids constructing a global state-space grid, at the cost of online computation and an approximation of the consequences beyond the horizon. 

The linear–quadratic special case. For regulation of xi+1 |k = Ax i|k + Bu i|k, consider

uk = −K0xk. (10.7) It does not successively apply K0, K 1, . . . from an old finite-horizon plan. If Pf = P∞ is the stabilizing DARE solution from equation (8.63), the Riccati recursion stays at that fixed point, and every gain equals the infinite-horizon LQR gain. In this case unconstrained MPC reproduces LQR for any N ≥ 1. 

# 10.2 Constraints and Stability 

10.2.1 State and Input Constraints 

MPC imposes restrictions on the entire predicted motion. Input constraints represent actuator limits; state constraints represent joint ranges, speed limits, obstacle clearance, or admissible contact configurations. Mixed constraints couple state and input. For example, a motor’s available torque may decrease with joint speed because of voltage limits. The optimizer must account for these future restrictions when choosing the current input. Including actuator limits during optimization is more informative than saturating an unconstrained 234 Chapter 10. Model Predictive Control 

command afterward. Clipping a planned force changes the motion on which future constraint predictions were based. An optimizer that includes the limit can instead plan braking or rerouting before the available control authority becomes insufficient. 

Hard constraints and soft constraints. A hard constraint must be satisfied by every accepted solution. A soft constraint permits an explicit violation through a slack variable. For example, a scalar performance constraint g(x, u ) ≤ 0 can be replaced by 

g(xi|k, u i|k) ≤ si|k, si|k ≥ 0, (10.8) with the stage-cost addition 

ρ1si|k + 12 ρ2s2

> i|k

, ρ1 > 0, ρ2 ≥ 0. (10.9) Slack can prevent a tracking or comfort specification from making the problem infeasible, but it does not relax the remaining hard constraints. A positive slack represents a planned violation of the original requirement. Physical actuator limits should remain hard; softening collision constraints likewise changes the meaning of a feasible solution. 

Input increments. Rapid changes in the control input may excite unmodeled dynamics. Define ∆ui|k = ui|k − ui−1|k, u−1|k = uapplied  

> k−1

. (10.10) Penalizing ∆ ui|k discourages abrupt command changes; bounding it imposes an explicit limit on the change between successive inputs. The first increment must be measured relative to the input actually applied during the previous interval, not a planned command that was never executed. 

Predicted feasibility is not physical safety. Feasibility in equation (10.2) does not guarantee safety between samples or in the presence of model error, estimation error, and delays. Constraint margins, intersample checks, and supervisory safeguards therefore require separate consideration. 

10.2.2 Terminal Costs and Terminal Sets 

The finite horizon omits everything after its final predicted state. A terminal cost Φ estimates the consequences of that omitted future. A terminal set Xf can require the prediction to finish in a region where a known local controller can continue safely. To make this idea precise, consider time-invariant regulation with the target shifted to the origin, 

f (0 , 0) = 0. Use fixed state and input sets X and U, fixed mixed constraints g(x, u ) ≤ 0, a Section 10.2 235 

nonnegative stage cost ℓ, and ℓ(0 , 0) = Φ(0) = 0. Suppose a terminal controller κf satisfies 

x ∈ X f ⊆ X =⇒ κf (x) ∈ U , g(x, κ f (x)) ≤ 0, (10.11) 

f (x, κ f (x)) ∈ X f , x ∈ X f , (10.12) Φ(f (x, κ f (x)) ) − Φ( x) ≤ − ℓ(x, κ f (x)), x ∈ X f . (10.13) The first two conditions mean that the terminal region can be kept admissible by the local controller. The last condition means the terminal cost pays for at least the next stage of that continuation. These are sufficient conditions for the nominal argument below. Other MPC designs can establish feasibility or stability without an explicit terminal set. 

Constructing terminal ingredients from LQR. For the linear system, let P∞ solve the DARE and let K∞ be the corresponding gain. Choose Φ( x) = 12 xTP∞x, κf (x) = −K∞x. (10.14) The DARE implies the closed-loop identity (A − BK ∞)TP∞(A − BK ∞) − P∞ = −Q − KT

> ∞

RK ∞. (10.15) Multiplying by xT/2 and x gives equation (10.13) with equality for the quadratic stage cost. The remaining task is to choose a positively invariant terminal set in which the state and input constraints hold. A sufficiently small ellipsoid Xf = {x : xTP∞x ≤ c} is one possible choice when P∞ ≻ 0 and the constraints admit a neighborhood of the origin. An invariant polytope is another option. An ellipsoidal terminal constraint is a quadratic inequality, so the resulting optimization is not a pure QP with only affine constraints. For a nonlinear robot, a local LQR design is a useful starting point, but admissibility, invariance, and cost decrease must be verified for the nonlinear transition map, not only its linearization. 

Why a terminal weight matters. Repeated optimization alone does not ensure stability. Consider the scalar system x+ = ax + u with a > 1, prediction horizon N = 1, and cost 

J1(x, u ) = 12 qx 2 + 12 ru 2, q, r > 0. (10.16) There is no penalty on the next state, so the unique minimizing input is u = 0. Replanning this same problem at every sample leaves the unstable dynamics x+ = ax unchanged. Adding the terminal cost Φ( x+) = s(x+)2/2, with s ≥ 0, gives 

u⋆ = − as r + s x, x+ = ar r + s x. (10.17) The closed loop is stable precisely when s > r (a − 1) in this example. This calculation illustrates why the tail of the prediction matters; it does not supply a universal terminal-weight rule for 236 Chapter 10. Model Predictive Control 

nonlinear constrained systems. A large arbitrary terminal penalty is not a substitute for checking the required terminal properties. 

10.2.3 Recursive Feasibility and Stability Intuition 

Recursive feasibility means that a feasible MPC problem remains feasible after the next control is applied. Under an exact model and fixed constraints, the terminal conditions above provide a simple explanation: remove the input just executed, shift the remaining plan forward, and append the terminal control. The predicted next state is the actual next state, and terminal invariance makes the appended step admissible. This gives a feasible candidate at the next sample. The terminal decrease condition also makes the candidate’s cost no larger than the previous optimal cost minus the stage cost just incurred. The new optimum can only improve on this candidate. With the usual positivity and regularity conditions, this decrease supports nominal asymptotic stability. These are sufficient conditions, rather than the only way to design a stabilizing MPC controller. Disturbances, estimation errors, changing constraints, and incomplete solves require additional analysis Mayne et al. [2000], Rawlings et al. [2017]. 

# 10.3 Practical MPC 

10.3.1 Linear and Nonlinear MPC 

With linear or affine prediction dynamics, a quadratic objective, and affine constraints, the finite-horizon problem is a quadratic program (QP). Positive-semidefinite state weights and positive-definite input weights give a convex objective. The QP can retain the predicted states as decision variables or eliminate them using the dynamics, as in Chapter 9. An ellipsoidal terminal set introduces a quadratic constraint, so it falls outside this affine-constraint QP form. 

Nonlinear MPC (NMPC) retains nonlinear prediction dynamics or constraints. Robot kinematics, configuration-dependent inertia, and collision geometry make this formulation natural, but the resulting problem is generally nonconvex. SQP solves a sequence of local QP approximations; iLQR and DDP exploit the trajectory structure through forward and backward passes. These solvers usually seek a local solution, whose quality depends on initialization and the available computation time. The receding-horizon architecture is the same in both cases. The choice is how much of the robot’s dynamics and geometry the online optimization can represent within its timing budget. 

10.3.2 Warm Starts and Solver Timing 

Successive MPC problems share most of their horizon. The previous solution is therefore a natural starting point for the next solve. A typical control warm start is 

U init  

> k+1

= (u⋆

> 1|k

, . . . , u ⋆N −1|k, u tail 

> )

, (10.18) Section 10.3 237 

where utail may be a terminal feedback command. Simply repeating the last input is convenient but need not provide a feasible tail. The terminal-controller extension used in the nominal feasibility argument is one principled choice. The predicted states should also be updated. In shooting, initialize x0|k+1 = ˆ xk+1 and roll out the shifted controls. In transcription, one may shift the old states and adjust the initial condition, accepting temporary defects that the solver must close. Multipliers and active-set information can be shifted as well. A warm start is a numerical aid, not a guarantee that the changed problem remains feasible. 

The computation deadline. Let Test , Tsolve , and Tcomm denote the estimation, optimization, and command-communication times. A simple synchronous implementation needs a timing budget such as 

Test + Tsolve + Tcomm < h − Tmargin . (10.19) Here Tmargin > 0 reserves time for variability and other tasks. Average solve time is not enough: occasional overruns can cause the input to be based on an outdated state. Timing should be evaluated under difficult initializations, active constraints, and changing references, not only near an easy equilibrium. The plant continues moving while the controller computes. Let tm be the time of the latest measurement and ta the expected actuation time. Propagate ˆ x(tm) to ta using the model and the input history, including commands scheduled to remain active during computation. Initialize the new prediction from this propagated estimate, and align references and obstacle forecasts with 

ta, t a + h, . . . . Significant actuator or communication delays should be included in the model. In an asynchronous implementation, each solution should carry timestamps and identify the state and reference data used to compute it. A solution that arrives too late may need to be updated or discarded in favor of a specified fallback. Even an accurately solved optimization can give poor commands if its prediction and actuation timelines disagree Rawlings et al. [2017], Diehl et al. [2005]. 

Limited-iteration optimization. For nonlinear MPC, it may be more useful to perform a small, predictable number of structured solver steps than to converge an entire new nonlinear program at every sample. Real-time iteration schemes coordinate successive Newton or SQP updates with the evolving plant. A preparation phase can form derivatives and local models before the newest state is available, followed by a shorter feedback update when the measurement arrives Diehl et al. [2005]. Their effectiveness depends on suitable initialization and regularity; a fixed iteration count does not by itself ensure an adequate solution. The distinction between the ideal and implemented controller is important. An exact optimizer defines the state feedback κN (x). A warm-started, limited-iteration implementation also depends on stored trajectories, multipliers, and solver state. Its closed-loop behavior is therefore a property of the plant–optimizer combination, not only of the mathematical finite-horizon problem. 238 Chapter 10. Model Predictive Control 

Failure handling. Before accepting a solution, check actuator limits, constraint residuals, dynamic consistency, and timely availability. A low objective value or a solver success flag alone does not establish that the command is usable. The implementation needs a specified response to infeasibility, numerical failure, and missed deadlines. Possible responses include a verified remaining plan, a local backup controller within its admissible region, or a controlled stop. None is automatically safe from every state. In particular, holding the last command or clipping a newly returned command is not a general feasibility-preserving fallback. 

10.3.3 State Estimation and Model Mismatch 

Robots typically measure joint positions, inertial signals, images, or other outputs rather than the complete state. A simple uncertainty model is 

xk+1 = f (xk, u k) + wk, yk = hy(xk) + vmeas  

> k

, (10.20) where wk represents process disturbances or model mismatch, and vmeas  

> k

is measurement noise. An observer or filter produces ˆ xk for the MPC initial condition. Chapter 7 introduced the basic observer and Kalman-filter ideas; their role here is to keep the prediction anchored to the estimated physical state. Replanning can correct deviations from an earlier prediction, but it does not make the model exact. A persistent load can repeatedly shift the actual motion away from the nominal one. Velocity-estimation error can make the controller underestimate stopping distance. Unmodeled actuator dynamics can make a command change take effect later than predicted. These errors matter most when the nominal plan runs close to a constraint boundary. Using ˆ xk in place of xk is a certainty-equivalent implementation. It ignores uncertainty when constructing the nominal prediction. The LQG separation principle does not automatically extend to constrained nonlinear MPC: estimation error can change which constraints are active or whether the planned motion is safe. The estimator and controller should therefore be evaluated together. 

What to monitor. Useful closed-loop diagnostics include prediction errors, constraint margins, slack values, solver residuals, and timing. For example, ˆ xk+1 − x1|k measures the disagreement between the next estimate and the previous one-step prediction when their timestamps are aligned. Large errors can indicate a poor model, an unreliable estimator, or incorrect timing. They should not be interpreted solely as a need for larger tracking weights. 

10.3.4 A Braking Example 

Consider a cart moving toward a target at position pg while keeping a safe distance dsafe from a wall. Its state is position and velocity, x = ( p, v ), and its input is acceleration a. At sample k, let Section 10.3 239

(10.21) Here eN |k = ( pN |k − pg, v N |k), Pf ⪰ 0, and qp, q v, r a > 0. The quadratic objective and affine dynamics and constraints make this a convex QP. No terminal safe set is imposed here, so feasibility over the prediction horizon does not establish that a safe continuation exists afterward. The wall forecast is updated from each new measurement. For example, a constant-velocity forecast is 

pwall ,i |k = ˆ pwall ,k + ih ˆvwall ,k . (10.22) If the wall moves closer, the predicted-clearance constraints tighten and the new solution may brake earlier or retreat. MPC applies only the first acceleration, then updates the cart state and wall forecast and solves again. This reactivity comes from using new measurements at each cycle; it does not guarantee that the cart can always avoid a collision. For a stationary wall and a cart moving toward it, the minimum stopping distance under maximum braking is 

dstop = v2

2amax 

, v > 0. (10.23) At v = 1 m /s and amax = 2 m /s2, the cart needs 0 .25 m to stop. If only 0 .15 m remains before the clearance boundary pwall − dsafe , no admissible input can preserve the required clearance. Whether the cart actually hits the wall also depends on dsafe . Replanning cannot create braking authority. For a moving wall, its predicted motion must also be included. In either case, clearance must be checked between sampling instants as well as at the predicted samples. 

10.3.5 Outlook: Robust and Stochastic MPC 

Nominal MPC predicts one trajectory from one state estimate. Robust MPC instead accounts explicitly for an assumed set of disturbances. In a tube-based construction, a nominal trajectory is accompanied by local feedback and a bounded error region; nominal constraints are tightened so that the physical state and input remain admissible for the allowed errors. The resulting protection is conditional on the disturbance bounds and the validity of the model and error analysis. Stochastic MPC models uncertainty probabilistically. It may replace a hard prediction constraint 240 Chapter 10. Model Predictive Control 

by a chance constraint such as 

P(g(xi|k, u i|k) ≤ 0) ≥ 1 − ε. (10.24) The probability model and risk level are part of the design. A marginal constraint at one stage is not automatically a joint guarantee over every stage or over indefinitely repeated operation. The specified risk level permits violations with nonzero probability. These extensions add uncertainty handling to the same receding-horizon architecture; they are distinct from merely replanning a nominal problem more frequently. A detailed treatment is beyond this chapter; see Rawlings et al. [2017]. Their guarantees depend on the stated uncertainty model, disturbance bounds, or probability assumptions. 

# 10.4 Representative MPC Applications in Robotics 

The following papers provide representative hardware demonstrations of MPC in robotics. They illustrate different choices of prediction model, optimization method, and low-level control archi-tecture rather than one universally best formulation. • Quadruped locomotion with convex MPC. Carlo et al. [2018], Dynamic Locomotion in the MIT Cheetah 3 Through Convex Model-Predictive Control . The authors demonstrate dynamic quadruped locomotion by optimizing ground-reaction forces with a simplified body model and a prescribed contact schedule. This is a useful example of making a demanding control problem tractable through model reduction and a convex QP. • Whole-body NMPC. Khazoom et al. [2024], Tailoring Solution Accuracy for Fast Whole-Body Model Predictive Control of Legged Robots , demonstrate coordinated humanoid motion using constrained NMPC with limited solver accuracy. Section 10.5 develops this example. • Whole-body contact-implicit MPC. Kim et al. [2025], Contact-Implicit Model Predictive Control: Controlling Diverse Quadruped Motions Without Pre-Planned Contact Modes or Trajectories ,use full-body dynamics and contact-implicit DDP to optimize motion without a fixed contact schedule. Hardware experiments on the KAIST HOUND quadruped include forward trotting and front-leg rearing. This contrasts with force planning under a prescribed gait: contact choices emerge during optimization, with cost terms guiding the resulting motion. • Torque-controlled legged locomotion with nonlinear MPC. Grandia et al. [2019], Feedback MPC for Torque-Controlled Legged Robots . The authors demonstrate motion tracking on the ANYmal quadruped using nonlinear MPC with a local feedback policy. The paper illustrates how the optimizer's local feedback policy bridges the gap between slower planning updates and faster torque-control updates. • Quadrotor flight with perception-aware MPC. Falanga et al. [2018], PAMPC: Perception-Aware Model Predictive Control for Quadrotors . The authors combine motion and visual-perception objectives and demonstrate the controller in real flight. This example shows that an MPC cost can account for sensing quality, not only position tracking and control effort. • Robotic manipulation with a learned prediction model. Lenz et al. [2015], DeepMPC: Learning Deep Latent Features for Model Predictive Control . The authors use a learned dynamics model within MPC for robotic food cutting. The paper is a useful example when the interaction Section 10.5 241 

dynamics are difficult to model analytically: learning supplies the predictor, while online optimization chooses the action. • Autonomous racing with contouring MPC. Liniger et al. [2015], Optimization-Based Autonomous Racing of 1:43 Scale RC Cars . The authors demonstrate real-time racing control on miniature cars. Their model predictive contouring approach balances progress along a track with path error and physical constraints, illustrating how MPC can optimize task progress rather than merely track a fixed time-indexed trajectory. When reading these papers, identify what the optimizer chooses, which dynamics and constraints it includes, and what remains the responsibility of estimation, planning, and low-level feedback. Hardware results establish performance under the tested conditions; they do not replace feasibility or stability analysis. 

# 10.5 Whole-Body NMPC for Humanoid Locomotion 

A walking humanoid must move in a commanded direction while keeping its body balanced, placing its feet, and respecting the limits of its joints and contacts. These requirements are coupled: moving a leg changes both the available support and the motion of the rest of the robot. This section explains how they become a finite-horizon optimal-control problem, then connects that formulation to the implementation of Khazoom et al. [2024]. 

10.5.1 The Control Task and the Meaning of Whole-Body NMPC 

Nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC) repeatedly solves an optimal-control problem whose prediction model or constraints are nonlinear. The letter “N” in NMPC stands for nonlinear ; the symbol N used below denotes the number of prediction intervals. At each update, the controller initializes the prediction from the estimated state and optimizes a future motion. It executes only the first control before replanning. For interval lengths ∆ ti, the prediction duration is 

Tp = ∑N −1 

> i=0

∆ti. Unlike the controller-update period, this horizon can span many controller updates. Here, whole-body describes the model and motion variables: the prediction includes the torso’s motion in space, the individual joint motions, and their mechanical coupling. Thus an arm motion, a leg motion, and a change in torso orientation are considered in the same optimization. This contrasts with a reduced-order prediction, such as a point-mass or single-rigid-body model, which can describe overall balance without predicting every joint trajectory. Whole-body prediction supports direct reasoning about reachability, joint limits, limb inertia, and self-collision, while still relying on modeling approximations. It does not imply that every flexible or electrical effect is modeled. For locomotion, the command may specify forward and lateral body velocity and a turning rate. The controller should follow that command while maintaining a suitable torso height and orientation and producing feasible steps. A nominal joint posture helps select among many possible motions. It is a preference, not a demand that every joint remain fixed while walking. The 242 Chapter 10. Model Predictive Control 

optimizer can trade posture error against body tracking and other costs, subject to the physical constraints. Recovering from a push is the same control task initialized from a newly disturbed state. 

10.5.2 Configuration, Velocity, and Actuation 

Unlike a manipulator bolted to a table, a humanoid has no permanently fixed base. Its torso can translate and rotate. We therefore use a floating-base configuration. For nj actuated joints, choose a local orientation chart with three coordinates and write the configuration and generalized velocity as 

q = ( pb, θ b, q j ), v = ( ˙ pb, ω b, ˙qj ), x = ( q, v ).

Here pb ∈ R3 is the base position, θb ∈ R3 is a local coordinate vector parameterizing its orientation (for example, Euler angles in a chosen chart), qj ∈ Rnj contains the joint angles, and ωb ∈ R3 is the base angular-velocity vector. Thus, q and v each have 6 + nj components in this representation. The six base degrees of freedom describe motion in space; the remaining coordinates describe the articulated body. The distinction between v and ˙ q matters: angular velocity is generally not the derivative of the orientation coordinates. For example, Euler-angle rates must be transformed to obtain ωb. The map E(q), which depends on the orientation chart and angular-velocity convention, converts generalized velocities to coordinate derivatives: ˙q = E(q)v. With a unit quaternion, replace θb by 

qb ∈ R4 satisfying ∥qb∥2 = 1. Then q has 7 + nj entries (but still only 6 + nj configuration degrees of freedom), while v has 6 + nj components; accordingly, E(q) is rectangular. The equations below allow either representation, provided orientation is integrated consistently. The motors supply joint torques τ ∈ Rnj . They do not directly command the six base coordinates. Ground contacts provide additional external forces Fc ∈ R3, indexed by candidate contact points 

c ∈ C . Multiple points on one foot can represent both a net support force and a moment. The controller must find torques and contact forces that can jointly produce the desired motion. 

10.5.3 Why the Equations of Motion Have This Form 

For a rigid articulated robot, the equations are 

M (q) ˙ v + b(q, v ) = STτ + ∑

> c∈C

Jc(q)TFc, ˙q = E(q)v. (10.25) This is Newton’s law expressed in generalized coordinates. In one vertical translation it reduces to 

m¨z + mg = Fz . For an articulated robot, a matrix replaces the scalar mass, and generalized forces include both translational forces and rotational torques. The terms have the following roles. 

Inertia and the forces on the left-hand side. The mass matrix M (q) ∈ R(6+ nj )×(6+ nj )

describes the inertia of all modeled links and their coupling. The kinetic energy is 12 vTM (q)v. Its dependence on configuration explains why extending a leg or moving an arm changes the robot’s Section 10.5 243 

response to a torque. The vector b(q, v ) collects gravity and velocity-dependent terms, including Coriolis and centrifugal effects. In a common notation, b(q, v ) = C(q, v )v + g(q). These terms arise when the link equations of motion are assembled, or equivalently from the kinetic and potential energies. No new optimization principle is involved: this equation is the mechanical constraint that admissible trajectories must satisfy. 

Why the actuator term contains a selection matrix. With the base coordinates first, the matrix

The zeros express the absence of a motor directly translating or rotating the floating base. Joint torques can nevertheless affect base motion through the coupled dynamics. The system is underactuated : it has more generalized velocity components than independent motor torques. Internal limb motion can redistribute angular momentum between links, but it cannot provide the external force needed to change the center-of-mass acceleration beyond that due to gravity and contact. 

Why a contact force appears as JT

Fc is the generalized force associated with the contact. The sum accounts for all active points; inactive points have zero force. This also explains why foot placement matters: changing the contact location changes how a ground force acts on the robot. The nonlinear dependence of M , b, and Jc on the state is one reason the resulting controller is NMPC. Even a quadratic tracking cost does not make an optimal-control problem with these dynamics a linear–quadratic problem. 

10.5.4 Contacts, Gait Information, and Motion Constraints 

A contact force is an optimization variable, but it is not an independently commanded actuator. The predicted force must be consistent with the torques, motion, and contact conditions. For a nonadhesive contact with a stationary support surface, a common model requires 

Fc,n ≥ 0, ∥Fc,t ∥2 ≤ μF c,n , (10.26) where Fc,n is the force along the outward surface normal, Fc,t ∈ R2 contains the tangential components, and μ is the friction coefficient. The first inequality says that the ground can push but cannot pull the foot. The second limits tangential force relative to normal load. A proposed horizontal acceleration that needs too much friction must be changed by the optimizer. 244 Chapter 10. Model Predictive Control 

The controller also needs to know which points are in stance (supporting the robot) and which are in swing (moving to the next support location). A prescribed schedule gives a binary parameter 

γc,i for each point and prediction stage: one for stance and zero for swing. During stance, the contact point stays on the support surface with zero tangential velocity. During swing, its contact force is zero, and a height reference can guide it above the ground, while a clearance constraint can enforce a minimum height. A prescribed contact schedule specifies when support changes; it need not prescribe the horizontal location of every future footstep. Optimizing contact timing itself would introduce an additional decision that this scheduled-contact formulation does not make. Further constraints bound joint angles, joint velocities, and torques. Self-collision constraints can require dab (q) ≥ dmin for selected pairs of links, where dab is their separation and dmin is the required clearance. These geometric constraints depend on the joint configuration, which is available in a whole-body prediction. They are particularly relevant when a recovery step passes one leg close to the other. The contact model describes the planned stance and swing phases; impact effects at touchdown require an appropriate transition model or approximation as well. 

10.5.5 The Finite-Horizon Optimization 

At one controller update, suppress the update index and let i index the future stages. Define 

xi = ( qi, v i) and stack the contact forces as Fi. The decision variables are 

Z = ({xi}Ni=0 , {τi, F i}N −1

The estimated initial state, command, model parameters, and contact schedule are data. Both the motion and the forces required to produce it are unknowns. A representative stage cost is 

ℓi = ∥eq,i ∥2

The reference configuration can combine a desired body height and orientation with a nominal joint posture, while the velocity reference encodes the commanded translation and turning. A force reference can favor a reasonable load distribution among stance contacts. The positive-semidefinite weights express the relative importance of these preferences. A quadratic cost does not prescribe the motion in advance: the dynamics and constraints determine how closely its competing references can be followed. Let Di(xi, x i+1 , τ i, F i) = 0 denote a discretization of equation (10.25). In a first-order discretization, the acceleration is approximated by ˙vi ≈ vi+1 − vi

A compatible configuration update supplies the remaining kinematic relations. Here xi, xi+1 , τi,and Fi are optimized jointly, and this equality requires their combination to satisfy the equations of motion. This is called an inverse-dynamics transcription: for each candidate motion, the equations constrain the joint torques and contact forces needed for dynamic consistency, subject to actuator and contact limits. A forward-dynamics transcription would instead compute acceleration from the current state and forces by applying M (qi)−1 to the net generalized force, then integrate the state. Keeping force balance as a constraint avoids explicitly forming M (qi)−1 at every stage. The constraints remain nonlinear because M , b, and Jc depend on the state. The optimization then has the form minimize  

> Z

subject to x0 = ˆ x, Di(xi, x i+1 , τ i, F i) = 0 , i = 0 , . . . , N − 1,

(xi, τ i, F i) ∈ A i(γi), i = 0 , . . . , N − 1,xN ∈ A N .

(10.28) Here γi = ( γc,i )c∈C is the vector of prescribed contact indicators, which are data rather than optimization variables: γc,i = 1 selects stance conditions for contact c, and γc,i = 0 selects swing conditions. The stage set Ai(γi) contains the tuples ( xi, τ i, F i) satisfying the constraints active at stage i. For stance contacts, these include staying on the support surface, zero tangential velocity, nonnegative normal force, and the friction limit; for swing contacts, they set Fc,i = 0 and include any modeled hard clearance constraint. The set also includes the applicable joint-position and velocity bounds, actuator-torque bounds, and selected self-collision constraints. Thus the contact indicators choose which contact conditions apply, while contact timing remains fixed by the prescribed schedule. The terminal set AN contains only the applicable constraints on xN , such as specified terminal state bounds or geometric clearance requirements; it has no stage torque or force variables. The terminal cost Φ represents consequences beyond the prediction horizon. The last cost term discourages a sudden change from the previously applied torque τprev , with R∆ ⪰ 0. It is a smoothness preference, separate from torque bounds. This schematic problem does not assume that AN is a terminal invariant set or that Φ is an exact value function. 

10.5.6 Real-Time Solution and the Humanoid Implementation 

The optimization must return a useful command before the state changes too far from its initial estimate. Sequential quadratic programming (SQP) linearizes the constraints and uses a quadratic local objective, then updates the trajectory by solving the resulting quadratic program (QP). Reusing a previous trajectory as a warm start reduces the work needed at the next update. Solving each nonlinear problem to high precision can be less useful than obtaining an adequate update quickly, especially when the prediction already contains model error. Khazoom et al. [2024] implement one SQP iteration per update, with a fixed number of iterations of the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) in the operator-splitting QP solver 246 Chapter 10. Model Predictive Control 

OSQP. Their contact schedule and swing-height references are supplied to the optimizer; foot placements and coordinated body motion are optimized within these restrictions. Their walking implementation also reduces the general formulation above: joint-torque bounds and the actuated inverse-dynamics equations are enforced only at the first two stages. This retains torque consistency near the action being executed while reducing computation farther into the prediction. The authors add control barrier function (CBF) constraints for self-collision at the first stage. A CBF constrains the evolution of the separation, accounting for motion toward a collision as well as the current distance. At the measured state, the relevant acceleration constraint is affine in the acceleration variables. Later stages retain geometric distance constraints. This construction improves collision avoidance under incomplete solves; it does not make an approximate solution an exact feasibility guarantee. The reported MIT Humanoid controller runs at approximately 90 Hz with 32 prediction stages and demonstrates walking and disturbance recovery with coordinated arm and crossed-leg motions. The 32 stages describe the plan being optimized; the update rate describes how often that plan is revised. Optimized position, velocity, and torque trajectories are interpolated and supplied to faster motor-level feedback. To see the role of this last layer, a typical joint-level command has the form 

τcmd = τff + Kp(qdes  

> j

− qj ) + Kd( ˙ qdes  

> j

− ˙qj ), (10.29) where the feedforward torque and desired joint motion come from the optimized trajectory, and 

Kp and Kd are the proportional and derivative gain matrices. These feedback terms correct local tracking errors between optimization updates. The predicted contact forces are realized through interaction with the ground, rather than sent as independent motor commands. New measurements update the state estimate and start the next optimization. For example, after a lateral push, the estimated sideways velocity changes. Replanning can adjust the next foot placement, torso motion, arm motion, and supporting forces together while preserving clearance between the legs. The optimizer evaluates their consequences over the horizon, and local feedback tracks the beginning of the resulting motion. The achievable recovery still depends on the available contacts, actuator authority, estimation accuracy, and computation delay; the hardware results do not establish stability or feasibility for every disturbance. 

Exercises. Exercise 10.1. Explain why MPC is feedback even when the finite-horizon optimizer returns an open-loop sequence. What part of that sequence is normally executed? 

Short solution. Only the first input is executed. At the next sample, a new state estimate changes the initial condition of the optimization and therefore the next executed input. The repeated optimization defines an implicit feedback law. 

Exercise 10.2. Distinguish the prediction horizon, the control horizon, and the number of controls applied before replanning. Section 10.5 247 

Short solution. The prediction horizon specifies how far the model is propagated. The control horizon specifies how many future moves are independently parameterized. Standard MPC applies one control before replanning, regardless of either horizon length. 

Exercise 10.3. For the scalar system x+ = ax + u, derive equation (10.17) from the horizon-one objective with terminal weight s. Why does s = 0 fail for a > 1? 

Short solution. Differentiating the control-dependent cost gives ru + s(ax + u) = 0, hence u =

−asx/ (r + s). Substitution gives x+ = arx/ (r + s). When s = 0, the minimizing input is zero and the unstable open-loop coefficient a is unchanged. 

Exercise 10.4. Why does unconstrained linear MPC with terminal matrix P∞ reproduce infinite-horizon LQR for any positive horizon length? 

Short solution. The stabilizing DARE solution is a fixed point of the backward Riccati recursion. Initializing its terminal matrix at P∞ therefore gives the same matrix and gain at every stage. The first MPC control equals the infinite-horizon LQR control. 

Exercise 10.5. Explain how a terminal invariant set supports recursive feasibility in nominal MPC. What can break this guarantee? 

Short solution. Shifting the previous feasible sequence and appending an admissible terminal control gives a candidate next plan; invariance keeps its endpoint in the terminal set. A disturbance or estimation error can make the actual next state differ from its prediction, invalidating that candidate. 

Exercise 10.6. Why does adding slack to a tracking constraint not guarantee feasibility or safety of the entire MPC problem? 

Short solution. Slack relaxes only the constraint to which it is attached. The dynamics, actuator bounds, and other hard constraints can still be infeasible. Moreover, a positive slack explicitly permits violation of the original tracking requirement and says nothing about physical safety. 

Exercise 10.7. For the cart with v = 1 m /s and amax = 2 m /s2, compute the stopping time and distance. Can it maintain wall clearance if only 0 .15 m is available? 

Short solution. The stopping time is v/a max = 0 .5 s and the stopping distance is v2/(2 amax ) = 0.25 m. The available distance is smaller, so the stated continuous-time clearance is impossible under this model. 

Exercise 10.8. When is the linear–quadratic MPC problem a QP, and why can an ellipsoidal terminal set change that classification? 

Short solution. It is a QP when the objective is quadratic and all constraints are affine. An ellipsoidal terminal set imposes a quadratic inequality rather than an affine one. The problem can remain convex, but is no longer a pure QP with only linear constraints. 248 Chapter 10. Model Predictive Control 

Exercise 10.9. Why should a limited-iteration MPC implementation be analyzed together with its warm start and failure-handling rules? 

Short solution. Its returned control depends on stored solver state as well as the current estimate. An incomplete solve may fail feasibility or cost-decrease tests, and a fallback may apply a different control entirely. These choices are part of the implemented closed-loop system. 

Chapter summary. Model predictive control repeatedly solves a finite-horizon trajectory problem, executes its first control, and replans from new measurements. The prediction is finite, but the repeated execution is feedback. MPC can anticipate state and input constraints before they become unavoidable, using the structured solvers developed in Chapter 9. Linear dynamics, quadratic costs, and affine constraints give a QP. Without constraints and with an infinite-horizon LQR terminal cost, the controller reproduces LQR. Nonlinear robot dynamics and nonconvex geometric or contact constraints require more general optimization. Prediction horizon, control parameterization, and terminal ingredients determine what future consequences the controller can represent. Recursive feasibility and stability are not automatic consequences of replanning. In the nominal setting, a terminal invariant set and a terminal cost-decrease condition allow the old plan to be shifted and extended. This gives a feasible next candidate and a decrease of the finite-horizon value. Disturbances, state-estimation errors, changing constraints, and incomplete solves require additional reasoning. A practical controller must combine warm starts, reliable derivatives, consistent timestamps, state estimation, constraint checks, and explicit responses to missed deadlines or failed solves. The braking example shows why position alone is insufficient: velocity and braking authority determine whether the required clearance can be maintained. Robust and stochastic MPC extend the framework by explicitly modeling uncertainty rather than assuming that frequent nominal replanning provides every needed guarantee. Whole-body NMPC applies these ideas to coordinated robot motion by optimizing joint motion and contact forces together. The humanoid example also illustrates why solver accuracy must be balanced against the delay before a command can act. The next chapter keeps this receding-horizon architecture but changes the computational en-gine. Sampling-based MPC and model predictive path integral control search over simulated control sequences, providing alternatives when useful derivatives are unavailable or parallel rollout computation is attractive. 11 

# Sampling-Based MPC and MPPI 

Chapter map. Derivative-free trajectory search, GPU and NPU computation, probability densities and importance sampling, information-theoretic control, the Gibbs variational principle, the MPPI update, practical constraints, and robotics examples. 

Chapter 10 established the model predictive control architecture: predict the consequences of candidate actions, optimize a finite-horizon plan, execute its first action, and replan from new measurements. It did not require any particular optimizer. This chapter keeps that architecture but changes the computational engine. Instead of differentiating a rollout and solving a local quadratic problem, a sampling-based optimizer evaluates many candidate control sequences and uses their costs to construct a new plan. This choice is useful when derivatives are unavailable, unreliable, or expensive. A simulator may contain contact switches, a cost may include an obstacle indicator or a lookup table, and a learned dynamics model may be available only through an inference interface. Sampling asks each model the simpler question: what happens if this sequence is applied? It does not make the model more accurate or remove nonconvexity, but it allows optimization to use the original rollout without constructing its local derivatives. The apparent disadvantage is the number of sample trajectories. Modern parallel hardware changes this tradeoff: independent candidate trajectories can be evaluated concurrently, and neural predictors can process batches of candidate states and actions. A graphics processing unit (GPU) can accelerate suitable simulation and cost computations; a neural processing unit (NPU) can accelerate supported neural-model inference. These are opportunities, not automatic speedups. The entire estimation–planning–execution loop must still meet its deadline. The main algorithm is model predictive path integral control (MPPI). We derive its cost-weighted update through a finite-dimensional information-theoretic formulation, introducing the probability tools before using them. This route explains both the exponential weights and the likelihood-ratio term that is easily omitted in informal implementations. The principal references are Williams et al. [2017a,b, 2018]. We also connect the derivation to classical path-integral stochastic control, 249 250 Chapter 11. Sampling-Based MPC and MPPI 

while distinguishing its special assumptions from the more general sampling-based optimization procedure. 

# 11.1 Why Sampling, and the Probability Tools It Needs 

11.1.1 A Rollout Is the Basic Computational Primitive 

At one MPC update, let x0 = ˆ xk be the current state estimate and use the prediction model 

xi+1 = fi(xi, v i), i = 0 , . . . , N − 1. (11.1) We suppress the conditioning index |k within this solve. A candidate input sequence is V =(v0, . . . , v N −1) ∈ RN m . Starting from x0, forward simulation determines its state trajectory and cost 

J(V ) = Φ( xN ) +  

> N−1∑
> i=0

ℓi(xi, v i). (11.2) The inputs can be torques, accelerations, or reference commands to a low-level controller, provided the prediction model represents the consequences of those commands. For deterministic dynamics, one rollout needs N model transitions. Evaluating K candidates needs 

KN transitions, but candidates do not depend on one another. Time steps within a trajectory are normally sequential because xi+1 depends on xi; the sample index is the main source of parallelism. This distinction matters when estimating the benefit of a larger accelerator. Derivative-free does not mean model-free. The optimizer still needs a prediction of future motion, and it can confidently choose a poor action if that prediction is wrong. Nor does parallel hardware eliminate the dimensionality problem. If a useful set occupies fraction r of the proposal probability, 

K independent draws miss it with probability (1 −r)K . When r is tiny, simply increasing the sample count is an inefficient substitute for a good proposal, warm start, or control parameterization.

At each MPC update, we start from a nominal control sequence U = ( u0, . . . , u N −1) and draw candidate sequences around it. The distribution used to generate these candidates is called the proposal. Their predicted costs guide the update of U . A convenient proposal adds Gaussian noise:

Yet, under the support and integrability assumptions, the law of large numbers makes the numerator and denominator, after division by K, approach their respective expectations; their ratio then approaches I. Thus finite-sample bias and consistency as K grows can both hold. Highly concentrated weights can nevertheless make the estimate inaccurate at practical sample counts.

11.1.5 Random Shooting and the Cross-Entropy Method 

Random shooting is the most direct sampling-based optimizer. It draws K candidate control sequences from a proposal distribution, simulates each to evaluate equation (11.2), and selects the lowest-cost admissible candidate. Under receding-horizon execution, only its first input is applied before replanning from the next measurement. The method requires no derivatives and is straightforward to implement, but a poorly centered or overly broad proposal can waste most of the rollout budget on unpromising candidates. The cross-entropy method (CEM) adapts the proposal. For a Gaussian sequence distribution with mean μ and covariance C, sample a batch and retain an elite index set I containing the best Ke

candidates. A maximum-likelihood fit to these elite sequences gives 

μnew = 1

Ke

> ∑
> j∈I

V (j), (11.31) 

Cnew = 1

Ke

> ∑
> j∈I

(V (j) − μnew )( V (j) − μnew )T. (11.32) Repeat the selection-and-refitting cycle as needed within each MPC update. In practice, imple-mentations often smooth parameter updates, constrain the covariance to a structured form, and maintain a covariance floor. The covariance sets the spread of candidate sequences around the current mean. If the selected elites happen to cluster tightly, repeated refitting can shrink this spread until later candidates are nearly identical, reducing exploration too early. A covariance floor enforces a minimum spread—for example, a lower bound on each variance for a diagonal covariance, or on the smallest eigenvalue for a full covariance. The floor should reflect the scale and units of the controls: too small may not prevent collapse, while too large can keep samples needlessly broad. CEM gives zero weight to non-elite samples after a hard cutoff, whereas MPPI uses continuous exponential weights with a correction for the proposal density. 

11.1.6 Why GPUs and NPUs Change the Tradeoff 

Sampling replaces one tightly coupled local solve by many forward evaluations. This is attractive when the available hardware can perform those evaluations concurrently. GPU-based MPPI implementations exploit this structure directly Williams et al. [2017a]. GPU physics systems such as Isaac Gym also demonstrate the benefit of keeping simulation data and tensor computation on the same device Makoviychuk et al. [2021]; that simulator work is evidence about the computational primitive, not a claim that its learning experiments are themselves MPC. A typical batched implementation stores candidate inputs in a tensor of shape K × N × m and current predicted states in a tensor of shape K × n. At stage i, it evaluates the model for all 

K states and inputs, accumulates their costs, and proceeds to stage i + 1. After the horizon, reductions compute the best candidate or the weighted update. A learned predictor fθ(x, u ) can similarly receive a batch of concatenated state–input vectors. An NPU is particularly relevant to the latter case: supported neural-network operations may be executed by a dedicated inference engine. It is not interchangeable with a general-purpose GPU 256 Chapter 11. Sampling-Based MPC and MPPI 

Table 11.1: Typical opportunities for parallel sampling-based MPC. The appropriate device depends on the workload, not only its peak throughput. Device Useful work Main qualification CPU small models, branching logic, parallel simulator instances a small batch may not justify accelerator overhead GPU batched rollouts, compatible physics, neural inference, reductions memory traffic and sequential horizon steps still matter NPU supported neural dynamics or perception inference neural inference support does not imply support for the entire planner physics implementation. A rigid-body contact solve, random-number generation, obstacle queries, and the weight reductions may still run elsewhere. Supported operations, shapes, precision, and transfer overhead must be checked for the actual deployment; inference runtimes explicitly impose device-specific restrictions OpenVINO contributors [2025]. Increasing K usually improves coverage but also increases memory and computation. If every model step copies data between CPU and accelerator, communication can erase the benefit of parallel arithmetic. Useful measurements are end-to-end latency, missed deadlines, model accuracy, and closed-loop performance, not just the number of simulated trajectories per second. Gradient-based MPC can also exploit accelerators; sampling is a good match to parallel hardware, not its exclusive beneficiary.

The scaled negative log of the partition function is called the free energy in this formulation: 

F := −λ log Z. (11.37) Therefore, 

J (Q) = F + λD KL (Q∥Q⋆). (11.38) Since KL divergence is nonnegative, the minimum objective value is F, attained when Q = Q⋆.This equality is the Gibbs variational identity . Sign and scaling conventions for the name free energy vary across the literature. Low-cost sequences receive more probability than high-cost sequences, relative to the same reference measure. This is exponential tilting . The target is not a uniform distribution over the best sequences: the prior probability p(V ) also matters. For two sequences with equal prior density, a cost difference ∆S gives a target-density ratio exp( −∆S/λ ). Thus λ determines which differences are significant.

Shifting the mean along a direction with large sampling variance incurs a smaller KL penalty; shifting it along a tightly sampled direction incurs a larger one. Thus Σ sets both the exploration scale and the implicit effort metric. The temperature λ scales the strength of this penalty, giving the quadratic weight 

R = λΣ−1.

The distributional objective, restricted to this Gaussian family, is 

J (QU ) = EQU [S(V )] + 12 

> N−1∑
> i=0

uT 

> i

Ru i. (11.44) Here the first term averages rollout cost over the sampled sequences, while the second penalizes moving the sampling mean away from the zero-mean reference. This gives a stochastic expected-cost interpretation when input deviations really follow the assumed Gaussian distribution. When they are artificial search noise, it instead defines a smoothed, regularized optimization objective. In either case, the sampling covariance need not be a measured physical disturbance covariance. 

11.2.3 Approximating the Ideal Measure by a Gaussian Proposal 

We generally cannot draw samples directly from the ideal distribution Q⋆. Its density is 

q⋆(V ) = Z−1 exp( −S(V )/λ ) p(V ), but computing its normalizing constant Z is generally in-tractable. Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods, including Langevin-based methods, can target an unnormalized density, but may require many costly evaluations of the rollout cost S(V ). Gradient-based Langevin methods also require derivatives that may not be available. Instead of sampling Q⋆ directly, information-theoretic MPPI approximates it with an easy-to-sample Gaussian measure QU Williams et al. [2018]. The proposal uses the previously chosen covariance Σ, which is held fixed in this projection; only the mean sequence U is optimized: 

U new = arg min 

> U

DKL (Q⋆∥QU ). (11.45) Notice the direction of KL. The distributional objective used DKL (Q∥P ); this projection uses 

DKL (Q⋆∥QU ). It is not the same as minimizing J (QU ) over U .260 Chapter 11. Sampling-Based MPC and MPPI 

By definition, 

DKL (Q⋆∥QU ) = EQ⋆ [log q⋆(V ) − log qU (V )] .

The term involving q⋆ is independent of U . In qU (V ), the normalizing constant is also independent of U because ¯Σ is fixed. Thus only the quadratic term affects the minimizer, and the projection reduces to minimize

The general nonlinear trajectory-optimization problem is much harder and may be intractable. Also, if the ideal distribution has several modes, their mean can produce a poor control sequence.
