For this quadratic example, the mean also minimizes 12 a(u − b)2 + 12 λu 2/σ 2; this convenient coincidence should not be generalized to nonlinear, multimodal tasks. Section 11.3 263 

11.2.5 Why the Name Path Integral? 

The earlier derivation sums over candidate input sequences and the trajectories they produce. Classical path-integral control comes from continuous-time stochastic control, where a system evolves under both control and random disturbances. Its dynamic-programming equation tracks the best expected future cost. In some systems, control and disturbance enter in a compatible way. Under this special condition, an exponential transformation simplifies the dynamic-programming equation into a linear one. The transformed value can then be represented as an average over uncontrolled trajectories, with lower-cost paths contributing more. Averaging over complete paths—not integrating over a spatial grid—is the classical path-integral idea Kappen [2005], Theodorou et al. [2010]. This connection has specific assumptions. Classical path-integral control requires a particular relationship between how control affects the system and how noise enters it. The finite-sequence Gibbs derivation and Gaussian mean update earlier in this chapter do not require that relationship, so they can be used with general simulators and learned models. This broader use of sampling does not, by itself, guarantee the classical continuous-time optimal-control result. Williams and colleagues discuss the connection and its practical MPPI formulation Williams et al. [2017a, 2018]. Finally, discrete input perturbations and continuous-time process noise are not interchangeable without accounting for the time step. The two approaches share the idea of favoring lower-cost paths, but their assumptions and guarantees differ. 

# 11.3 Implementing MPPI in a Receding-Horizon Loop 

11.3.1 Stable Weights and an Explicit Algorithm 

Directly exponentiating large negative scores can underflow. For the finite valid scores, let 

ρ = min j ˜Sj and compute 

bj = exp 

> (

− ˜Sj − ρλ

> )

, ωj = bj 

> ∑
> ℓ

bℓ

. (11.61) The common shift cancels exactly, so it changes numerical conditioning, not the target distribution. Invalid rollouts can be assigned zero weight. If every rollout is invalid, there is no finite baseline or meaningful weighted update; the controller must take its specified fallback branch. The following pseudocode states the mathematical core and the execution checks separately. The core assumes unconstrained Gaussian input samples with equal reference and proposal covariance, as derived above. 

MPPI at one MPC update 

1. Read the timestamped estimate ˆ xk, current reference, and environment prediction. Initialize the mean sequence U from a shifted previous plan or another available 264 Chapter 11. Sampling-Based MPC and MPPI 

warm start. 2. For each allowed optimization batch: (a) Freeze the current U . For j = 1 , . . . , K , independently draw ϵ(j) 

> i

∼ N (0 , Σ) and set v(j) 

> i

= ui + ϵ(j) 

> i

.(b) In parallel across samples, initialize x(j)0 = ˆ xk, roll out the N transitions, and accumulate S(V (j)). Also record constraint violations and invalid model evaluations. (c) Compute the corrected scores in equation (11.54). If no sample is usable, return to the failure-handling procedure. (d) Compute equation (11.61) and update every stage with equation (11.59). Use the same frozen U in the proposal, likelihood ratio, and update. 3. Roll out and validate the updated mean plan. Accept it only if it meets the deployment's admissibility and timing tests; otherwise use an appropriate checked incumbent or backup policy. 4. Apply the first accepted input. Store the remaining plan for the next update, append a suitable tail, and repeat from new measurements. Step 3 is necessary because averaging candidates is not itself a feasibility test. A deployment with bounded inputs or transformed control variables must use the corresponding sampling distribution, rather than assuming the unconstrained core automatically handles those changes. 

11.3.2 Warm Starts, Horizon Length, and the Sampling Budget 

At each MPC update, shift the accepted plan forward and append a suitable tail: 

U init  

> k+1

= ( u1|k, . . . , u N −1|k, u tail ). (11.62) The tail can come from a local controller or reference. New measurements, obstacle motion, or a task change can invalidate the shifted plan. The sample count, horizon, and number of batches share a fixed compute budget: more samples improve coverage, a longer horizon predicts farther ahead, and more batches recenter the proposal. Keep a checked incumbent as a separate acceptance or fallback candidate; the MPPI mean update guarantees neither monotonic improvement nor recursive feasibility. 

11.3.3 Temperature, Covariance, and Weight Concentration 

For fixed corrected scores, lower λ concentrates weight on the best samples. But changing λ also changes the target distribution; as it grows, the target approaches the reference P , not necessarily the current proposal QU . The effective sample size summarizes weight concentration: 

Keff = 1 

> ∑Kj=1

ω2

> j

, 1 ≤ Keff ≤ K. (11.63) Section 11.3 265 

It is a diagnostic, not a measure of feasible paths or a guarantee of accuracy. Small covariance limits search to a local region; large covariance explores more broadly but may generate mostly invalid samples. Scale perturbations by input, monitor both weights and task progress, and recompute the likelihood ratio whenever the proposal covariance changes. Changing the reference itself changes the regularized problem. 

11.3.4 Smooth Sequences and Lower-Dimensional Search 

Independent stagewise noise can produce jagged controls. Instead, sample a small vector of spline or knot coefficients and interpolate to a full sequence, for example 

V = Lθ + Vbase , (11.64) where the coefficient dimension is smaller than the full sequence dimension. Define the reference and proposal in this coefficient space. Temporal correlations are another option, using a positive-definite sequence covariance. Do not smooth an update after scoring without rerolling and validating the modified plan; a lower-dimensional search can also miss useful fast corrections. 

11.3.5 Constraints, Clipping, and Multimodal Plans 

Constraints can be represented by finite penalties or by assigning infeasible candidates infinite cost. For a hard feasible set A, the target is proportional to 

1A(V )e−S(V )/λ p(V ). (11.65) The set must have positive probability under the reference; exact equalities are generally missed by unconstrained continuous sampling. Penalties allow tradeoffs and are not hard guarantees. Clipping changes the sampling distribution. Either define the proposal and weights on latent variables and apply clipping inside the rollout, or use the correct density for a truncated proposal. In every case, validate the plan that will actually be executed. Even feasible sampled paths can have an infeasible average—for example, paths that pass on opposite sides of an obstacle may average into a collision. A checked sample or safety supervisor may be preferable to the mean in a multimodal problem. 

11.3.6 Model Uncertainty and Failure Handling 

More samples do not correct model bias. Monitor prediction residuals, constraint margins, and whether commands are realizable. If uncertainty matters, specify how it enters the objective; for example, worst-case and mean-cost criteria are different: 

Srob (V ) = max  

> ξ∈Ξ

J(V ; ξ), Smean (V ) = Eξ[J(V ; ξ)] . (11.66) 266 Chapter 11. Sampling-Based MPC and MPPI 

One favorable disturbance sample is not a robust evaluation, and averaging cost before exponential weighting differs from averaging the weights. Define fallback behavior for invalid samples, missed deadlines, and rejected mean plans. A stored plan or backup controller is useful only within its verified operating conditions; safety remains a system-level requirement. 

# 11.4 Robotics Examples 

These examples show how the predictor and control representation shape the sampling problem; their details are illustrative, not exact reproductions of the cited implementations. 

11.4.1 Ground-Robot Navigation 

A car-like robot can be planned with a low-speed bicycle model or a learned predictor, scoring reference tracking, speed, and obstacle clearance. Higher-speed driving needs a model that captures tire dynamics. Update moving-obstacle forecasts and validate the robot footprint between sampled time steps; no sample count can create a safe path when actuation or clearance makes one impossible. Williams and colleagues demonstrate information-theoretic MPPI on an autonomous ground vehicle using learned dynamics Williams et al. [2018]. 

11.4.2 Object Pushing with a Learned Contact Model 

A learned predictor can forecast object motion and uncertainty through sliding, sticking, and loss of contact. MPPI can score goal progress, collisions, and predictive uncertainty, then replan after each short push. An uncertainty penalty is a planning preference, not a calibrated success probability; a useful forward model is still essential. Arruda and colleagues demonstrate uncertainty-aware MPPI pushing on a real robot Arruda et al. [2017]. 

11.4.3 Whole-Body Locomotion and Manipulation 

For legged robots, sample a small number of joint-target knots, track them with a low-level controller, and simulate the resulting body motion, actuator limits, and contacts. Scores can include body tracking, gait regularity, joint limits, and manipulation goals. On a Unitree Go1, Alvarez and colleagues use joint-target splines, PD tracking, gait references, and parallel MuJoCo rollouts on a multicore CPU; their results illustrate why reduced search dimension and low rollout latency can matter more than maximizing sample count Alvarez-Padilla et al. [2025]. Across these applications, MPPI is MPC because it predicts future motion, executes the first accepted action, and replans from new measurements—not simply because it repeatedly solves an optimization problem.

Exercise 11.2. Derive equation (11.17). Why is support coverage required? 

Short solution. Write ∫ hp = ∫ h(p/q )q. If q vanishes on a set with positive target probability, samples cannot visit that set and its contribution cannot be estimated through the ratio. 

Exercise 11.3. Prove the Gibbs variational identity and identify the optimal distribution. Does it imply that its mean is a globally optimal deterministic control? 

Short solution. Substitute q⋆ = e−S/λ p/Z into DKL (Q∥Q⋆) and rearrange to obtain J (Q) = 

F + λD KL (Q∥Q⋆). The measure Q⋆ is optimal for the distributional objective. A nonlinear rollout of its mean can differ greatly from its low-cost sampled rollouts, so deterministic optimality does not follow.

Exercise 11.6. Why does subtracting the minimum corrected score leave MPPI weights un-changed? What if every score is infinite? 

Short solution. The numerator and denominator acquire the same factor eρ/λ , which cancels. If all scores are infinite, there is no valid finite baseline or normalized distribution over usable candidates; invoke failure handling.

Exercise 11.8. Explain why two feasible sampled paths around an obstacle can have an infeasible average control sequence. What should be checked before execution? 

Short solution. Nonlinear trajectory feasibility is not generally convex in the inputs. Opposite avoidance motions can average into a collision course. Roll out and validate the sequence actually intended for execution, including any filtering, clipping, or safety modification. 268 Chapter 11. Sampling-Based MPC and MPPI 

Exercise 11.9. Which parts of sampling-based MPC can benefit from a GPU or NPU, and why does high throughput not imply a short control-loop latency? 

Short solution. A GPU can parallelize compatible simulations and reductions. An NPU can accelerate supported neural inference. The horizon still has sequential state dependencies, and estimation, transfers, obstacle queries, and communication also consume time. Measure the complete update deadline. 

Chapter summary. Sampling-based MPC uses many forward predictions to search for a control sequence without differentiating its rollout. Random shooting selects a tested candidate; CEM refits a proposal to elites; MPPI uses weighted moments. GPU-parallel rollouts and accelerated neural inference can make these methods practical, but cannot remove poor model accuracy, weak sampling coverage, or execution delays. The information-theoretic formulation minimizes expected trajectory cost plus KL divergence from a reference measure. Its exact distributional solution is a Gibbs density. Fitting an equal-covariance Gaussian to that target gives a mean-matching update, and importance sampling turns the unknown expectation into exponentially weighted perturbations. The Gaussian likelihood ratio supplies the control–noise cross term. Keeping the reference, target, and proposal distinct prevents misleading cost formulas and optimality claims. MPPI remains a finite-horizon, locally sampled controller. Constraint penalties, input clipping, smooth parameterizations, and uncertain models must be handled consistently; an averaged sequence is not automatically feasible. The robotics examples show how a suitable action represen-tation and predictor matter as much as the sample count. The next part changes the problem from repeatedly optimizing with a given model to learning values and policies through experience. The expectation notation and distributional reasoning introduced here will reappear in Markov decision processes, Bellman equations, and reinforcement learning. Part IV 

# From Optimal Control to Reinforcement Learning 

> 269

# 12 

# Markov Decision Processes and Bellman Equations 

Chapter map. Continuous-state and continuous-action MDPs, returns and values, Bellman equations, model-based planning, and connections to optimal control. 

# 12.1 Why Learn a Policy After Model Predictive Control? 

Chapters 10 and 11 introduced model predictive control (MPC) and its sampling-based variant, MPPI. Both use a state estimate and a model to plan over a finite horizon, execute an action, and replan. On a robot, each update must finish before its control deadline: state estimation, trajectory optimization or rollout evaluation, constraint checks, and communication all consume time. Parallel sampling can increase throughput, but it does not make the computation free. The plan can also be sensitive to errors in the estimated state or prediction model. In particular, an optimizer may favor actions that exploit a model error. Contact-rich manipulation and locomotion make online optimization harder. Sticking, slipping, impacts, and stance changes create hybrid dynamics; the unknown contact sequence introduces discrete choices, while the resulting constraints and transitions may be nonsmooth. Contact-aware trajectory optimization can address these effects, but repeatedly solving such problems within a tight control-cycle budget remains challenging Posa et al. [2014], Patel et al. [2019]. Deep reinforcement learning (RL) shifts some computation from control time to training time. It uses experience to learn a policy : a rule that maps the robot’s observations to actions. Once trained, the policy produces actions by evaluating a neural network, rather than searching through many action sequences at every control step. Training cost is therefore spread across the policy’s later use. Domain randomization can help bridge the gap between simulation and the real robot by varying properties such as mass, friction, sensor noise, and delay during training. It improves robustness to the variations represented in training, but does not guarantee robustness to every 270 Section 12.2 271 

possible condition Peng et al. [2018]. The two approaches have complementary strengths. MPC can adapt its plan to new measured states and goals, provided its model is accurate enough and its optimization finishes within the control deadline. It remains vulnerable to model and state-estimation errors, and contact-rich planning can be difficult because contact changes create hybrid, nonsmooth dynamics and discrete choices Posa et al. [2014], Patel et al. [2019]. A learned policy is fast at deployment, but its behavior depends on what it learned. Combining the approaches—for example, using MPC to guide policy training or a policy to initialize online planning—is an active direction Zhang et al. [2016]. This chapter introduces the basic language of RL and connects it to ideas from optimal control. A key limitation to keep in mind is that a learned policy may fail when deployed in out-of-distribution (OOD) conditions, where the observations, contacts, or dynamics differ substantially from those encountered during training. 

# 12.2 Markov Decision Processes 

A Markov decision process (MDP) describes how a controller chooses actions when the outcome may be uncertain. This chapter focuses on continuous robotic states and controls. Model-based planning uses a model to predict outcomes; reinforcement learning (RL) uses experience to evaluate or improve decisions and can do so without a complete model. Standard treatments include Sutton and Barto [2018], Bertsekas [2017]. 

12.2.1 States, Actions, Transitions, and Rewards 

Let the state be a vector s ∈ S ⊆ Rn and the action be a vector a ∈ A (s) ⊆ Rm. Both spaces are usually continuous for a physical robot. For example, a manipulator state can contain joint positions and velocities, while its action can be a vector of torques. The admissible action set may depend on the state through actuator, contact, or safety limits. The dynamics are described by a transition model. In a stochastic MDP this is a probability kernel P (B | s, a ): the probability that the next state falls in a region B ⊆ S , given the current state and action. If the transition has a density, we write p(s′ | s, a ), so that 

P (St+1 ∈ B | St = s, A t = a) = 

> ∫
> B

p(s′ | s, a ) ds ′. (12.1) In later equations, P (ds ′ | s, a ) means integration with respect to this conditional next-state distribution. For any suitable function f , the integral 

> ∫
> S

f (s′)P (ds ′ | s, a ) = E[f (St+1 ) | St = s, A t = a]is its conditional expected value. The notation P (ds ′ | s, a ) denotes a measure, not a density or the probability of one exact state. If a density exists, then P (ds ′ | s, a ) = p(s′ | s, a ) ds ′,272 Chapter 12. Markov Decision Processes and Bellman Equations 

yielding the usual density integral. For discrete states, the integral becomes a sum over next states, weighted by their transition probabilities. Deterministic dynamics are also included: their transition distribution is a point mass at f (s, a ). The state should retain all information from the past needed to predict future transitions and rewards. This is the Markov property: once the current state and action are given, the earlier history provides no further information about the next state and reward. For t ≥ 1, let Ht =(S0, A 0, R 1, S 1, . . . , A t−1, R t, S t) denote the history through state St, and let H0 = S0. For every measurable region B ⊆ S and reward set C ⊆ R, the property is 

P (St+1 ∈ B, R t+1 ∈ C | Ht, A t) = P (St+1 ∈ B, R t+1 ∈ C | St, A t). (12.2) A raw sensor reading need not have this property. A filtered estimate may be sufficient; otherwise, augment the state with relevant variables such as velocity, actuator temperature, or previous control. Under partial observations, a belief over underlying states can be used instead. In practice, a short history of observations and actions is often concatenated and used as an approximate state. This can supply context, such as motion inferred from successive positions, but does not guarantee a Markov state Sutton and Barto [2018]. A reward r(s, a, s ′) scores the immediate outcome of applying a at s and reaching s′. If a transition density exists, its expected immediate reward is ¯r(s, a ) = 

> ∫
> S

p(s′ | s, a )r(s, a, s ′) ds ′. (12.3) The transition model plays the role of a dynamics model, but can describe a range of possible next states rather than a single prediction. 

Example 12.1 (A continuous robot docking task) . Let pt be a mobile robot’s signed position error relative to a docking station and vt its velocity. The continuous state is st = ( pt, v t) ∈ R2,and the continuous action is an acceleration command at ∈ [−amax , a max ]. A simple noisy model is 

pt+1 = pt + hv t + ηpt , (12.4) 

vt+1 = vt + ha t + ηvt , (12.5) where h is the sample interval and the noise vectors ηt = ( ηpt , η vt ) are independent across time and Gaussian with zero mean and positive-definite covariance Q. With positive weights qp, q v, r a, one possible stage reward is 

r(st, a t, s t+1 ) = −

> (

qpp2 

> t+1

+ qvv2 

> t+1

+ raa2

> t
> )

The model has continuously many states and actions; the transition density averages over noise, and the controller selects an acceleration from an interval. This simple example will illustrate the continuous Bellman formulas below. Section 12.2 273 

12.2.2 Policies and Trajectories 

A policy specifies how actions are chosen. For continuous actions, a stochastic policy is a density 

π(a | s) satisfying 

π(a | s) ≥ 0,

> ∫
> A(s)

π(a | s) da = 1 . (12.7) The probability that the policy chooses an action in a region A is the integral of this density over 

A. A deterministic feedback policy a = μ(s) is another kind of policy; it maps each state to a single continuous action. Thus, the optimal-control feedback law and the RL policy play the same basic role, though an RL policy may deliberately randomize. Starting from an initial state, the policy and transition model generate a trajectory of states, actions, and rewards. For the docking robot this is a sequence of positions, velocities, accel-eration commands, and noisy motion. Because the dynamics are stochastic, the same policy and starting state may produce different trajectories. Write a finite state–action trajectory as 

τ = ( s0, a 0, s 1, a 1, . . . , a T −1, s T ), and let ρ0(s0) be the density of the initial state. For a stochastic policy with density π and a transition model with density p, the trajectory density is 

pπ(τ ) = ρ0(s0) 

> T−1∏
> t=0

π(at | st) p(st+1 | st, a t). (12.8) If the initial state is fixed, condition on s0 and omit the factor ρ0(s0). Each factor π(at | st)describes the policy’s action choice, and p(st+1 | st, a t) describes the resulting state transition. Their product gives the joint density by repeatedly applying the conditional probability rule and the Markov property. Because the states and actions are continuous, pπ(τ ) is a density, not the probability of that exact trajectory; probabilities are obtained by integrating over a region of trajectories. Rewards are assumed to be determined by each state–action–next-state triple. If rewards are random and included in the trajectory, augment τ with rewards and include their conditional law. 

12.2.3 Finite and Discounted Infinite Horizons 

The return is the total reward from a given time onward. For an episode ending at time T , with terminal reward g(ST ) and discount factor 0 ≤ γ ≤ 1, the discounted return is 

Gt = 

> T−t−1∑
> j=0

γj Rt+j+1 + γT −tg(ST ). (12.9) A finite task may use γ = 1; discounting is optional. Its value can depend on time because fewer decisions remain near the deadline, as in a finite-horizon optimal-control problem with a terminal cost. 274 Chapter 12. Markov Decision Processes and Bellman Equations 

For a continuing task, the discounted infinite-horizon return is 

Gt =

> ∞∑
> j=0

γj Rt+j+1 , 0 ≤ γ < 1. (12.10) When rewards are bounded, discounting makes the infinite sum converge absolutely and reduces the influence of distant outcomes. A value of γ close to one emphasizes long-term behavior; a smaller value emphasizes nearer outcomes. The discount factor is part of the objective, not the physical sample interval. RL commonly maximizes reward, whereas optimal control usually minimizes cost. Setting reward equal to negative cost makes maximizing expected return equivalent to minimizing expected accumulated cost. We use reward notation for the MDP equations and translate back to costs when making connections to optimal control. 

# 12.3 Value Functions and Bellman Equations 

A trajectory gives one possible outcome; a value function averages over the outcomes that may follow. It describes expected future performance from a state or state–action pair. When reward is negative cost, a value is the negative of the corresponding cost-to-go, whether evaluating a fixed policy or an optimal one. 

12.3.1 State and Action Values 

First consider a stationary policy in a discounted continuing task. Its state-value function is the expected return from state s:

vπ(s) = Eπ[Gt | St = s]. (12.11) Its action-value function, also called the Q-value, is the expected return when the first action is fixed to a and the policy is followed afterward: 

qπ(s, a ) = Eπ[Gt | St = s, A t = a]. (12.12) For a stochastic policy with density π, the state value averages its action values according to 

vπ(s) = 

> ∫
> A(s)

π(a | s)qπ(s, a ) da. (12.13) For a deterministic feedback policy, the corresponding action average is simply qπ(s, μ (s)). In either case, the value averages over uncertain future transitions; a stochastic policy also averages over its action choices. Section 12.3 275 

12.3.2 Bellman Expectation Equations 

Separate the return into the immediate reward and the discounted return remaining after one transition. For a stochastic policy and a transition density, conditioning on the first action and next state gives

p(s′ | s, a ) [r(s, a, s ′) + γv π(s′)] ds ′ da. (12.14) For a fixed first action, the corresponding action-value equation is

They are coupled equations over functions, because each value depends on values at a continuum of successor states. For deterministic transitions, the expectation with respect to the transition law reduces to substituting s′ = f (s, a ). The recursive structure is the same dynamic-programming idea used in Chapter 8: account for what happens now, then add the value of continuing from the next state (equation (8.10)). The important distinction is which recursion is being computed. The Bellman expectation equations above evaluate a specified policy; their optimal-control counterpart would compute the cost-to-go of a prescribed feedback law, without optimizing over controls. By contrast, Chapter 8’s Bellman recursion computes the optimal cost-to-go by minimizing over admissible controls. It therefore corresponds to the Bellman optimality equations below, after replacing reward by negative cost, rather than to the expectation equations above. In the MDP, stochastic transitions replace the deterministic next-state substitution with an expectation over possible next states, and a stochastic policy adds an expectation over its action choices. With deterministic dynamics and a fixed feedback policy, the expectation equation reduces to the fixed-policy cost-to-go recursion. For a finite horizon with γ = 1, the optimality recursion reduces to the Chapter 8 minimization after the reward-to-cost sign change. For a finite-horizon task, values also depend on time. With terminal reward given by vπT (s) = g(s), a policy is evaluated backward using 

vπt (s) = 

> ∫
> A(s)

πt(a | s)

> S

p(s′ | s, a ) [r(s, a, s ′) + γv πt+1 (s′)] ds ′ da, t = T − 1, . . . , 0. (12.16) The known terminal value supplies the boundary condition for the recursion.

The optimal state value is the greatest return achievable over all policies. At each state, the controller chooses a continuous action and then continues optimally from the resulting state. For 276 Chapter 12. Markov Decision Processes and Bellman Equations

Because the action space is continuous, finding it is itself an optimization problem; when a maximum is not attained, the supremum is used instead. The state integral averages all possible outcomes, not just the most likely next state. These equations express the discrete-time principle of optimality developed in Chapter 8. An optimal continuation from the next state must itself be optimal; otherwise, replacing it with a better continuation would improve the full plan. The continuous MDP equations are the stochastic, reward-based counterpart of dynamic programming Bertsekas [2017], Sutton and Barto [2018]. 

# 12.4 Planning and Control with a Known Model 

When the transition model and reward are known, the Bellman equations can be used to compute values and improve policies. This computation is called planning; the resulting policy is a feedback controller when applied to the robot. For continuous states and actions, exact dynamic programming is usually impractical: values are functions over continuous state spaces, and each backup may require continuous action optimization. The equations still show what value and policy approximations should compute. 

12.4.1 Policy Evaluation and Policy Improvement 

Policy evaluation computes vπ(s) for a fixed policy from its Bellman expectation equation. Policy improvement then chooses actions that are greedy with respect to that value. For a deterministic policy, one such update is 

μnew (s) ∈ arg max 

> a∈A (s)
> ∫
> S

p(s′ | s, a ) [r(s, a, s ′) + γv π(s′)] ds ′. (12.20) Alternating evaluation and improvement is called policy iteration. In continuous robot problems these steps generally require function approximation and numerical action optimization, so exact tabular guarantees do not automatically apply. Section 12.4 277 

12.4.2 Value Iteration 

Value iteration repeatedly applies the optimal Bellman backup. In a continuous MDP, this operator maps a value function to an updated value function: (T v)( s) = max 

> a∈A (s)
> ∫
> S

p(s′ | s, a ) [r(s, a, s ′) + γv (s′)] ds ′, vk+1 = T vk. (12.21) At each state, the backup averages immediate reward plus discounted successor value over possible next states for each action, then selects the best action. Applying it across states produces the next value function. For the Bellman operator in equation (12.21), assume bounded rewards, nonempty admissible action sets, and 0 ≤ γ < 1. Assume also that each backup is well-defined, using a supremum if an action maximum is not attained. Then T is a contraction on the space of bounded measurable value functions under the sup norm: 

|(T v)( s) − (T w)( s)| ≤ γ sup 

> a∈A (s)
> ∫
> S

p(s′ | s, a )|v(s′) − w(s′)| ds ′

≤ γ∥v − w∥∞,

∥T v − T w∥∞ ≤ γ∥v − w∥∞.

(12.22) The transition density integrates to one, so averaging v − w over next states cannot increase its largest absolute value; discounting scales that bound by γ. Maximizing over actions also preserves the bound. Thus, by the contraction mapping theorem, T has a unique fixed point, the optimal value v∗, and exact value iteration converges to it. This guarantee concerns the exact backup; approximating the value function or action maximization for a high-dimensional robot can change the convergence behavior. 

12.4.3 Connections to Optimal Control 

The terminology maps directly to continuous optimal control. The reward convention differs because RL maximizes while control usually minimizes. c(s, a, s ′) the transition cost. The cost-minimizing Bellman recursion in MDP notation is 

Vt(s) = min 

> a∈A (s)
> ∫
> S

p(s′ | s, a ) [c(s, a, s ′) + Vt+1 (s′)] ds ′. (12.23) For deterministic dynamics, this reduces to Vt(s) = min a{c(s, a, f (s, a )) + Vt+1 (f (s, a )) }. In optimal-control notation, the same recursion is written Vt(x) = min u{ℓ(x, u ) + Vt+1 (f (x, u )) }, as developed in Chapter 8. Shrinking the sampling interval leads, under suitable conditions, to the continuous-time HJB description from that chapter. In the standard continuous-time optimal-control formulation used in Chapter 8, one usually works directly with the value or cost-to-go V (t, x ) and does not introduce a separate action-value function analogous to the discrete-time Q-value. This is a convention of the formulation, not a mathematical restriction. To see the connection, hold a control u fixed for a short interval ∆ t, then follow the optimal continuation. Let xu(τ ) denote the trajectory starting at x at time t under the fixed control u. The cost over this interval plus optimal remaining cost is 

Q∆t(t, x, u ) =  

> ∫t+∆ tt

ℓ(τ, x u(τ ), u ) dτ + V (t + ∆ t, x u(t + ∆ t)) . (12.24) For smooth f , ℓ, and V , a first-order expansion gives 

Q∆t(t, x, u ) = V (t, x ) + ∆ t

> [

Vt(t, x ) + ℓ(t, x, u ) + ∇xV (t, x )Tf (t, x, u )

> ]

+ o(∆ t). (12.25) At fixed ( t, x ), V and Vt do not depend on the chosen control. Therefore, to first order, minimizing this short-interval action value over u is equivalent to minimizing ℓ(t, x, u ) + ∇xV (t, x )Tf (t, x, u ), the Hamiltonian’s control-dependent term ( equations (8.25) and (8.26)). This term is thus a Q-like instantaneous cost rate, not itself a full discrete-time Q-value; the HJB equation compares controls through this rate rather than through a separate action-value function. This connection also clarifies MPC. Dynamic programming seeks a value and feedback decision over the whole state domain. MPC instead solves a finite-horizon problem online from the measured state and applies its first control before replanning. That is practical for continuous states because it avoids precomputing an exact value at every point, though optimization must meet the control deadline. Chapter 9’s trajectory optimization and Chapter 11’s MPPI provide different ways of carrying out that online search. In linear–quadratic control, the value has a quadratic form and Bellman recursion reduces to the Riccati recursion of Chapter 7. The mathematical structure is shared even though the numerical methods differ. The chapters that follow emphasize continuous robot states and low-level controls. They replace exact model-based value computation with value or policy approximations learned from data, then develop actor–critic methods for directly representing and improving continuous feedback policies. The later chapter on hierarchical behavior also uses discrete skill choices as high-level abstractions over continuous robot control. 

Chapter summary. An MDP can describe continuous robot states and actions with uncertain transitions. Policies are feedback laws or action densities; values are expected returns; Bellman Section 12.4 279 

expectation equations average over policy actions and transition outcomes, while optimality equations optimize over continuous actions. These equations match the cost-to-go recursions of optimal control after changing reward maximization to cost minimization. Exact dynamic programming is difficult in high dimensions, motivating MPC, trajectory optimization, sampling, and learned function approximations. 

Exercises. Exercise 12.2. A robot controller uses a continuous stochastic policy π(a | s). What quantity gives the probability that its action falls in a region A ⊆ A (s)? Why is the density at one exact action not that probability? 

Short solution. The probability is ∫ 

> A

π(a | s) da . A single exact action has zero probability under a continuous distribution; the density is probability per unit action-space volume. 

Exercise 12.3. Write the Bellman expectation equation for a continuous stochastic policy and transition density. Which integrals are over controls and which are over next states? 

Short solution. Use equation (12.14). The outer integral averages actions under π(a | s); the inner integral averages next states under p(s′ | s, a ). 

Exercise 12.4. Show how the stochastic cost Bellman recursion in equation (12.23) simplifies when the dynamics are deterministic. 

Short solution. The transition distribution is concentrated at s′ = f (s, a ), so the integral reduces to Vt(s) = min a{c(s, a, f (s, a )) + Vt+1 (f (s, a )) }.

Exercise 12.5. Why does replacing continuous states and actions by a finite grid make planning easier, and what does the grid approximation leave out? 

Short solution. A grid permits table-based sums and finite action comparisons. It ignores states and controls between grid points and can therefore introduce approximation error or exclude useful motions. 13 

# Policy Gradients and Actor–Critic 

Chapter map. Direct optimization of stochastic continuous-control policies, likelihood-ratio gradients, critics and advantage estimates, and the clipped policy updates used by PPO. 

Chapter 12 introduced the MDP value functions vπ and qπ and their Bellman equations. One way to improve a policy is to estimate qπ(s, a ) and choose the action with greatest estimated value. For a robot, however, the action is a real-valued vector; maximizing an approximate q-function over all admissible torques or accelerations can itself be a difficult optimization problem. Policy-gradient methods instead represent the feedback controller directly by a parameterized policy and adjust its parameters to increase expected return. The action distribution can be continuous, so this approach fits the bounded controls used by physical robots without first replacing them by a finite menu of actions. The gradient is estimated from trajectories sampled by interacting with the robot or a simulator. It does not require a known dynamics model or differentiation through the simulator, but its raw Monte Carlo estimate can be noisy. A critic estimates future return to reduce that noise and to construct advantage estimates. This chapter develops the on-policy route from the likelihood-ratio estimator to actor–critic learning and proximal policy optimization (PPO). The following chapter turns from algorithmic foundations to practical design choices for deep reinforcement learning in robotics. Policy optimization can improve task performance, but neither a stochastic policy nor PPO by itself guarantees that every action or trajectory is safe. 

# 13.1 Policy Gradients 

13.1.1 Parameterized Policies for Continuous Actions 

Let s ∈ S denote the robot state and let the admissible control set be a bounded region A(s) ⊆ Rm.A stochastic policy πθ(a | s) gives a density over admissible actions; its parameters θ determine 280 Section 13.1 281 

how the action distribution varies with state. For a finite-horizon task, the policy may also depend on time-to-go t. For brevity, we write the actor input simply as s; when time dependence is needed, this denotes the augmented input ( s, t ). In a neural policy, a network commonly maps the state to the mean and spread of a distribution. Sampling from that distribution provides exploration during training, while the same network can be evaluated quickly at deployment. For box-bounded controls, a convenient construction starts with a Gaussian latent action and maps it smoothly to the actuator limits. The policy is stochastic during training even though a robot may later use its mean action for deterministic execution. A stochastic policy supplies exploration, not a safety certificate; actuator limits and any independent safety monitor remain necessary. 282 Chapter 13. Policy Gradients and Actor–Critic

This means E[̂ g] = ∇θJ(θ), with the expectation taken over batches collected by the current policy; any one finite batch can still differ from the true gradient. Despite being unbiased, the estimate can have high variance: similar initial states may lead to very different returns because of contact events, sensor noise, or exploratory actions. 

REINFORCE: episodic policy-gradient update 

1. Initialize the policy parameters θ and step size α.2. Collect M complete episodes with the current policy, storing each state, bounded action, reward, and terminal state. 3. For each episode, set GTi = g(STi ) and compute backward Gt = Rt+1 + γG t+1 for 

t = Ti − 1, . . . , 0. 4. Form ̂ g using equation (13.11) and update θ ← θ + α̂ g.5. Discard the batch and repeat with newly sampled episodes. 

13.1.3 The Policy-Gradient Theorem 

For a finite-horizon task, define qπt (s, a ) = Eπ[Gt | St = s, A t = a] and vπt (s) = Eπ[Gt | St = s]. These extend Chapter 12’s value definitions to values that depend on the remaining time. To see how the action value enters, let ξt = ∇θ log πθ(At | St), the score at time t. Because ξt is determined by ( St, A t), conditioning on that pair leaves it fixed. In a finite-horizon task, time-to-go may be included in the critic input, as in the actor input convention of section 13.1.1. The critic vw is typically fit by squared-error regression, matching its predictions at sampled states to targets computed either from sampled returns or by bootstrapping from value estimates. We call these critic targets. The on-policy actor–critic batch update below summarizes the training loop. 

On-policy actor–critic (batch update)

With Monte Carlo, the sampled return-to-go is the critic target; subtracting the value baseline from it gives the actor’s advantage estimate. One-step temporal-difference (TD) learning bootstraps the critic target from the next-state value prediction and uses the TD residual as the actor’s advantage estimate. Generalized advantage estimation (GAE) accumulates TD residuals across a rollout segment to form the actor advantage, then adds that advantage to the frozen critic prediction at the current state to form the critic target. The following subsections derive these constructions.

13.2.3 One-Step Temporal-Difference Targets and Advantages 

One-step temporal-difference (TD) learning reduces Monte Carlo noise by using a frozen next-state value prediction in place of the unsampled return. For simplicity, include any terminal payoff in the reward on the transition that ends the episode. Under the return convention in equation (13.3), add γg (ST ) to the final transition reward, since that reward is weighted by γT −1. Thus no separate terminal payoff term is needed below. The task specification defines which events end an episode; examples include task success and a safety violation. Let Mt be the bootstrap mask: set Mt = 0 on a transition that reaches one of these task-defined terminal conditions, and set Mt = 1 when the task continues. In a continuing Section 13.2 289 

infinite-horizon task with no terminal conditions, Mt = 1 at every step. A cutoff used only to limit data collection has Mt = 1; a time limit that the task specification defines as episode completion has Mt = 0. With frozen rollout critic v ¯w, define the one-step TD residual: 

δt = Rt+1 + γM tv ¯w(St+1 ) − v ¯w(St). (13.22) Use the following one-step target for critic regression: ̂

During the actor update, hold the computed advantages fixed and do not backpropagate through the critic values used to form them.

# 13.3 Stable Policy Updates 

Policy-gradient estimates are local to states visited by the current policy. If one update changes the policy too much, the new policy may visit states that were rare in the rollout, where the gradient estimate is unreliable. Trust-region methods address this by limiting how far the updated action distribution can move from the behavior policy that generated the data. 

13.3.1 Trust Region Policy Optimization 

An ordinary policy-gradient update estimates g ≈ ∇ θJ(θold ) from an on-policy rollout and takes a step such as θ ← θold +αg . This gives a local ascent direction, but does not by itself score the return change from a finite step. Evaluating each candidate with fresh rollouts is costly, while reusing the old rollout requires accounting for the fact that it was generated by πθold . TRPO estimates candidate improvement from that batch and limits the policy change with a KL constraint. For each stored state–action pair, define the candidate-to-old probability ratio

It is exact at θold ; away from that policy, the approximation error comes from the state-visitation shift. The theoretical TRPO bound controls this shift using exact advantages and a maximum-over-states policy-divergence constraint. In practice, equation (13.30) uses estimated advantages and an empirical average KL, so surrogate improvement does not guarantee that the true return increased Schulman et al. [2015]. The surrogate also has the correct gradient at the reference policy. Because ∇θϱt(θ) = ϱt(θ)∇θ log πθ(At |

model around θold rather than treating the surrogate as the true return at distant parameters. Let ∆θ = θ − θold and g = ∇θLθold (θ)|θ=θold . For a small step, 

Lθold (θold + ∆ θ) ≈ L θold (θold ) + g⊤∆θ, DKL (θold , θ old + ∆ θ) ≈ 12 ∆θ⊤F ∆θ, 

where DKL is the rollout-state average in equation (13.30), and F is its KL Hessian at the old policy, equal to the empirical Fisher information matrix (the average over rollout states and old-policy actions of policy-score outer products). Dropping the constant surrogate value leaves the local constrained problem max  

> ∆θ

g⊤∆θ subject to 12 ∆θ⊤F ∆θ ≤ δ. 

Its solution points along the scaled natural-gradient direction F −1g. TRPO uses conjugate gradients to approximate this direction without forming or inverting the full matrix. Because both Taylor models are local, a backtracking line search reevaluates the empirical surrogate and KL on the rollout data, without the Taylor approximations. It accepts a step only if the surrogate exceeds its value at θold and the measured KL stays within the limit Schulman et al. [2015]. Conjugate gradients avoid the dense solve, but do not make TRPO cheap: each iteration requires a Fisher-vector product, and line search adds policy evaluations. The cost grows with policy size, iteration count, and batch size. 

TRPO update 

1. Collect an on-policy rollout; compute and fix its advantages and critic targets. 2. Propose a step using the linearized surrogate and quadratic KL model, then backtrack until the surrogate exceeds its old-policy value and the measured KL is within the limit; otherwise keep the old policy. 3. Fit the critic, discard the batch, and collect fresh data. 

13.3.2 Proximal Policy Optimization 

TRPO’s constrained solve is more involved than first-order optimization. PPO keeps the old-policy rollout and probability ratio but replaces the constrained solve with a clipped surrogate optimized by minibatch methods. Clipping discourages large updates; it is not a hard trust region or a safety guarantee Schulman et al. [2017]. At a fixed state, the ratio reweights old-policy samples to estimate expectations under the new policy. PPO uses the following clipped surrogate: 

LCLIP (θ) = 1

range; for negative advantages, it stops rewarding further decreases. This limits the surrogate’s incentive, not the probability ratio or KL divergence itself. An entropy bonus can encourage exploration. Maximize the actor objective and minimize the critic loss:

PPO: clipped on-policy actor–critic 

1. Set θold ← θ, collect a fresh rollout, and store transitions and old action log probabilities. 2. With the critic frozen, compute bootstrap masks, TD residuals, GAE advantages, and fixed value targets. 3. Optionally normalize advantages, then optimize actor and critic on minibatches for several epochs. Stop early if the KL grows too large. 4. Discard the batch and use the updated actor for the next rollout. PPO is on-policy: it reuses each fresh batch for only a few epochs, then collects new data from the updated policy. 

13.3.3 Implementation Diagnostics 

Policy-gradient failures can look like ordinary training instability, so inspect the controller’s behavior as well as its loss curves: • Track return, success, violations, and termination causes on both training and held-out condi-tions; rising reward can hide loopholes. • Inspect action statistics, critic fit, policy KL, and clip fraction. • Verify bootstrap masks and confirm stored actions match their log probabilities. Normalize inputs, use several seeds, and test held-out conditions. Domain randomization covers only variations included in training. PPO clipping is not a safety filter; validate policies in stages before hardware use. 

13.3.4 Off-Policy Learning and Experience Reuse 

PPO is a good fit when fresh rollouts are cheap, as in parallel simulation Makoviychuk et al. [2021]. When robot interaction is costly, off-policy methods such as TD3 and SAC reuse transitions from replay buffers Fujimoto et al. [2018], Haarnoja et al. [2018]. Replay can improve sample efficiency, 294 Chapter 13. Policy Gradients and Actor–Critic 

but stale data and repeated critic updates can introduce error. Choose based on whether fresh interaction or wall-clock throughput is the main constraint; neither approach is always better. 

Chapter summary. Policy gradients optimize a stochastic policy using sampled rewards and log-probability gradients. Critics and GAE reduce variance but depend on value estimates. TRPO constrains average policy change; PPO offers a simpler clipped surrogate, not a hard trust region or safety guarantee. Correct action densities, terminal masks, and evaluation remain essential. Replay can reuse costly experience, but stale data and repeated critic updates can introduce error. 

Exercises. Exercise 13.1. Show that subtracting a baseline b(s) independent of the sampled action does not change the conditional expected policy-gradient contribution. Why can it nevertheless change the variance of the estimator? 

Short solution. The added term has expectation b(s) ∫ πθ(a | s)∇θ log πθ(a | s) da = b(s)∇θ 

> ∫

πθ(a |

s) da = 0. Its sample values can still be more or less variable than the uncentered return, so the baseline changes variance without changing the mean gradient. 

Exercise 13.2. Why does the likelihood-ratio policy gradient not require differentiating the robot dynamics? State one condition it still places on the policy. 

Short solution. The derivative of the trajectory log density contains the policy log probabilities; transition probabilities are independent of the policy parameters. The policy density must be differentiable with respect to its parameters, and its log probability must be evaluable for sampled actions. 

Exercise 13.3. For GAE, explain the roles of λ = 0 and λ = 1. Why can the latter be noisier? 

Short solution. At λ = 0, only the one-step TD residual is used. At λ = 1, the residuals telescope to the Monte Carlo return minus the value baseline. The latter uses more sampled future rewards and so can carry more trajectory noise, though it relies less on critic bootstrapping. 

Exercise 13.4. What does the PPO probability ratio compare? Explain why clipping it does not guarantee bounded actions or a hard bound on policy change. 

Short solution. It compares the new policy’s density for a stored action with the old policy’s density that generated it. Clipping limits incentives in this surrogate for sampled actions, not the policy’s support at all states or actions and not the KL divergence itself. Action bounds must be enforced by the policy parameterization or controller. 

Exercise 13.5. For ai = mi + di tanh zi, identify the log-Jacobian correction needed when evaluating the action density from the latent Gaussian. Section 13.3 295 

Short solution. The derivative is da i/dz i = di(1 − tanh 2 zi). The action log density equals the latent Gaussian log density minus ∑ 

> i

log[ di(1 − tanh 2 zi)]. 14 

# Practical Tips for Deep RL for Robotics 

Chapter map. Practical choices in policy inputs, training information, curricula, rewards, simulator fidelity, goal-conditioned control, and sim-to-real robustness. 

Reinforcement learning (RL) is a general framework for learning behavior through interaction. Robotics tasks commonly involve continuous state and action spaces, and modern simulators can generate fresh rollouts in large, GPU-parallel batches. Under these conditions, an on-policy method such as PPO is often a practical default. Yet the generality of the RL formulation does not make training plug-and-play: arbitrary choices of observations, actions, and rewards do not generally produce a useful policy. Successful robot learning depends on choices tailored to the task and its deployment constraints. This chapter surveys policy inputs and privileged information, system identification and actuator modeling, curricula and reward design, domain randomization, latency and filtering, hybrid model-based control, and goal-conditioned policies. These choices are often crucial to obtaining useful, deployable behavior. The guidance is a set of design checks, not a substitute for evaluating the complete controller on held-out tasks and, ultimately, through staged hardware validation. 

# 14.1 Policy Inputs and Architectures 

A state-based policy takes as input a state estimate, such as joint positions and velocities, base pose and velocity, and task-relevant object information. For manipulation, this may include an estimated six-degree-of-freedom (6-DoF) object pose, along with geometric information from a CAD model. This representation is often compact and aligned with robot kinematics and dynamics; it can simplify learning and debugging and support established controllers or safety checks. Its usefulness depends on reliable estimation. Errors can mislead the policy, and obtaining accurate estimates from partial observations, such as images with occlusions, can be notoriously difficult. An end-to-end observation-to-action policy maps sensor observations directly to controls, for 296 Section 14.2 297 

example from images and proprioceptive readings to joint commands. It bypasses a separately specified state-estimation module by learning perception and control together from data. This can be useful when a hand-designed estimator is difficult to build or discards important task information, but it raises the data and validation burden: the policy must learn both which features matter and how to act on them. End-to-end does not mean that hidden physical variables cease to matter; they must be inferable from the observations, supplied through memory, or handled robustly despite being unobserved. When a single observation is insufficient, stack a short observation–action history or use a recurrent policy with internal memory. Such a policy can infer quantities such as velocity, contact mode, or a slowly changing payload from observation sequences, performing some state estimation implicitly alongside control. Memory alone does not guarantee this: the history must contain informative measurements, and training must teach the policy to use them. Learned memory is useful when it solves a real observability problem, not as a default replacement for a good estimator. 

# 14.2 Privileged Information and Teacher–Student Frameworks 

A simulator often exposes variables that a physical robot cannot measure directly, such as exact link velocities, contact forces, object pose, terrain geometry, or the parameters used to generate the scene. These are privileged information . During training, a critic may use the full simulator state to estimate returns even when the actor receives only the observations that will be available at deployment. This asymmetric actor–critic design can make value estimation easier Pinto et al. [2017]. Additionally, another useful stretagy is to train a teacher policy with privileged inputs and then train a student policy to reproduce its useful behavior from deployable observations. The student can be supervised on teacher actions or action distributions, and can also be fine-tuned using the task reward. In either case, the student—not the teacher—is the policy that must pass the final evaluation. Teacher–student transfer has been used to turn privileged simulation training into policies that operate from robot observations Kumar et al. [2021]. The information boundary must be explicit. Do not accidentally feed a privileged contact label, exact velocity, or simulator terrain map to the student if it will be absent on the robot. If the teacher relies on a fact that cannot be inferred from observation history, exact imitation may be impossible; the student must instead learn a robust response or receive additional sensors. 

# 14.3 System Identification and Actuator Modeling 

Model task-relevant dynamics rather than attempting to reproduce the entire real world. Identify parameters that materially affect performance, such as link masses and inertias, center of mass, friction, damping, compliance, contact behavior, sensor calibration, and command delay. Use measurements and safe excitation where possible, then compare simulated and measured responses over task-relevant motions and speeds. 298 Chapter 14. Practical Tips for Deep RL for Robotics 

Model actuators at the same command interface used by the policy. Delivered torque may differ from the command because of motor and drive bandwidth, low-level position or torque control loops, current and voltage limits, saturation, rate limits, backlash, and communication delay. Available electric-motor torque typically decreases with rotational speed; represent the task-relevant torque–speed envelope, for example: 

|τi| ≤ τi, max (| ˙qi|), (14.1) Include thermal or voltage constraints when relevant. Assuming peak stall torque at every speed can teach the policy to request infeasible motions. System identification and actuator modeling guide learning; they need not yield a perfect digital twin. Model task-critical dynamics accurately, then represent residual uncertainty deliberately. Accurate actuator limits and delay are especially important for precise tracking and high-speed maneuvers, where small model errors can substantially change achievable trajectories. 

# 14.4 Curriculum Learning 

Some tasks are too difficult to learn from a broad, uniformly sampled set of episodes at the outset. A curriculum gradually expands the training distribution as the policy becomes competent. For a legged robot, one might begin on level ground with modest commanded speeds, then add uneven terrain, larger speed changes, pushes, and wider variation in starting pose. For manipulation, one might first use reachable, well-oriented objects, then increase pose variation, clutter, object diversity, and contact difficulty. Curriculum dimensions include task difficulty, initial-state and goal diversity, and environmental complexity. A schedule can be hand designed or adjusted using success rates, but it should not advance so quickly that the policy stops receiving useful learning signals. Keep some coverage of earlier cases as harder cases are introduced, and verify that the final training distribution still covers the intended deployment conditions. Otherwise, a policy can become good at the curriculum schedule while forgetting simpler behaviors or failing at the actual target distribution. 

# 14.5 Reward Engineering and Reward Curricula 

Start from the task objective and express it in measurable terms. For reaching, reward reduced goal error and low terminal speed; for locomotion, balance commanded-velocity tracking against energy use, slippage, and unsafe body motion. When successful rollouts are rare, dense progress rewards can provide useful feedback during early exploration and ease credit assignment. Keep shaping aligned with task completion: every reward term creates an incentive, and incorrect signs, poor scaling, or omitted failure conditions can yield high returns without task success. Track success, constraint violations, and individual reward components, not just total return. A reward curriculum changes the learning signal as training progresses. For example, dense distance-to-goal shaping can guide early exploration and then be tapered as the policy learns the Section 14.6 299 

final objective. Stable contact can likewise be emphasized early, with tracking accuracy weighted more heavily later. Keep the final objective visible during training and evaluate against the final task metric throughout. Abrupt or unmonitored changes can make critic targets nonstationary, encourage reward loopholes, or leave the policy optimized for an intermediate objective. Check shaping terms against the desired behavior, not just for smoother learning curves. 

# 14.6 Domain Randomization and the Robustness–Precision Trade-off 

Domain randomization varies simulated conditions during training so the policy encounters more than one nominal model. Common choices include inertial properties (mass, inertia, and center of mass), friction and contact parameters, actuator strength and delay, sensor bias and noise, observation latency, and external disturbances. Randomization can improve robustness to model uncertainty when the real system falls within, or near, the variations represented in training; it does not guarantee robustness to arbitrary conditions Peng et al. [2018]. Broader is not always better. A policy optimized across widely different dynamics may favor actions that are safe across the whole range, behaving conservatively even on the nominal robot. If a task depends on fast, accurate motion, excessive variation can reduce precision or make the training problem unnecessarily difficult. Randomizing implausible or strongly inconsistent parameter combinations can also teach the wrong invariances. For precision or high-performance tasks, first identify the important dynamics and actuator behavior as accurately as practical. Then randomize targetedly around the remaining uncertainty, using plausible ranges and dependencies between parameters. Evaluate both nominal performance and held-out parameter settings, sensor conditions, and delays. This balances robustness with precision: system identification narrows uncertainty, while targeted randomization prepares the policy for the uncertainty that remains. 

# 14.7 Latency, Delays, and Low-Pass Filtering 

Latency is the time from capturing a sensor measurement to the corresponding control input taking effect. In the loop, this appears as a delay: by the time the command is applied, the robot may already have moved. End-to-end latency includes sensing, state estimation and preprocessing, policy inference, communication, and actuator response; variation in latency is timing jitter. Many simulators are step-driven: physics advances only after the policy returns an action. Unless wall-clock computation is modeled, simulated time does not advance during inference, so the action appears to take effect immediately. On a physical robot, motion continues during sensing, computation, and communication, making the arriving command correspond to a stale measurement. Measure sensor-to-actuator latency and update timing on the robot, then reproduce them in simulation. Measure timing variation as well and model it as jitter when it occurs. For example, a policy running at 10–50 Hz has a nominal update interval of 20–100 ms. Missed 300 Chapter 14. Practical Tips for Deep RL for Robotics 

deadlines or irregular updates change the effective delay and can make control erratic. Low-pass filters can smooth high-frequency sensor noise or rapid command changes, but they do not correct timing jitter and add phase lag. When filtering is used on hardware, use an equivalent discrete-time filter in simulation, matching its cutoff, order, sample rate, and location in the signal path. This aligns effective control bandwidth and reduces one source of sim-to-real mismatch; validate the full closed-loop response, since filter matching alone does not reproduce all sensing and actuator dynamics. 

# 14.8 Hybrid Model-Based Control and Reinforcement Learning 

Use model-predictive control (MPC) for as much of the task as practical: when the dynamics and state are sufficiently well known and the optimization can meet the control deadline, MPC provides a structured way to plan actions while respecting constraints. Use learning for components that remain difficult for model-based methods, such as acting from observations when reliable state estimation is difficult or producing actions when online optimization is too slow. Design the learned action space to keep this learning problem as small as possible. For example, a policy can select among motion primitives, adjust a few MPC parameters, or provide bounded residual corrections to an MPC command instead of choosing unrestricted low-level actions. Restricting the scope of learning can improve sample efficiency and generalization, while preserving enough action authority to accomplish the task. 

# 14.9 Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning 

Robotic policies are often more useful when they can be steered toward a requested outcome instead of solving only one fixed task. A goal-conditioned policy receives both the current observation and a goal, for example πθ(at | ot, g ). A mobile robot might be given a waypoint; a manipulator might receive a desired object pose. At deployment, an operator or a higher-level planner can change g

without retraining the low-level policy. This is the practical sense in which goal conditioning makes a policy steerable. Goal-conditioned value functions similarly represent returns as a function of state and goal Schaul et al. [2015]. Training should expose the policy to a deliberate distribution of goals: 

g ∼ ptrain (g), max  

> θ

Eg∼ptrain [J(πθ; g)] . (14.2) Here J(πθ; g) is the expected return when pursuing goal g. Choose this distribution to cover the reachable workspace, task-relevant orientations, and important boundary cases, rather than sampling goals only near an easy nominal target. Performance is generally most dependable in well-covered regions of the training distribution. A function approximator may interpolate to nearby unseen goals, but seeing some examples from a distribution does not guarantee success at every goal in its support, and goals outside the trained region can fail abruptly. Evaluate on held-out goals across the intended operating range, including difficult but feasible ones. Section 14.9 301 

Direct goal conditioning asks the policy to learn how actions should change with g. Complementary reuse methods include successor features and forward–backward (FB) representations. Successor features predict future feature occupancies that can be rescored with goal-dependent reward weights, provided the features capture the relevant reward variation Barreto et al. [2017]. FB representations factor discounted state–action occupancy and support policy selection across many rewards and goals through a learned latent space Touati and Ollivier [2021]. 

Chapter summary. Successful robotic RL depends on deployable policy inputs, explicit bound-aries around privileged training information, and task-aligned curricula and rewards. For sim-to-real transfer, identify task-relevant dynamics and actuator limits, including torque–speed behavior, and match latency and filtering between simulation and hardware. Use MPC where practical; reserve learning for components that are difficult to estimate or optimize in real time, and keep the learned action space focused to support sample efficiency and generalization. Randomize plausible residual uncertainty: excessive variation can improve robustness at the cost of nominal precision. Goal-conditioned policies make behavior steerable at runtime, but only when training and evaluation cover the goals the robot is expected to reach. 

Exercises. Exercise 14.1. Give one reason to choose a state-based policy and one reason to choose an end-to-end observation policy. When might a history or recurrent policy help? 

Short solution. A state-based policy uses compact, structured state estimates and is easier to debug; end-to-end control can avoid a brittle hand-designed estimator and learn task-relevant features directly. Memory can help infer hidden quantities such as velocity or contact from changing observations. 

Exercise 14.2. How can a critic use privileged simulation information without making the deployed actor depend on it? What is the role of a student policy? 

Short solution. Train the critic with the richer simulator state while keeping the actor’s inputs restricted to deployable observations. A student imitates or distills a privileged teacher using only those deployable observations and is evaluated as the final controller. 

Exercise 14.3. Why might broad domain randomization improve robustness but reduce precision? What would you do when accurate tracking is essential? 

Short solution. Training over a wide range can favor actions that work conservatively across many models, sacrificing performance on any one model. Identify the important dynamics accurately and randomize only plausible residual uncertainty, then test nominal and held-out conditions. Bibliography
